===== 2022 Rate Case Rebuttal Testimony - Coyne FINAL.docx =====
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OFJAMES M. COYNEON BEHALF OFGEORGIA POWER COMPANYDOCKET NO. 44280
INTRODUCTION
PLEASE STATE YOUR NAME, BUSINESS ADDRESS, AND OCCUPATION.
My name is James M. Coyne, and I am employed by Concentric Energy Advisors, Inc. (“Concentric”) as a Senior Vice President. My business address is 293 Boston Post Road West, Suite 500, Marlborough, Massachusetts 01752.
dID YOU PREVIOUSLY FILE TESTIMONY IN THIS PROCEEDING?
Yes. I submitted direct testimony to the Georgia Public Service Commission (the “Commission”) on behalf of Georgia Power Company (“Georgia Power” or the “Company”) on June 24, 2022.
What is the purpose of your REBUTTAL testimony?
The purpose of my rebuttal testimony is to respond to the direct testimonies of the following witnesses as they relate to the appropriate return on equity (“ROE”) and capital structure for Georgia Power: Mr. Michael P. Gorman on behalf of the Commission’s Public Interest Advocacy Staff (“Staff”), Ms. Billie S. LaConte on behalf of Georgia Association of Manufacturers (“GAM”), Mr. Mark E. Ellis on behalf of Georgia Interfaith Power & Light (“GIPL”), Ms. Maureen L. Reno on behalf of the U.S. Department of Defense and Federal Executive Agencies (“DOD”), and Mr. Steve W. Chriss on behalf of the Commercial Group. I collectively refer to these individual witnesses as “Intervenor Witnesses.”
are you sponsoring any exhibits AS PART OF YOUR REBUTTAL TESTIMONY?
No.
HOW IS THE REMAINDER OF YOUR REBUTTAL TESTIMONY ORGANIZED? 
My rebuttal testimony is organized to respond to the Intervenor Witnesses by issue, starting in Section II with an overview and summary of the results and recommendations presented by the various ROE witnesses in this proceeding. Section III discusses economic and capital market conditions and how those conditions are affecting the various models used to estimate the cost of equity for Georgia Power. In Section IV, I address the proper application of the cost of equity analytical models employed by me and the Intervenor Witnesses, including the Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) model, the Capital Asset Pricing Model (“CAPM”), the Bond Yield Plus Risk Premium (“Risk Premium”) model and the Expected Earnings model. In Section V, I respond to comments raised by certain witnesses with respect to Georgia Power’s proposed capital structure and cost of debt, and I explain why that capital structure is reasonable by comparison to the proxy group. In Section VI, I respond to comments regarding Georgia Power’s company-specific risk factors raised by the Intervenor Witnesses. Lastly, in Section VII, I summarize my key conclusions and continue to recommend an 11.00 percent ROE (including flotation costs) and Georgia Power’s actual capital structure of 56.0 percent common equity and 44.0 percent long-term debt.
SUMMARY OF TESTIMONY
What are your conclusions regarding the analysis and recommendations provided by the Intervenor Witnesses regarding the appropriate ROE and capital structure for GEORGIA POWER?
My key conclusions are as follows:
The Intervenor Witnesses’ analyses contain flaws and inconsistencies that produce some results well below recent authorized returns for electric utilities, particularly those comparable to Georgia Power. Some recommendations are even 50 to 100 basis points below prevailing costs of debt for utilities. Such recommendations should be given no weight as no investor would take on the perpetual risks of equity ownership when they could earn a higher return for safer debt investments that have a senior claim on cash flows.
The cost of equity for regulated utility companies is being affected by several key factors in the current and prospective capital markets, including the interest rate environment and central bank monetary policy, as well as current inflationary pressure. Inflation has escalated to levels not seen since the early 1980s; interest rates across the yield spectrum have increased; and capital market volatility remains elevated. These circumstances reinforce the importance of considering the results of multiple models, as I have with the DCF, CAPM, Risk Premium, and Expected Earnings approaches.
All models are subject to certain limiting assumptions. However, in market conditions where ROE estimation models are producing return estimates below the current cost of debt for utilities (e.g., Mr. Ellis’ DCF and CAPM estimates), utility regulators recognize that such low returns are not compensatory for investors. Rather than endorsing the results of a specific methodology, the Commission should consider how current market conditions affect the risks for equity investors as well as take into account the results of a broader range of ROE estimation methodologies.
Please summarize the cost of capital recommendations presented by the Intervenor Witnesses in this proceeding?
The Intervenor Witnesses who performed an independent ROE analysis (Mr. Gorman, Ms. Reno, and Mr. Ellis) recommend an authorized ROE for Georgia Power between 5.54 percent and 9.47 percent. Other Witnesses did not perform their own ROE analysis, and instead adjusted certain inputs in my analyses (Ms. LaConte) or referenced authorized returns for electric utilities in other jurisdictions (Mr. Chriss). Based on adjustments to my analyses using her preferred inputs, Ms. LaConte asserts that Georgia Power’s cost of equity does not exceed 9.83 percent. 
As it relates to capital structure, Ms. Reno accepts the Company’s capital structure recommendation. Mr. Gorman accepts the Company’s proposed 56 percent equity ratio for 2023, but recommends the Commission impute a hypothetical capital structure of 51 percent equity for 2024 and 2025. Ms. LaConte recommends a capital structure that contains no more than 53 percent common equity. Lastly Mr. Ellis recommends a capital structure that contains 61.70 percent equity to offset his 5.54 percent ROE recommendation. 
As is evident, there is a broad array of recommendations from multiple witnesses. Notably, recommendations as low as 5.54 percent are below any authorized return for any electric utility in the last 40 years and are lower than current market indicators of utility and corporate debt costs. Some of the recommendations are supported by analytical approaches while others are more judgmental or based on decisions from other jurisdictions. I submit that the appropriate method for determining the cost of capital is through the application of rigorous analysis using financial models and current market data from reliable sources.

There are a number of flaws and inconsistencies with the analyses conducted by the Intervenor Witnesses. I address each analytical approach and explain why their assumptions are unreasonable. At the outset, one must question analyses producing results that are more than 300 basis points below any return authorized for any electric utility in at least 40 years. 
Please describe the legal standards that must be met to establish the authorized ROE for a regulated public utility such as Georgia Power.
As discussed in my direct testimony, the standards for a just and reasonable return established by the United States Supreme Court in the Hope and Bluefield cases are:
(1) Financial integrity: the return must be adequate to ensure the company’s financial soundness and support credit quality;
(2) Capital attraction: the return must be sufficient to enable the company to attract capital on reasonable terms and conditions; and
(3) Comparable return: the return must be comparable to those available to investors in firms with commensurate risk. 
Importantly, in Bluefield, the U.S. Supreme Court recognized that a return may be reasonable at one time, but may become too high or too low at another time due to changes affecting investment opportunities, and market or business conditions. This principle is relevant because, as discussed below, certain of the Intervenor Witnesses refer to recent authorized ROEs to support their recommendations, but do not fully consider the magnitude of the change in market conditions since those ROEs were determined. 
Several of the Intervenor Witnesses (GORMAN, LACONTE, RENO, CHRISS) reference authorized ROEs for electric utilities in other jurisdictions. Do you agree that these returns are relevant in establishing the ROE for GEORGIA POWER in this proceeding?
National average returns must be placed in the proper context in order to be useful. While I agree that investors consider authorized returns in other states in assessing the reasonableness of the authorized ROE for Georgia Power, I have several concerns with the nationwide average ROE information presented by the Intervenor Witnesses. First, the averages referenced by Mr. Gorman and Mr. Chriss include ROE determinations for transmission and distribution-only (“T&D”) electric utilities, as well as determinations in formula rate proceedings in which the ROE is determined by a formula solely tied to changes in Treasury bond yields. These ROE determinations are not comparable to authorized ROEs for vertically integrated electric utilities, nor are they comparable to determinations that are based on the use of various financial models as is the process used by the Commission. Vertically integrated electric utilities have a higher level of business risk than T&D utility companies that do not own regulated generation. This higher risk profile differentiates integrated electric utilities from T&D utilities and supports a higher authorized ROE and equity ratio in the capital structure. Lastly, their references to average authorized ROEs do not include approvals earlier this year by the Florida Public Service Commission to increase the authorized ROEs of Florida Power & Light, Tampa Electric Company, and Duke Energy Florida due to increases in the 30-year Treasury yield outlined as part of those utilities’ most recent rate case settlements.
Second, market conditions at the time the authorized returns were established are very different than conditions today and going forward. For example, equity returns set when interest rates were very low in 2020 are not a reasonable basis of comparison for an appropriate ROE in 2023 when bond yields have increased and are projected to continue increasing as the economy recovers and the Federal Reserve moves to a more neutral monetary policy. After the decline in interest rates in 2020-2021 driven by the Federal Reserve’s unprecedented actions to respond to the COVID-19 pandemic, interest rates have increased by approximately 160 to 230 basis points since the settlement adopting an ROE of 10.50 percent for Georgia Power was authorized in December 2019. Since my direct testimony was prepared in April 2022, the 30-day average 30-year Treasury yield has increased by more than 100 basis points and currently exceeds even the projections of the 30-year Treasury yield at the time I prepared my direct testimony. Although state utility commissions mitigated ROE reductions in 2020-2021 due to the knowledge that interest rates were being artificially suppressed due to the Federal Reserve’s actions, the use of prior decisions during that time period which set ROEs under previously lower interest rates understates the forward-looking cost of equity and the very different rising interest rate environment that we are now experiencing. In other words, authorized ROEs that may have been considered reasonable and appropriate in 2020 and 2021, and even into the first half of 2022, are not reasonable in the current market environment, consistent with Bluefield. 
does the annual average authorized roe appropriately reflect georgia’s more constructive regulatory environment?
No. Figure 2 below presents the annual average authorized ROEs for vertically integrated electric utilities by Regulatory Research Associates’ (“RRA”) ranking of the regulatory environment (Above Average, Average, and Below Average). As Figure 2 demonstrates, authorized ROEs by regulatory commissions ranked as “Above Average” are approximately 40 to 60 basis points above the average authorized ROE in “Average” jurisdictions.

ROE recommendations that are more consistent with those authorized by “Below Average” or “Average” jurisdictions would undermine investors’ perceptions of Georgia’s supportive and constructive regulatory climate, ultimately increasing the Company’s regulatory risk.  
does current MARKET DATA ILLUSTRATE THE UNREASONABLENESS OF MR. ELLIS’S ROE RECOMMENDATION?
Yes. Current utility bond yields measured by Moody’s utility bond indices are approximately 50 to 80 basis points above Mr. Ellis’s ROE recommendation. It is simply illogical to expect that a rational investor would take on the risk of a utility equity investment when they could earn a higher return from a safer bond that has a senior claim on cash flow. Mr. Ellis attempts to adjust current utility bond yields below his cost of equity estimates to account for certain risk premia (e.g., liquidity risk) and concludes without any evidence that an equity premium between 67 and 124 basis points would be a sufficient premium for equity investors. There is no evidence to demonstrate that equity investors would be willing to accept such a small premium for taking on the risk of utility equity investment. 
Mr. Ellis’s 5.54 percent ROE recommendation is substantially below national average authorized ROEs for vertically integrated electric utilities. In fact, it is nearly 400 basis points below the 9.53 percent average authorized ROE for vertically integrated electric utilities in 2021 cited by Ms. LaConte and Ms. Reno. Mr. Ellis’ higher equity ratio recommendation of 61.70 percent does not offset the unreasonableness of his ROE recommendation. The weighted cost of equity produced by Mr. Ellis’s ROE and capital structure recommendation is 3.42 percent (i.e., 5.54 percent multiplied by 61.70 percent). If adopted, Mr. Ellis’s weighted cost of equity would be well below that recently authorized for vertically integrated electric utilities. The average weighted cost of equity for vertically integrated electric utilities in 2022 is 5.06 percent, 163 basis points above Mr. Ellis’s recommended weighted cost of equity. In fact, the 2022 average authorized weighted cost of equity is only 39 basis points below his ROE recommendation, further demonstrating the unreasonableness of his recommendation. Mr. Ellis’s recommendation is far too low to be reasonable and would not satisfy the Hope and Bluefield standards for a risk comparable return that would allow the Company to attract capital and maintain its financial integrity. Moreover, it would represent a substantial departure from Georgia’s supportive regulatory environment, introducing significant regulatory risk and higher costs of both debt and equity to the detriment of customers.
HOW DOES THE CURRENT INTEREST RATE ENVIRONMENT COMPARE TO THE ENVIRONMENT DURING THE COMPANY’S PRIOR RATE CASES?
As shown in Figure 3 below, the interest rate environment is currently above the levels experienced during the Company’s 2019 rate case and 2013 rate case. Specifically, the spot yield on the 30-year Treasury bond as of October 31, 2022 was 4.22 percent, 191 basis points above the spot yield at the time of the Commission’s decision in the Company’s 2019 rate case and 34 basis points above the yield at the time of the Commission’s decision in the 2013 rate case when the Commission approved a 10.95 percent ROE. The last time the 30-year Treasury yield was 4.22 percent was in July 2011. Even if we looked to the 90-day moving average, the current 90-day average 30-year Treasury yield of 3.43 percent is at its highest level since August 2014. 
Figure 3: 30-Year Treasury Yield (2010-2022)

The Intervenor Witnesses’ recommendations to reduce the Company’s authorized ROE runs counter to the current capital market environment of increasing capital costs that are at their highest levels in at least seven years. My 11.00 percent ROE recommendation, on the other hand, reflects less than 30 percent of the 191-basis point increase in the 30-year Treasury bond yield since December 2019 when the Commission authorized the Company’s current 10.50 percent ROE. 
CAPITAL MARKET CONDITIONS
PLEASE SUMMARIZE THE economic and financial market conditions over the past several months since you submitted your Direct Testimony?
The conditions described in my direct testimony have continued since April 2022 and the primary capital market indicators continue to support higher return requirements by utility equity investors. Specifically, (1) inflation remains at levels not seen since the early 1980s, (2) interest rates across the yield spectrum have continued to increase, and (3) the elevated capital market volatility has sustained longer than anticipated. 
It is important to consider current and expected conditions in the general economy and financial markets because the authorized ROE for a public utility should allow the utility to attract investor capital at a reasonable cost under a variety of economic and financial market conditions. 
Please discuss these changes and root causes of these shifts in capital market conditions. 
Inflation has been both the greatest surprise and cause for concern among both policymakers and investors. As illustrated below, inflation levels have moved within a relatively narrow band over the past twenty years (other than during the Great Recession of 2007/2008). Starting with the initial stages of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020, inflation levels have been driven higher, reaching a peak of 9.0 percent in June of 2022 and leveling off at 7.7 percent in October 2022. Inflation is being driven by strong consumer demand and supply constraints, some of which have been exacerbated by COVID-19 related government stimulus programs in the U.S. and international lockdowns. More recently, the war in Ukraine, which began in February, is constraining the world’s supply of food and fuel, pushing overall inflation higher and feeding into the cost of other products and services.  
Figure 4: Consumer Price Index – All Urban, Not Seasonally Adjusted

As a result of these substantially higher inflation rates, the Federal Reserve has been left little choice but to pull back on its COVID-related monetary policies and battle inflation with its only effective tool: higher interest rates. In response to the economic effects of COVID-19, the Federal Reserve had decreased the federal funds rate in March 2020 to a target range of 0.00 percent to 0.25 percent (which remained in effect until March 2022) in addition to other stimulus measures which increased the supply of money in the economy. Since I prepared my ROE analysis provided in direct testimony (with market data through April 29, 2022), the Federal Reserve has continued unwinding its quantitative easing policy and increased the target rate six times, beginning with 25 basis points on March 17, 50 basis points on May 5, 75 basis points on June 16, another 75 basis points on July 27, another 75 basis points on September 21, and another 75 basis points on November 3 to a target range of 3.75 – 4.00 percent. Expectations are for further increases this year, with FOMC participants’ median projection of 4.375 percent by the end of 2022, increasing to 4.60 percent by the end of 2023. This demonstrates the level of Federal Reserve action necessary to reel in inflation. The Fed is willing to risk a slowdown in the economy, and it is clear that the era of record low interest rates and moderate inflation has closed. The 10-year bond, which closed out April 2022 at 2.89 percent, closed out October at 4.10 percent, and the 30-year yield, which closed out April at 2.96 percent, closed out October at 4.22 percent, both up substantially since I developed my direct testimony. Notably, the pace of the increase in Treasury bond yields escalated in September and October. For example, over the four months between the end of April and the end of August, the 10-year Treasury bond yield and 30-year Treasury bond yield increased by 26 and 31 basis points, respectively; however, over the most recent two months (end of August to end of October), the 10-year Treasury bond yield and 30-year Treasury bond yield both increased by 95 basis points, respectively. 
Equity markets have also been affected. The Chicago Board Options Exchange (“CBOE”) Volatility Index (“VIX”) climbed higher, from an average of 24.3 in April to 30.0 in October. For context, the VIX averaged 16.9 percent from 2010-2019, indicating increased levels of market volatility (and risk) for equity investors today. Because investors require higher returns to compensate them for greater risk, increased equity market volatility is indicative of higher return requirements for equities, including utilities. 
HAVE UTILITY STOCK PRICES ALSO BEEN MORE VOLATILE IN RECENT MONTHS? 
Yes, they have. The Coefficient of Variation is a measure of volatility defined as the ratio of the standard deviation to the mean over a given period. I calculated the Coefficient of Variation in the market price for the proxy group and the S&P 500 Utilities Index over a rolling 30-day period to assess the trend in utilities’ stock price volatility. As Figure 5 below shows, the Coefficient of Variation has been on an upward trend since the end of 2021 and has reached the highest point level since the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic in March 2020.
Figure 5: Utility Stock Price Volatility (2019-2022)

Mr. Gorman asserts that RISK PREMIUMS AS MEASURED BY credit spreads are “reasonably aligned with long-term historical averages.” DO YOU AGREE WITH HIS INTERPRETATION?
No, I do not. In his Table 12 on page 51, Mr. Gorman reviews annual average spreads between utility and corporate A-rated and Baa-rated bond yields and the 30-year Treasury yield from 2019 to 2022 and compares those to the average historical spread. However, his annual averages obscure variation over each year. Notably, his 2022 average masks the widening in credit spreads that have occurred over the course of 2022, resulting in his misleading conclusion that current credit spreads are “reasonably aligned with long-term historical averages.” As Figures 6 and 7 below show, utility credit spreads have widened considerably since January 2022 and are currently above both their 2022 annual average and long-term average. Widening credit spreads are an indication that investors are requiring higher premiums to invest in utility bonds over safer government bonds.
Figure 6: Credit Spread A-rated Utility – 30-Year Treasury Yield (Jan – Oct 2022)
Figure 7: Credit Spread Baa-rated Utility – 30-Year Treasury Yield (Jan – Oct 2022)

Are conditions expected to stabilize in the near-term? 
It does not appear so. While difficult to predict in the long-term, capital market conditions remain unsettled. The economy and capital markets remain in a tenuous state with ongoing global supply constraints and expectations of weaker consumer demand in the face of persistent inflation and higher interest rates. Interest rates may not fully reflect the effects of inflation because no one knows exactly how high interest rates must go before inflation returns to more acceptable levels. The Federal Reserve has been targeting inflation of around 2.0 percent for many years, as compared with current annualized inflation of 7.7 percent at the consumer level for the 12-month period ending in October 2022.
Looking forward, the inflation risks in the market are twofold: (1) either inflation becomes embedded in the economy if the Federal Reserve does not move aggressively enough in tightening monetary policy, or (2) the Federal Reserve responds to inflationary pressure by raising short-term interest rates to a level that causes a slowdown in economic growth or a recession. The November 2022 issue of Blue Chip Financial Forecasts (“Blue Chip”) reports the results of a survey of leading economists and market analysts taken in October 2022. Blue Chip reports that 19 percent think that by removing monetary accommodation, the Federal Reserve will be successful in slowing inflation in the U.S. without causing a recession, while 81 percent believe that monetary tightening will precipitate a U.S. recession.
mr. gorman and Ms. reno refer to recent utility stock performance to support their roe recommendations. How have utilities performed relative to the broader market in recent months? 
Mr. Gorman argues that utilities’ “robust valuations” are an indication that utilities can access equity capital at low cost. Ms. Reno observes that utility stocks outperformed the overall stock market between January and August 2022. While utility stocks caught up to the broad market in the first half of 2022, that changed quickly in September and October as utilities were one of the worst performing sectors of the S&P 500 in September and continued to underperform the market in October. As noted in my direct testimony, utilities often underperform the market in 12-month periods after top-decile monthly relative returns for the sector. As shown in Figure 8 below (an update to Figure 5 from my direct testimony), the S&P Utilities Index and The Southern Company’s stock price continue to lag behind the broader market compared to pre-pandemic levels. 
Figure 8: Relative Performance of Utilities and the S&P 500,
January 2020 – October 2022

As shown in Figure 9 below (an update to Figure 4 of my Direct Testimony), Beta coefficients for the proxy group and The Southern Company remain elevated.
Figure 9: Beta Coefficients for Proxy Group and The Southern Company 

Have you factored these circumstances into your ROE recommendation for Georgia Power AND, IF SO, what conclusions do you draw?
Yes. Long-term interest rates have increased substantially since the historic lows of 2020 and are expected to continue to increase as the Federal Reserve unwinds its pandemic response measures and focuses on inflation. This supports the use of both current and forecast bond yields in the CAPM and Risk Premium models. These circumstances also reinforce the importance of considering the results of multiple models, as I have with the DCF, CAPM, Risk Premium, and Expected Earnings approaches. By contrast, several of the Intervenor Witnesses have focused on one or two models, leading to an unnecessarily narrow view, especially under current market conditions that impact the models differently. On balance, my updated review of the capital market environment continues to support my recommendation for an 11 percent ROE and 56 percent equity ratio and their consistency with the Hope and Bluefield standards.
COST OF EQUITY ANALYSES
DO THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES ACCEPT YOUR PROXY GROUP?
Yes, the Intervenor Witnesses generally accept my proxy group as being reasonable. However, Mr. Gorman excludes American Electric Power Company (“AEP”), due to the pending sale of its Kentucky electric utility, and includes The Southern Company. As explained in my direct testimony, I disagree with the inclusion of Georgia Power’s parent company as it reflects circular logic.
is AEP’s pending sale of Kentucky Power company a SIGNIFICANT TRANSACTION That warrants AEP’s exclusion from the proxy group?
No, it is not. The primary reason to exclude companies on the basis of merger and acquisition activity is because significant transactions can affect the market data (e.g., stock prices) on which certain of the models rely. At the time the transaction was announced, AEP had a market capitalization of $42.23 billion; the Kentucky Power transaction is valued at $2.646 billion or only approximately 6.3 percent of AEP’s total market value at the time the transaction was announced. Consequently, the transaction is not significant enough to warrant AEP’s exclusion from the proxy group.
DCF MODEL
MS. RENO baseS HER ROE recommendations on the results of HER DCF analysis, while Mr. Gorman AND MR. ELLIS GIVE 50 PERCENT WEIGHT TO THEIR DCF model ESTIMATES IN DETERMINING THEIR ULTIMATE ROE RECOMMENDATIONS. Do you agree that it is appropriate to place this degree of reliance on the DCF model for a u.s. utility?
No, I do not. As discussed in my direct testimony, while many U.S. utility regulators have used the DCF model to establish the authorized ROE, several regulators, including FERC, have recognized the challenges associated with relying solely on the DCF to establish the authorized ROE for regulated utilities in recent years. For that reason, other federal and state regulators have relied on the results of multiple methodologies both to test the reasonableness of the DCF results and to establish a cost of equity that reflects investors’ required return on a going-forward basis. 
Please elaborate on your concerns with the DCF model under current market conditions.
Although I have included the DCF model in my analyses, I have concerns with the ability of the DCF model to produce reliable results under current market conditions. This concern is amplified with an ROE analysis or recommendation relying exclusively on the DCF model. As explained in my direct testimony, high utility valuations are expected to decline as utilities underperform the broader market and, under these circumstances, the results of the DCF model would understate the forward-looking cost of equity because the dividend yield is calculated using historical average stock prices which do not fully reflect these expectations. This shift has already begun to occur. 
Mr. Gorman observes the high valuations of electric utilities, referencing the higher than average P/E ratios. However, he disregards the effect of those high valuations on the results of the DCF model, in particular the dividend yield component. Exhibit__(MPG-3) page 4 demonstrates the decline in the Value Line electric utility dividend yields over the past decade, from 4.20 percent in 2012 to 3.36 percent in 2022. This indicates that investors are paying more for a dollar of earnings from electric utilities than they did 10 years ago. As interest rates increase from historic lows, these high valuations are likely to come under pressure and decline. As noted earlier, utility stocks have underperformed the broad market in September and October after outperforming earlier in 2022. As a result, my conclusion is that the DCF model is understating the forward-looking cost of equity for regulated utilities such as Georgia Power because the model is based on average historical stock prices that are based on unsustainable valuation levels.
Mr. Ellis regards high market-to-book (“M/B”) ratios as a sign that authorized ROEs for regulated utilities are higher than the investor required cost of equity. To test this premise, I performed an analysis that examines the correlation between government bond yields and the market-to-book ratios for electric utilities since 1980, using data provided in Exhibit__(MPG-15) and Exhibit__(MPG-18) to Mr. Gorman’s direct testimony. As shown in Figure 10, the R2 for this analysis is approximately 0.72, indicating a strong linear relationship between M/B ratios and interest rates. This relationship indicates that utility M/B ratios have increased not because authorized returns were higher than the true cost of equity, but because interest rates on government bonds have steadily declined for the past four decades. Low interest rates are favorable for capital-intensive industries such as utilities, while increasing interest rates are not.
Figure 10: Market-to-Book Ratios and Interest Rates

Growth Rates in the DCF Model
please comment on the intervenor witnesses’ use of growth rates in the DCF model.
Mr. Gorman and Ms. Reno consider a variety of growth rates including projected earnings per share (“EPS”), dividends per share (“DPS”), and book value per share, as well as “sustainable” growth rates and projected GDP growth. As explained in my direct testimony, over the long term, dividend growth can only be sustained by earnings growth, while dividend growth can depend on management decisions regarding the dividend payout ratio over the near-term which do not reflect the long-term growth prospects of the company. 
I agree, however, with Mr. Gorman’s and Ms. Reno’s decision to essentially discard the results of their Constant Growth DCF analysis that apply sustainable growth rates. I note that their sustainable growth rate calculations rely on Value Line’s projected ROE data for the proxy group companies. Those projected ROEs are substantially higher than the results of the DCF model using sustainable growth rates presented by any of the Intervenor Witnesses, and demonstrate the fact that investors are expecting to earn higher returns on equity from the proxy group companies than what is shown by the DCF model using “sustainable growth rates” calculated from the same source, Value Line. This is an inherent inconsistency in their sustainable growth approach to the DCF.
mr. ellis expresses concerns that analysts’ projected EPS growth rates are “optimistic,” while Mr. Gorman claims that long-term GDP growth serves as a cap on long-term EPS growth rates and suggests that short-term EPS growth rates are too high. Do you share those concerns about analysts’ projected EPS growth rates?
No, this is a familiar refrain, but unsubstantiated for utilities. The 2003 Global Analysts Research Settlement (the “Global Settlement”) served to significantly reduce the bias referred to by Mr. Ellis. In fact, the Global Settlement required financial institutions to insulate investment banking business from the analysis business, prohibited analysts from participating in “road shows,” and required the settling financial institutions to fund independent third-party research.
A 2010 article in Financial Analysts Journal found that analyst forecast bias declined significantly or disappeared entirely after the Global Settlement: 
Introduced in 2002, the Global Settlement and related regulations had an even bigger impact than Reg FD on analyst behavior. After the Global Settlement, the mean forecast bias declined significantly, whereas the median forecast bias essentially disappeared. Although disentangling the impact of the Global Settlement from that or related rules and regulations aimed at mitigating analysts’ conflicts of interest is impossible, forecast bias clearly declined around the time the Global Settlement was announced. These results suggest that the recent efforts of regulators have helped neutralize analysts’ conflicts of interest. 
In addition, analysts covering the common stock of the proxy companies certify that their analyses and recommendations are not related, either directly or indirectly, to their compensation. Thus, it is unclear why investors would assume that the proxy companies are susceptible to a continuing upward bias in earnings projections, especially given the fact that electric utilities operate in the mature stage of a stable industry with a very high degree of financial transparency due to their regulation. Further, to the extent Mr. Ellis believes that investors are well aware of these optimistic or biased growth rates, that suggests that utility stock prices already reflect that information.
Likewise, actual earnings data belie Mr. Gorman’s position that projected GDP growth represents a cap on long-term EPS growth. The suggestion that equity earnings are limited by future growth in GDP may hold for aggregate corporate earnings in a closed economy but these are not realistic assumptions for an individual firm nor for utilities in general. To illustrate this point, I have compared the actual historical EPS and dividend per share (“DPS”) growth rates (to the extent data was available through Value Line) of all U.S. electric utilities and the companies in my proxy group from 2011-2021 to historical and projected GDP growth rates from Blue Chip, the Energy Information Administration, and the Social Security Administration. The results are shown in Figure 11 below.
Figure 11: Analysis of EPS, DPS, and GDP Growth Rates

As shown in Figure 11, the EPS and DPS growth rates of utilities can, and do, exceed GDP growth for sustained periods. Specifically, for the Georgia Power proxy group, historical EPS has exceeded historical GDP growth by 0.93 percent from 2011-2021 and historical DPS has exceeded historical GDP growth by 1.98 percent over the same period. This rate of growth is 10 to 40 percent greater than GDP over this same period. My conclusion is that it is not unreasonable to rely on analyst EPS growth projections, as I and other experts commonly do, just because they exceed GDP growth.  
I do not share Mr. Ellis’ and Mr. Gorman’s concern that analysts’ projected EPS growth rates are too high. Mr. Gorman ultimately relies on analysts’ projected EPS growth rates in his Constant Growth DCF model, which forms the lower boundary of his recommended ROE range, effectively discarding the results of his Multi-Stage DCF model results that include projected GDP growth in the terminal stage. The average EPS growth rate that Mr. Gorman uses in his Constant Growth DCF model (5.45 percent) is consistent with the average EPS growth rates used in my Constant Growth DCF analysis (5.61 percent) and is more reasonable. His over-reliance on this result, however, unduly forces his overall ROE recommendation downward.
Please describe the issue with the application of sustainable growth or retention growth in the Constant Growth DCF model.
The sustainable growth rate or retention growth rate calculation assumes that future earnings will increase as the retention ratio increases. However, this relationship may not hold for a given company based on management decisions associated with the dividend payout rate. This conclusion is supported by two articles published in the Financial Analysts Journal that discussed the theory that high dividend payouts (i.e., low retention ratios) are associated with low future earnings growth. Each of those articles cited to a 2003 study by Arnott and Asness that found, over the course of 130 years of data, future earnings growth is associated with high, rather than low payout ratios. Specifically, Arnott and Asness concluded:
Unlike optimistic new-paradigm advocates, we found that low payout ratios (high retention rates) historically precede low earnings growth. This relationship is statistically strong and robust. We found that the empirical facts conform to a world in which managers possess private information that causes them to pay out a large share of earnings when they are optimistic that dividend cuts will not be necessary and to pay out a small share when they are pessimistic, perhaps so that they can be confident of maintaining the dividend payouts. Alternatively, the facts also fit a world in which low payout ratios lead to, or come with, inefficient empire building and the funding of less than-ideal projects and investments, leading to poor subsequent growth, whereas high payout ratios lead to more carefully chosen projects. The empire-building story also fits the initial macroeconomic evidence quite well. At this point, these explanations are conjectures; more work on discriminating among competing stories is appropriate.
Given that all three studies found that there is a negative relationship between earnings growth and retention ratios, the application of retention growth rates in the DCF model is not appropriate.
Multi-Stage DCF Approach
Mr. Gorman and Mr. Ellis have also presented the results of a Multi-Stage DCF model. Do you agree that the results and weight placed on those analyses are reasonable?
No, I do not. Mr. Gorman presents the results of a Multi-Stage DCF analysis but elects not to rely on those results in setting his range or recommendation for Georgia Power, presumably because he views the results as being too low. Mr. Ellis also performs a Multi-Stage DCF model, but unlike Mr. Gorman, he relies on those results for his ROE recommendation, even though his Multi-Stage DCF result of 5.97 percent is more than 450 basis points below Georgia Power’s current authorized ROE and significantly below any ROE authorized for any electric utility in at least the last 40 years. 
Mr. Ellis’s use of an inflation rate as a proxy for earnings growth has no reasonable basis of support, as he has provided no evidence that investors would accept growth in perpetuity that tracks inflation. There are many investment alternatives available that offer more attractive growth and return potential. As noted earlier, utility bond yields are currently offering returns comparable to or higher than Mr. Ellis’s DCF-based cost of equity recommendation. No investor would commit capital to an equity investment if they can receive an equal or higher return from a safer debt investment that has a senior claim on cash flow.
CAPM ANALYSIS
Before responding to the Intervenor Witnesses’ criticisms of your CAPM analysis, are there areas of agreement with respect to your respective CAPM analyses?
Yes, there are. First, Mr. Gorman, Ms. LaConte, Ms. Reno, and Mr. Ellis, and I agree that the 30-year Treasury bond yield is appropriate to use as the risk-free rate. Second, although certain of the Intervenor Witnesses disagree with the use of projected interest rates, Mr. Gorman includes a projection of the 30-year Treasury in his analysis, suggesting he believes that the current rate underestimates the expected risk-free rate. Third, Mr. Gorman, Ms. Reno, and Ms. LaConte each rely on Blume-adjusted Beta coefficients reported by Value Line.
Are CAPM results developed by certain Intervenor Witnesses reasonable?
No, they are not. In particular, Mr. Ellis’ CAPM result (5.41 percent) is well below the lowest authorized ROE for any electric utility in the last 40 years. Estimates as low as 5.41 percent are nearly 100 basis points below the reported cost of debt for utilities in October 2022. Debt investors require a return commensurate with the risk that the company will fail to meet that obligation. However, equity investors bear the residual risk associated with ownership, and have a claim on cash flows only after debt holders are paid. As such, equity holders bear greater risk and therefore require a higher return. The fact that Mr. Ellis’ CAPM analysis produces a result that is well below debt returns demonstrates his inputs are unreasonable and his results should be disregarded.
Risk-free rate
Mr. Ellis questions the accuracy of interest rate forecasts and objects to your use of a projected 30-year Treasury bond yield as the risk-free rate. How do you respond?
A.As explained earlier in this rebuttal testimony and in my direct testimony, I believe the use of projected 30-year Treasury bond yields as the risk-free rate in the CAPM analysis is appropriate because interest rates are expected to continue increasing as the Federal Reserve unwinds its balance sheet and tightens monetary policy. Notably, both the current and projected risk-free rates presented in my direct testimony (2.72 percent and 3.40 percent, respectively) are well below the 30-day average 30-year Treasury yield of 3.92 percent as of October 31, 2022. In addition, I do not share Mr. Ellis’ concerns with the accuracy of projected bond yields. This is because the cost of equity depends on what investors expect, not what actually happens. Nonetheless Mr. Ellis has not presented any evidence that current interest rates are any more accurate at predicting future interest rates in the long-term.
Further, even though I do not agree with the exclusive reliance on the current average Treasury bond yield as the risk-free rate, I note using current bond yields in the CAPM model produces results (shown in Exhibit JMC-5.2) well above the DCF model results and much higher than the CAPM results put forth by Mr. Gorman, Ms. Reno, Ms. LaConte, and Mr. Ellis. My conclusion is that it is reasonable and appropriate to use the projected 30-year Treasury bond yield as the risk-free rate. 
What is your response to MR. GORMAN’s criticism that your projected long-term risk-free rate is “unreasonable”?
Mr. Gorman’s criticism is puzzling. His CAPM estimate of 9.88 percent relied upon to develop his recommended ROE range uses a higher projected risk-free rate of 3.60 percent from Blue Chip published on August 1, 2022 than my 3.40 percent projected risk-free rate that he criticizes. If his position is that his higher projected long-term risk-free rate of 3.60 percent is more current than the risk-free rates I used in my direct testimony in the current market environment, I agree. Further, projected risk-free rates align with Georgia Power’s 2023 test year and Alternative Rate Plan that would be effective through 2025, contrary to Mr. Gorman’s position. 
Beta
How do the Intervenor Witnesses estimate Beta?
As discussed above, Ms. LaConte and Ms. Reno each rely on current Value Line Beta coefficients. Mr. Gorman considered current Value Line Beta coefficients before ultimately relying on a historical quarterly average of Value Line Beta coefficients between Q3 2014 and Q2 2022. Mr. Ellis relies on Zacks and Yahoo! Finance estimates of Beta that are calculated based on monthly returns, rather than the weekly convention used by Value Line and that I use with Bloomberg. 
Mr. Gorman observeS that current Beta coefficients from Value Line are higher than the historical average for the electric utility industry. Do you view this as a reason to adjust the current Beta coefficients?
No, I do not. Beta is the measure of relative risk in the CAPM analysis. The utility industry has typically had lower than average Beta coefficients because utilities generally tend to be less volatile than the broad market. However, as discussed in my direct testimony, that was not the case during the market dislocation that occurred in response to the COVID-19 pandemic. Five-year Beta coefficients from both Value Line and Bloomberg increased substantially in February and March 2020 to levels not seen since the financial crisis of 2008/2009 and have remained at those elevated levels ever since. In my view, there is no reason to use the longer-term average Beta coefficients, as suggested by Mr. Gorman. I have used Value Line and Bloomberg Beta coefficients calculated using five years of weekly return data against a broad market index (either the S&P 500 or the NYSE Composite). This five-year period pre-dates the COVID-19 period by 2.5 years, which suggests that the proxy group Beta coefficients are being affected by factors other than the pandemic. 
As discussed in my direct testimony, electric utilities have not served as a safe haven for investors during the recent economic downturn. According to Mr. Gorman’s Exhibit__(MPG-20) pages 2-5, Beta coefficients for the proxy group and the electric and natural gas utilities have stabilized at elevated levels since Q3 2020, contradicting Mr. Gorman’s position that utilities’ higher Beta coefficients are the result of just two anomalous months in March and April 2020. In my opinion, this shift may also be attributable to the market’s recognition of the complex challenges facing the industry in response to climate change, transitioning to a lower carbon generation mix, grid modernization, and shifting consumer preferences.
Further, I do not agree with Mr. Ellis’ concerns with Value Line’s methodology for calculating Beta coefficients, including the time period over which Betas are calculated, the market index, and the Blume adjustment. I note that Beta is a measure of relative risk in the CAPM analysis. While utilities have underperformed the broader market since January 2020, Beta coefficients demonstrate that utilities have traded more in line with the broad market than they have historically, indicating that utility investors recognize the structural shifts affecting the industry. 
WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE TO MR. ELLIS’ USE OF MONTHLY BETAS REPORTED BY YAHOO! FINANCE AND ZACKS?
By their nature, Beta coefficients calculated using monthly returns do not capture intra-month return volatility and could lead one to conclude utility stock returns are more stable than they actually are. Consider, for example, stock prices for two utilities – Utility A and Utility B – over a 60-trading day period (roughly three calendar months), illustrated in Figure 12 below. Both utilities start trading at a price of $100 per share. Both utilities report a stock price return of 11.69 percent at the end of three months. At the end of every month (the 20th trading day), both stocks have the same price. However, Utility B’s stock price is clearly more volatile (and therefore riskier) than Utility A’s. 
Figure 12: Illustrative Example of Weekly vs. Monthly Stock Volatility

If the Beta coefficient for Utility B was calculated using monthly returns, it would not capture intra-month stock price volatility and Utility B’s Beta coefficient would be the same as Utility A’s. On the other hand, if the Beta coefficients were calculated using weekly returns (every five trading days), Utility B’s Beta coefficient would be higher than Utility A’s, all else equal, better reflecting its higher volatility. In other words, Mr. Ellis suggests that the Commission should ignore evidence of increasing volatility and risk for utility companies, and the proxy group specifically. 
Market Risk Premium
The INTERVENOR WITNESSES challenge the forward-looking market risk premium you used in your CAPM analysis. Can you please respond to their concerns?
The use of a forward-looking or projected market risk premium (“MRP”) is appropriate because the historical average market risk premium does not reflect the inverse relationship between interest rates and the equity risk premium. The Ibbotson data used to calculate the historical MRP of 7.46 percent indicates that the long-term average return on large company stocks from 1926-2021 was 12.33 percent, while the average income-only return on government bonds was 4.87 percent over the same period. It is therefore not reasonable to use the historical MRP when the current average yield on the 30-year Treasury bond is lower than the bond yield used to calculate the historical MRP. With interest rates at these levels, the forward-looking MRP should be higher than 7.46 percent.
Second, the method I have used to calculate the forward-looking MRP is similar to the methodology used by FERC in Opinion No. 531-B. Specifically, the forward-looking MRP in my CAPM analysis is derived by calculating the expected total return for the companies in the S&P 500 Index less the projected risk-free rate. Ms. LaConte adjusts my forward-looking MRP to exclude companies with negative growth rates and growth rates above 20 percent. However, when investors purchase the S&P 500 Index or a mutual fund or exchange traded fund that mirrors the S&P 500 Index, their total return is based on the returns for all 500 companies in the S&P 500 Index, not only those companies with positive EPS growth rates or growth rates less than 20 percent. Further, my MRP calculation is internally consistent because the Betas used in my CAPM analysis are calculated against all companies in the S&P 500 Index or the NYSE Composite Index, not just against those companies that have positive growth rates or growth rates less than 20 percent.
The Intervenor Witnesses argue that the total market return used in my forward-looking MRP calculation are not reasonable. To assess their reasonableness, I analyzed the annual performance of the S&P 500 from 1926-2021. As shown in Figure 13 below, the actual return on the S&P 500 Index has exceeded 15 percent in 50 percent (48 out of 96) of the years from 1926-2021. These data demonstrate that actual total returns for the broad market greater than 15 percent are not uncommon.
Figure 13: Total Returns of S&P 500 Index – 1926-2021
 
Mr. Gorman, Ms. LaConte, and Ms. Reno apply market risk premia based on historical data. How do you respond?
As explained earlier, the use of a historical market risk premium is not appropriate under current market conditions because it does not reflect the inverse relationship between interest rates and the equity risk premium, nor the earnings growth for today’s S&P 500 companies. When the current average yield on U.S. Treasury bonds is below the long-term historical average yield, it is reasonable to expect that the MRP would be above the historical average market risk premium. Consequently, the long-term average historical market risk premium would be appropriate only if the expected risk-free rate was similar to the long-term historical risk-free rate. Further, equity market returns calculated over the last century do not represent the growth available today to U.S. companies from international markets and the roles of technology and innovation in driving earnings growth.  
Do you agree with Mr. Gorman and Ms. Reno’s reference to kroll’s recommended equity risk premium of 5.50 percent?
No, I do not. As a case in point, Figure 14 below demonstrates just how far off Kroll’s recommended market equity risk premium has been over the past seven years in relation to the forward-looking approach I have used. Kroll’s recommended market equity risk premium has substantially underestimated actual market performance over several averaging periods. 
Figure 14: Market Equity Risk Premium Methods

Further, it is not clear that Kroll develops its recommended market equity risk premium in relation to its risk-free rate. Because the market equity risk premium is a function of the market return and the risk-free rate at a point in time, the market equity risk premium is related to the risk-free rate (as explained earlier, there is an inverse relationship between the two). As Figure 15 below shows, there is no apparent relationship between Kroll’s recommended market equity risk premium and risk-free rate.  
Figure 15: Kroll’s Recommended Market Equity Risk Premium and Risk-Free Rate 
(2008-2022)

What is your conclusion with regard to the CAPM?
My conclusion is that using reasonable forward-looking inputs for the risk-free rate and MRP, along with current Betas from Value Line and Bloomberg, the CAPM is producing results that should be considered along with the results from the DCF, Risk Premium, and Expected Earnings models.
RISK PREMIUM MODEL
the Intervenor Witnesses challenge the use of a Risk Premium model such as the one you have presented, or they contend that your application of the Risk Premium model is not reasonable. How do you respond to their concerns?
Mr. Gorman expresses several concerns with my Risk Premium analysis, including: (1) he disputes the inverse relationship between interest rates and risk premia; (2) he claims that, while academic studies have shown that in the past there was such an inverse relationship, the relationship has changed over time, particularly since interest rate volatility is not as extreme as it was in the 1980s; and (3) he contends that I have ignored investment risk differentials in my regression analyses, and that my adjustment to the equity risk premium is based exclusively on changes in nominal interest rates.
In response to Mr. Gorman’s first concern, there is a large body of research in addition to my own statistical analyses that supports the inverse relationship between interest rates and equity risk premia, including the March 1998 article published by Dr. S. Keith Berry, which came to similar conclusions regarding the inverse relationship between interest rates and the risk premia. Several other studies were published after those that Mr. Gorman cites as evidence that this inverse relationship is a relic of the 1980s. As summarized in Dr. Roger Morin’s New Regulatory Finance, two of these studies were published in 2005, demonstrating that the inverse relationship between interest rates and the equity risk premium are contemporary concepts in finance:
Published studies by Brigham, Shome, and Vinson (1985), Harris (1986), Harris and Marston (1992, 1993), Carleton, Chambers, and Lakonishok (1983), Morin (2005), and McShane (2005), and others demonstrate that, beginning in 1980, risk premiums varied inversely with the level of interest rates—rising when rates fell and declining when interest rates rose. The reason for this relationship is that when interest rates rise, bondholders suffer a capital loss. This is referred to as interest rate risk…. Conversely in low interest rate environments, when bondholders’ interest rate fears subside and shareholders’ fears of loss of earning power dominate, the risk differential will widen and hence the risk premium will increase. 
Furthermore, my Risk Premium analysis has an R2 of approximately 0.84, which indicates that there is a high degree of correlation between the observed equity risk premium and changes in interest rates. 
With regard to Mr. Gorman’s statement that interest rate volatility was more extreme in the 1980s than it is today, I conducted an analysis that compares the volatility in 30-year Treasury bond yields in each year during the 1980s to the volatility from 2019 to present. As shown in Figure 16, the relative standard deviation of Treasury bond yields is substantially higher in 2022 than it was during any year in the 1980s, indicating that interest rate volatility is higher in recent years than it was in the 1980s, and has been higher since 2019 than all but one year during the 1980s (i.e., 1982).
Figure 16: Treasury Bond Yield Volatility

In response to Mr. Gorman’s third concern, he contends that I have ignored investment risk differentials in my regression analyses, and that my adjustment to the equity risk premium is based exclusively on changes in nominal interest rates. I agree that my analysis is based solely on the relationship to interest rates, as is Mr. Gorman’s Risk Premium analysis. Mr. Gorman’s Risk Premium analysis also does not consider any other variables except average authorized ROEs and bond yields. In my analysis, with an R2 of 0.84, the relationship to interest rates accounts for 82 percent of the change in awarded ROEs, which is quite strong. In addition, the inclusion of 682 rate cases over a 30-year period provides a broad perspective on case-specific issues. To the extent that shifts in industry risk are left out of this equation, the recent increases in utility betas would suggest that the Risk Premium results are biased downwards, and would likely understate the cost of equity. This relationship is picked up directly, however, in the CAPM model, and these results are meaningfully higher. Therefore, I believe the model is useful as a method to estimate the cost of equity for electric utilities like Georgia Power, along with the results from the DCF, CAPM, and Expected Earnings models.
DOES MR. GORMAN’S RISK PREMIUM ANALYSIS SUPPORT THE INVERSE RELATIONSHIP between the equity risk premium and bond yields?
Yes, it does. Mr. Gorman’s Risk Premium analysis is conceptually similar to mine in that he calculates the risk premium as the difference between authorized ROEs and Treasury bond yields (as well as utility bond yields). As Figure 17 below shows, Mr. Gorman’s annual 30-year Treasury bond yield and indicated risk premium data in Exhibit__(MPG-16) show a strong negative, inverse relationship. 
Figure 17: Mr. Gorman’s Treasury Bond Yield and Indicated Risk Premium

Ms. Laconte adjusts your risk premium analysis to combine the long-term average equity risk premium of 6.06 percent with your projected 30-year Treasury bond yield. is her adjustment appropriate?
No, it is not. The risk premium model is designed to adjust the risk premium for any given level of bond yield, based on the historical relationship between the two variables over the last 30 years. Adding the long-term average to a current or projected bond yield does not appropriately reflect the statistical relationship between the equity risk premium and 30-year Treasury bond yield. As noted earlier in my discussion of the market risk premium in the CAPM, the equity risk premium and bond yields are inversely related. The only time the historical average equity risk premium is appropriate is when the current or projected bond yield is approximately equal to the historical average bond yield. Ms. LaConte’s adjustment to my Risk Premium analysis is inappropriate and should be rejected.
EXPECTED EARNINGS ANALYSIS
Before responding to the criticisms of your Expected Earnings analysis, do any of the Intervenor Witnesses perform a similar analysis?
Yes, Ms. Reno’s Comparable Earnings Model (“CEM”) reviews historical and forecasted earned returns on book value and market-to-book ratios for the same proxy group of companies that I use. Her 2025-2027 proxy group projected CEM results of 10.50 percent to 10.86 percent are reasonably consistent with my Expected Earnings results presented in Exhibit JMC-7 that range from 11.03 (mean) to 11.08 percent (median). However, Ms. Reno does not give these results any weight in developing her 9.47 percent recommendation, which forces her overall ROE recommendation downward.
PLEASE SUMMARIZE THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES’ POSITIONS with the use of an Expected Earnings analysis to estimate the cost of equity for Georgia Power in this proceeding. 
Mr. Gorman, Mr. Ellis, and Ms. LaConte disagree with the Expected Earnings analysis because they believe it does not measure the market cost of equity capital. In addition, Mr. Gorman contends that “the earned return on book equity is simply not an accurate or legitimate basis upon which to determine a fair and reasonable return on equity for both investors and customers.” Mr. Ellis further asserts that it has been rejected by the FERC and therefore is not appropriate.  
What is your response to these concerns related to the Expected Earnings approach?
The Expected Earnings approach provides an expected return for like-risk companies, which is a core strength of the model and consistent with the basic tenets of Hope, which requires that “the return to the equity owner should be commensurate with returns on investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.” In the context of considering regulated utility investment opportunities, an investor would naturally consider both current market valuations in deciding between companies of like risk and the value of the expected return on book value. 
The Hope and Bluefield standards establish that a utility should be granted the opportunity to earn a return that is commensurate with the return on other investments of similar risk. However meritorious the theoretical criticism of the Expected Earnings methodology in the context of comparing non-utility investments, the Intervenor Witnesses fail to acknowledge the regulated utility’s authorized book return does, in fact, materially affect the earnings and earnings growth of a regulated utility. The Expected Earnings approach provides valuable insight into the opportunity cost of investing in Georgia Power’s electric utility operations precisely because of the regulated context of electric utility investment opportunities. 
If investors decide to devote capital to the Company (which would offer a return of only 5.54 percent on book value if Mr. Ellis’ recommendation was adopted), they forgo the opportunity for that same capital to earn a potentially greater return on book value through investment in the proxy companies. As a result, the Expected Earnings approach is informative because it provides a measure of the return on book value that is available to investors through other investments with comparable risk to Georgia Power.
As Dr. Morin explains, the Expected Earnings approach (also referred to as the Comparable Earnings approach below and by Ms. Reno) is highly meaningful in a regulatory setting similar to the one being used to set the cost of equity for Georgia Power:
The Comparable Earnings approach is far more meaningful in the regulatory arena than in the sphere of competitive firms. Unlike industrial companies the earnings requirement of utilities is determined by applying a percentage rate of return to the book value of a utility’s investment, and not on the market value of that investment. Therefore, it stands to reason that a different percentage rate of return than the market cost of capital be applied when the investment base is stated in book value terms rather than market value terms. In a competitive market, investment decisions are taken on the basis of market prices, market values, and market cost of capital. If regulation’s role was to duplicate the competitive result perfectly, then the market cost of capital would be applied to the current market value of rate base assets employed by utilities to provide service. But because the investment base for ratemaking purposes is expressed in book value terms, a rate of return on book value, as is the case with Comparable Earnings, is highly meaningful.
Further, in developing his sustainable growth rates for the DCF model, Mr. Gorman assumes the reasonableness of the projected returns on book equity from Value Line for the proxy group companies, which are the same returns that he dismisses as unreliable in the Expected Earnings analysis.
Lastly, although the FERC has not included the Expected Earnings analysis in its most recent orders setting its ROE methodology (i.e., Opinion Nos. 569-A and 569-B) for electric transmission companies, FERC has left the door open for presentation of an Expected Earnings analysis on a case-by-case basis. In my view, the Expected Earnings analysis provides a more stable picture of the returns that investors are expecting for companies in the Electric Utility sector based on Value Line data. This stability is due to Value Line’s analysis and projections which change when updated, in contrast to the CAPM and DCF results which shift with more volatile market data. Moreover, as explained in this section, the use of accounting returns is appropriate because the authorized ROE is being applied to an accounting rate base in order to determine the net income a company is authorized to recover in rates. For all of these reasons, I continue to support the use of an Expected Earnings analysis as one model to estimate the cost of equity for Georgia Power in this proceeding.
CAPITAL STRUCTURE AND COST OF DEBT
PLEASE SUMMARIZE THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES’ CAPITAL STRUCTURE RECOMMENDATIONS.
Ms. Reno accepts the Company’s proposed capital structure. Mr. Gorman accepts Georgia Power’s requested capital structure for 2023 until Vogtle units 3 and 4 are in service. He recommends, however, that the Commission authorize an equity ratio of 51 percent for 2024 and 2025. Ms. LaConte recommends the Commission authorize a capital structure that consists of no more than 53 percent common equity. Lastly, Mr. Ellis recommends a capital structure of 61.70 percent with his 5.54 percent ROE recommendation. 
As to the cost of long-term debt, only Ms. Reno rejects the Company’s proposed cost of debt. She recommends that Georgia Power’s proposed 2023 cost of debt of 3.53 percent be applied for the entire term of the Alternative Rate Plan (ARP) and that the cost of debt should not increase in 2024 and 2025.
ON WHAT BASIS DOES MR. GORMAN DEVELOP HIS RECOMMENDED 51 PERCENT EQUITY RATIO FOR 2024 AND 2025?
Mr. Gorman’s recommendation is based on his review of authorized equity ratios for electric and gas utilities between 2010 and 2022, as well as adjusted debt ratios reported by S&P for electric gas, and water utilities. He concludes that adjusting Georgia Power’s ratemaking capital structure to reflect a 51 percent equity ratio in 2024 and 2025 “would be consistent with the normalized industry ratemaking capital structure” and support the Company’s current bond rating, or even improve it.
Mr. Gorman’s recommendation to reduce Georgia power’s ratemaking capital structure in 2023 and 2024 is based, IN PART, on a review of national average authorized capital structure for electric and natural gas utilities. Please comment on that comparison.
Mr. Gorman’s analysis is not an apples-to-apples comparison to Georgia Power’s proposed capital structure. First, his analysis includes authorized equity ratios for all rate case types reported by Regulatory Research Associates, including electric T&D rate cases and limited issue rate rider rate cases that are not base rate cases. Electric T&D utilities have a different risk profile than vertically integrated electric utilities. As Figure 18 shows, the average annual authorized equity ratios for vertically integrated electric utilities are higher than Mr. Gorman’s annual averages that include electric T&D utilities.

Further, although Mr. Gorman removed rate case decisions from states that include non-investor supplied capital in the ratemaking capital structure, his analysis includes decisions that, unlike Georgia Power, also use short-term debt in the ratemaking capital structure, biasing the authorized equity ratio downward. 
Nonetheless, the range of authorized equity ratios for vertically integrated electric utilities reported by RRA since 2010 has been from 40.25 percent to 59.09 percent. BUSINESS RISK AND FLOTATION COSTS
Do you agree with Mr. Gorman’s contention that credit ratings take into account all business and financial risks that are relevant to investors?
No. Credit ratings, while important, are not the only consideration in assessing business or financial risk, and the risks for equity investors are not the same as the risks for bondholders. Credit ratings are assessments of the likelihood a company could default on its debt, whereas, the purpose of setting an ROE is to determine the riskiness and cost of the Company’s equity. Equity investors are more concerned with earnings and rate base growth, regulatory support for recovery of prudently incurred costs, the strength of the local economy and housing markets, capital spending, changes in interest rates, changes in long-term weather patterns, and more recently exposure and opportunities related to decarbonization of the industry. Bondholders are primarily concerned with stability and predictability of cash flows and timeliness of cost recovery as those affect a utility’s ability to meet its fixed obligations. 
S&P states that it puts a negative ratings modifier on Georgia Power and ranks the Company at the lower end of S&P’s range for the excellent business risk category based upon Georgia Power’s exposure to energy transition risk and the risks associated with the completion of Vogtle. Further, while I have considered Georgia Power’s relative regulatory risk, it is important to recognize that I did not make an adjustment to my ROE recommendation for business risk. Instead, I relied on the mean results of the four financial models I used to estimate the cost of equity for Georgia Power.
Several Intervenor Witnesses argue that the Company’s Alternative Rate Plan and rate structures mitigate Georgia Power’s risk. What is your response?
It is important to remember that the cost of equity is a comparative analysis. As such, the relevant point of comparison is the Company’s risk relative to its peers. As explained in my direct testimony and shown in Exhibit JMC-9, a substantial majority of the proxy group companies employ a variety of rate structures and mechanisms to mitigate regulatory lag. As demonstrated in my direct testimony, the regulatory structures available to Georgia Power are no different from the perspective of the investment community than those in place at the proxy companies. As a result, Georgia Power is no less risky than the proxy companies on account of its regulatory mechanisms. Therefore, contrary to these Intervenor Witnesses, my testimony and recommendation accounts for Georgia Power’s regulatory mechanisms.
THE Intervenor Witnesses DISAGREE WITH THE INCLUSION OF FLOTATION COSTS. WHY IS IT APPROPRIATE AND NECESSARY TO CONSIDER FLOTATION COSTS IN THE AUTHORIZED ROE?
Flotation costs are the direct out-of-pocket costs associated with the sale of new issues of common stock. In the same way that costs associated with debt issuances are recovered through the cost of debt, equity issuance costs should also be recovered. Flotation costs are not expenses, so they do not appear on the income statement. Instead, they are reflected on the balance sheet under “Paid in Capital.” Further, flotation costs are accumulated over time and remain on the balance sheet even if no new equity issuances are planned. For these reasons, I continue to include an adjustment for flotation costs in my ROE recommendation.
CONCLUSIONS
What are your conclusionS regarding a fair ROE for Georgia Power?
The only reliable method for determining the cost of capital is through the application of rigorous analysis using financial models and market data from reliable sources. Additionally, the Commission’s cost of capital determination should consider the general economic and capital market environment and the influence capital market conditions exert over the results of the ROE estimation models. Interest rates on government and utility bonds have increased substantially in 2022 and projections suggest a continued upward pressure in the coming years. This increase in the capital costs, and other risk factors indicate that the uncertainty and volatility in financial markets have caused equity investors to require a higher rate of return, including for electric utilities.
My recommended ROE of 11.0 percent and capital structure with a common equity ratio of 56.0 percent is fair and reasonable for Georgia Power. This capital structure is consistent with the Company’s actual equity ratio, and combined with the authorized ROE will support continued financial strength and access to debt and equity capital to meet the Company’s operating requirements.
Does this conclude your REBUTTAL testimony?
Yes, it does.

===== 2022 Rate Case Rebuttal Testimony - Fetter FINAL.docx =====
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF
STEVEN M. FETTER
ON BEHALF OF GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
DOCKET NO. 44280

INTRODUCTION
Q.Please state your name, position, and business address. 
A.My name is Steven M. Fetter. My business address is 1240 West Sims Way, Port Townsend, Washington 98104. 
Q.DID YOU PREVIOUSLY FILE TESTIMONY IN THIS PROCEEDING?
A.Yes. I submitted direct testimony to the Georgia Public Service Commission (the “Commission”) on behalf of Georgia Power Company (“Georgia Power” or the “Company”) on June 24, 2022.
II.PURPOSE OF REBUTTAL TESTIMONY
Q.What is the purpose of your rebuttal testimony?
A.The purpose of my rebuttal testimony is to respond to the following points testified to by Kevin O’Donnell and Michael Gorman in their direct testimonies on behalf of the Georgia Public Service Commission Public Interest Advocacy Staff (“PIA Staff”):
I draw a different conclusion than Mr. O’Donnell when he states, “As can be seen in the above chart [Chart 1], most utilities are in the A-, BBB+, and BBB range.” (page 5);
I disagree with Mr. O’Donnell’s assessment that, “As can be seen in Table 3 Georgia Power’s credit rating is similar to other southeast electric utilities.” (pages 5-6);
Mr. O’Donnell is not accurate when he states that the position I took in the 2019 rate case about potential downgrades was shown to be inaccurate by an S&P downgrade of Georgia Power on October 27, 2021, and a Moody’s downgrade “on August 8, 2019, just prior to the final order being issued in the last Company rate case in December, 2019.” (pages 19-24);
Mr. O’Donnell’s reference to the Moody’s downgrade noted above is inaccurate: it is off by twelve months. Moody’s downgraded Georgia Power on August 8, 2018, not August 8, 2019, and thus the negative rating action occurred 16 months prior to the Commission’s Final Order in the 2019 rate case. (page 20); 
I disagree with Mr. O’Donnell’s rejection of my views related to Georgia Power’s credit ratings because this rejection is based solely on his quantification of interest costs compared to rate costs to consumers; by adopting this approach, Mr. O’Donnell ignores the approximate 50% weighting of qualitative factors that is part and parcel of the analysis and assignment of credit ratings. (pages 24-27);
I disagree with Mr. Gorman’s financial recommendations of authorized ROE of 9.45% and equity layer moving down to 51%. (page 2); and
I disagree with Mr. Gorman when he states, “Mr. Fetter provided no substance or evidence that supports his conclusion that GPC’s credit rating and financial integrity are only achieved if the Company’s return on equity and proposed ratemaking capital structure are adopted.” (page 27)
III.DISCUSSION
Q.What is your overall conclusion in this rebuttal testimony? 
A.Georgia Power’s credit ratings now straddle above and below the bottom of my recommended credit rating range of ratings no lower than BBB+. For this reason, I encourage the Commission to set Georgia Power’s equity level and authorized return on equity consistent with Mr. Coyne’s recommendation of an 11% ROE and the Company (Abramovitz/Adams/Houston/Robinson) Panel’s recommendation of 56% equity ratio. In my opinion, the Company’s recommendations are designed to maintain the Company’s financial integrity with the potential of improvement over time. 
Mr. O’Donnell supports PIA Staff witness Gorman’s recommended 9.45% ROE with an equity layer that sinks down from 56% to 51%. I agree with Company witnesses Abramovitz and Adams that Mr. O’Donnell’s recommendations would result in significant movement down from Georgia Power’s current financial position. Mr. O’Donnell’s recommendations, if adopted, would also result in a negative impact to the qualitative side of credit rating analysis. In short, Mr. O’Donnell’s recommendation would represent such a stark undermining of the Company’s financial integrity that it would likely be interpreted by the rating agencies as an abrupt departure from the Commission’s long record of constructive regulation characterized by consistency and gradualism in balancing the interests of customers and investors and would be very detrimental to the Company’s overall credit rating profile. 
Q.MR. O’DONNELL PROVIDES TWO TABLES IN HIS TESTIMONY TO SUPPORT HIS VIEW THAT ALL IS WELL AT GEORGIA POWER. YOU INDICATE THAT YOU DISAGREE WITH HIS ASSESSMENT. PLEASE EXPLAIN. 
A.In both Table 3 (“Georgia Power Comparison to Southeast Electric Utilities”) and Chart 1 (“Utility Credit Ratings”), Mr. O’Donnell attempts to show a stable ratings environment for Georgia Power by submitting an incomplete picture. Based solely on his Table 3, Mr. O’Donnell states: “Georgia Power’s credit rating is similar to other southeast electric utilities.” (pages 5-6). This is not accurate. Mr. O’Donnell’s Table 3 ignores the Company’s lowest rating, BBB from Fitch. Below I have provided a version of his list, modified to include Fitch ratings, to show the disparity I have described:

Q.IN ADDITION, DO YOU BELIEVE THAT MR. O’DONNELL’S CHART 1 FURTHER SUPPORTS YOUR CONCERNS ABOUT georgia power’S CREDIT RATINGS?
A.Yes. Mr. O’Donnell’s Chart 1 is provided below:
