In Discussing his Table 1, Mr. O’Donnell states: “As can be seen…, most utilities are in the A-, BBB+, and BBB range.” (page 5). In view of my longstanding position that regulated utilities should target having credit ratings no lower than BBB+, I think a more appropriate analysis of this chart shows that the majority of regulated utilities hold ratings of BBB+ or above – 226 – as compared to those rated below BBB+ – 91. In fact, those below BBB+ include 41 at levels no utility management, investor, or regulator would find to be acceptable: 20 below investment grade, plus 21 at the very precarious lowest investment grade rating level of BBB-. For these marginally rated utilities, one operational or financial setback, one major storm, or one cyber security attack could drive the utility below investment grade, requiring a substantial number of its investors to modify their equity/debt holdings. With Georgia Power straddling that BBB+ / BBB dividing line, now is not the time to adopt a position – as advocated by Mr. O’Donnell – that would all but guarantee that Georgia Power would for the short- and medium-term remain among the precarious marginal utilities that have little to no financial margin to mitigate the impacts of unforeseen adverse events. 
Q.IN DESCRIBING YOUR TESTIMONY FROM THE COMPANY’S 2019 RATE CASE, DID MR. O’DONNELL MISCHARACTERIZE ANY PART OF YOUR TESTIMONY? 
A. Yes. In reference to my testimony during the 2019 Georgia Power rate case, Mr. O’Donnell incorrectly states: “The high ROE and equity ratio in the last rate case did not, contrary to Mr. Fetter’s statements in the 2019 case, prevent a credit downgrade.” (page 24). First and importantly, Mr. O’Donnell is factually incorrect. In his testimony Mr. O’Donnell stated that the Moody’s downgrade occurred “on August 8, 2019, just prior to the final order being issued in the last Company rate case in December, 2019.” This statement is not accurate. The Moody’s downgrade was announced on August 8, 2018, which was 16 months before the Commission’s December 2019 decision in the Georgia Power rate case, and, indeed, more than a year before the initial hearing and before PIA Staff filed any testimony in the 2019 rate case. At the same time, prior to the 2019 rate case, S&P (on March 25, 2017) and Fitch (on September 28, 2018) had already placed Negative outlooks on their Georgia Power ratings, signaling the potential direction of a credit rating change within the foreseeable future. The timing of the Moody’s action and those Negative outlooks are relevant. They indicate that the financial strength of the Company had already been weakening well before the 2019 rate case, and they help explain why, after a relatively supportive regulatory decision at the end of 2019, downgrades at S&P and Fitch might occur. 
In addition to this inaccuracy, Mr. O’Donnell completely mischaracterizes my 2019 rate case testimony to the Commission. Below is a full quote from my 2019 testimony: 
In order for Georgia Power to be able to maintain its current ratings range across all three agencies, I encourage the Commission to set the ROE and capital structure at levels consistent with Dr. Vander Weide’s recommendations. Such a Commission determination would show a continuation of the strong regulatory support that Georgia Power has long experienced, which should avoid further weakening in the Company’s credit profile. On the other hand, a less than constructive decision by the Commission in this case could lead to further negative credit rating actions, potentially moving the Company below the ‘BBB+’ / ‘Baa1’ rating level that I have long testified represents the lowest appropriate credit rating level for a U.S. regulated utility. (pages 22-23).
What is important to note is that in my prior testimony, I was referring to Dr. James Vander Weide’s recommendation that the Commission set an authorized ROE of 10.9% with an equity level of 56%. The Company ultimately agreed to a settlement resolving all issues except the Company’s set point ROE and equity ratio, which were deferred to the Commission, which authorized an ROE of 10.5% and an equity ratio of 56%. Even though my recommendation was superseded by the settlement, I was still hopeful that the lower, albeit still supportive, Commission-ordered financial parameters would overcome the pending Negative outlooks at S&P and Fitch. Unfortunately, they could not, with S&P bringing Georgia Power’s rating to BBB+ and Fitch going even lower at BBB. (Moody’s, of course, had acted long before both my testimony and the Commission decision.) I still firmly believe that the financial enhancement that would have been provided by Dr. Vander Weide’s recommended 10.9% ROE level could have forestalled Fitch’s move to BBB, below my recommended range of no lower than BBB+. 
Q.YOU ALSO CHALLENGE MR. O’DONNELL’S APPROACH OF FOCUSING ON THE FINANCIAL SIDE OF CREDIT RATINGS WITHOUT CONSIDERING THE QUALITATIVE FACTORS INVOLVED IN THE RATINGS PROCESS. PLEASE EXPLAIN.
A.While credit rating agencies certainly give great weight to financial metrics when formulating their credit ratings, Mr. O’Donnell has not given appropriate consideration to qualitative rating factors, most especially “Regulation.” Based on my leadership role at a major rating agency, I know that the qualitative assessment is given virtually equal weight as the quantitative factors in coming to a rating determination. As I discussed in my direct testimony, “Regulation” is the most important qualitative factor. Mr. O’Donnell supports Mr. Gorman’s proposed significant downward movement in Georgia Power’s financial measures, while both of them ignore the qualitative impacts. This is a mistake, and, in my view, has resulted in incomplete analyses by those two witnesses. If adopted by this Commission, Mr. Gorman’s recommendations would represent a clear break from the Commission’s past constructive decision-making. The likely result would be negative actions by the rating agencies as they recognize both the less supportive financial measures as well as qualitative judgments pointing toward a potential reordering of the state’s regulatory philosophy. 
Q.WOULD SUCH A MAJOR RESETTING OF THE GEORGIA REGULATORY CLIMATE HAVE A SIGNIFICANT EFFECT ON GEORGIA POWER’s day-to-day OPERATIONs?
A.Yes, and the key reason is “Access” to the capital markets. During the past 15 years, we have seen even “A”-rated utilities encounter issues regarding access to capital markets during times of stress such as the 2008-2009 recession and the economic concerns that accompanied the early onset of the COVID contagion. At cross-examination of my direct testimony, I shared the story of Entergy New Orleans (“ENO”) pre-and-post Hurricane Katrina. Prior to Katrina, ENO was a financially improving regulated utility that had solidified its Moody’s credit rating at the Baa3 investment-grade level with expected near-term positive movement. Katrina hit New Orleans during the last week of August 2005. Within three weeks, ENO’s rating had fallen to Ca, far below investment-grade level. With its access to the capital markets completely shut off, parent Entergy Corp. moved ENO into bankruptcy, which then opened the way for the parent to begin funding ENO’s long struggle back toward financial stability. It is important to note that all of my talk about the precarious nature of BBB- ratings can be seen within ENO’s saga – that utility has had its ups and downs since Katrina, but as of now, 17 years later, ENO sits below investment grade in the Ba / BB category at both Moody’s and S&P. 
With access in mind, I attempted to follow Mr. O’Donnell’s complex financial analysis (pages 25-27) about the cost of a downgrade compared to the potential cost to customers resulting from this rate case. I found it interesting that, if I understand his methodology correctly, Mr. O’Donnell is calculating Georgia Power’s potential movement down through the BBB category, with each step equal in financial pain. Thus, he sets the cost of a downgrade from Baa1 to Baa2 as 14 basis points of interest, resulting in additional financing cost for the Company of $96 million. He then compares this to his hypothetical calculated increased customer rate cost of $1.26 billion. He at no time states that a Baa1 utility (which would be within my recommended range) would be a much more attractive investment target for the financial community than would be the declining number of utilities holding Baa2 ratings. I have always believed that the best way to test a hypothesis that appears compelling, but which one fears might be suspect, is to take the assumptions another step further, and even still further (eventually to absurd levels), which I will proceed to do.
I noted earlier the precarious situation a regulated utility would find itself in with a rating of Baa3 – the lowest investment-grade level and thus the bane of investors who fear that one notch down puts the rating “off a cliff.” Taking Mr. O’Donnell’s assumptions, financing at the anxiety-producing Baa3 level would cost another 14 basis points, or $96 million more, providing this comparison: additional financing costs of $192 million compared to consumer costs of $1.26 billion. Now, let’s take this to the absurd level – Georgia Power and its regulators, with the encouragement of some intervenors, decide to allow the Company’s credit rating to free fall to below investment-grade level at BB. The chart below demonstrates the differential in the cost of 10-year debt going down into the junk bond level of the BB category over the past decade.
