As this chart shows, the increase in interest cost between the BBB and BB categories varies depending upon capital markets conditions but approximately spans the range of 10% to 60% more. I would estimate that a significant percentage of investment institutions would no longer be permitted to invest in such below investment-grade bonds – or would have to modify their current holdings going forward. Utilizing the above data, one could multiply the negative cost of Mr. O’Donnell’s one-notch downgrade analysis by 100% and still show a preference for the increased interest costs at close to $400 million for the three-notch downgrade versus consumer rate costs of $1.26 billion, all the while basically eliminating most of the outside financing access then available to the Company. At cross-examination on his testimony, Mr. O’Donnell even suggested that if the Company’s ratings were to fall too low to access traditional financing, there would always be the option to run its multi-billion-dollar business using private placement financing, usually reserved to fund construction of individual plants or projects. Mr. O’Donnell’s suggestion stretches access to capital markets far beyond logical reasoning and does not equate to the required level of access on reasonable terms. 
The bottom line is that credit rating analysis is not solely a numbers game. Rating agencies weigh quantitative and qualitative factors on a virtually equal basis, so only looking at quantitative factors will not render an appropriate strategy, nor will only focusing on qualitative factors, most especially regulation, provide a valid plan. The two sides must be weighed equally, which I have done in every one of the cases in which I have testified, using the same fairness parameters whether I was testifying for a regulated utility, a public utility commission, or a consumer advocate.
Q.Mr. Gorman CLAIMS that you provide “no substance supporting or evidence that SUPPORTS [YOUR] CONCLUSION THAT GPC’S credit rating and financial integrity ARE ONLY achieved if the Company’s return on equity and PROPOSED RATEMAKING capital structure are adopted.” Please comment on THIS STATEMENT. 
A.Mr. Gorman is wrong. And if I may, I would like to begin my answer to Mr. Gorman’s claim by offering a serious and critical question: “Where would Georgia Power – and its customers and, indeed, the state of Georgia – be now if, instead of approving the settlement and setting authorized ROE at 10.5% with a 56% equity layer, the regulators in 2019 had adopted Mr. Gorman’s recommendation of authorized ROE of 9.25% with equity at 51%?” Purely on a financial analytical basis, I expect the Company would have probably been just slightly “north” of the investment-grade / non-investment grade dividing line at BBB- / Baa3. But when the negative qualitative effects were layered in, junk bond status would likely not have been out of the question. With Vogtle so close to completion now (assisted by that supportive 2019 rate case settlement and Commission approval), I cannot imagine that the small increment of rate relief flowing from Mr. Gorman’s position back then would have balanced out the negative effects of Georgia Power’s impaired capital markets access, higher interest costs for multiple years to come, and a degree of doubt whether Vogtle would have been able to progress to the point of knocking on the door of startup. Those are the facts upon which I stand now, as the Commission is faced with almost the very same question – whether to accept Mr. O’Donnell’s financial proposals for short-term rate gain, or solidify the Company’s current ratings, with positive movement likely with the successful startup of Vogtle. 
IV.CONCLUSION
Q.MR. FETTER, Do you have ANY concluding thoughts? 
A.Yes, I do. I have testified before the Georgia Public Service Commission for almost two decades. As a former Commission Chairman and head of a major utility ratings practice, I have respected this Commission’s commitment to adjudicating cases with a long-term vision, one that incorporates both quantitative and qualitative factors. Accordingly, for the reasons stated above, my recommendation remains the same as in my direct testimony. The Commission should maintain its longstanding commitment to constructive regulation, which has provided the Company a framework within which to attract capital and provide quality service to customers, while also taking the steps necessary to move forward when unexpected impediments have arisen. 
Q.Does that conclude your rebuttal testimony? 
A.Yes.


===== 2022 Rate Case Rebuttal Testimony Abramovitz-Adams-Houston-Robinson FINAL.docx =====
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF
AARON P. ABRAMOVITZ, SARAH P. ADAMS, ADAM D. HOUSTON, 
AND MICHAEL B. ROBINSON
ON BEHALF OF GEORGIA POWER COMPANY
GPSC DOCKET NO. 44280

I. INTRODUCTION

Q.PLEASE STATE YOUR NAMES, TITLES AND BUSINESS ADDRESS.
A.Aaron P. Abramovitz. I am the Executive Vice President, Chief Financial Officer, and Treasurer for Georgia Power. My business address is 241 Ralph McGill Boulevard N.E., Atlanta, Georgia 30308.
A.Sarah P. Adams. I am the Vice President and Comptroller for Georgia Power. My business address is 241 Ralph McGill Boulevard N.E., Atlanta, Georgia 30308.
A.Adam D. Houston. I am the Assistant Comptroller for Georgia Power. My business address is 241 Ralph McGill Boulevard N.E., Atlanta, Georgia 30308.
A.Michael B. Robinson. I am the Vice President of Planning, Operations and Policy for Georgia Power. My business address is 241 Ralph McGill Boulevard N.E., Atlanta, Georgia 30308.
Q.MR. ABRAMOVITZ, MS. ADAMS, MR. HOUSTON, AND MR. ROBINSON, DID YOU PRESENT DIRECT TESTIMONY AND EXHIBITS ON BEHALF OF GEORGIA POWER IN THIS PROCEEDING?
A.Yes.
WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF YOUR REBUTTAL TESTIMONY?
A.The purpose of our testimony is to respond to the recommendations of the Georgia Public Service Commission’s (“Commission”) Public Interest Advocacy (“PIA”) Staff and various Intervenors. To the extent this rebuttal testimony does not address a particular PIA Staff or Intervenor recommendation, that should not be construed as support for such position.
Q.PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR TESTIMONY.
A.Our testimony requests that the Commission continue the constructive regulatory environment, effectuated through an Alternate Rate Plan (“ARP”), that has benefitted Georgia Power’s customers for decades. This Commission’s constructive regulatory policies have created a sustained period of high customer satisfaction, high reliability, and rates below the national average, and have contributed to Georgia being consistently ranked as the best state in the nation in which to do business. This Commission has a well-deserved national reputation for maintaining a consistent and balanced regulatory environment that benefits all stakeholders. The Commission’s regulatory structure has created an environment in which: (i) the Company can provide the clean, safe, and reliable service that our customers depend on to meet their evolving energy needs; (ii) the Company can access capital on favorable terms to meet the current and future needs of our customers; and (iii) the Company’s investors have an opportunity to receive the returns appropriate for a well-run and efficient utility that meets the needs of its customers.
Absent an ARP agreed to by the Company, this case will result in a traditional rate case order, which would eliminate the numerous positive attributes of a three-year predictable rate plan, including any sharing of revenues above the top end of the ARP earnings band. This fact notwithstanding, PIA Staff and several Intervenors have proposed radical and harmful modifications to the ARP. PIA Staff goes so far as to suggest that the Commission impose an earnings cap, which is fundamentally inconsistent with the concept of the ARP sharing mechanism that benefits both customers and the Company. These proposed modifications undercut core aspects of the ARP and, as such, the Commission should reject them. 
The positions put forth by PIA Staff and several Intervenors would undermine Georgia’s well-established and balanced regulatory structure by drastically reducing the Company’s return on equity (“ROE”). PIA Staff’s recommended 105 basis point ROE reduction would represent the largest single ROE reduction for Georgia Power in decades and would be particularly egregious in light of the Company’s superior performance over the last three years, including during the COVID-19 pandemic. Many of these same parties also propose to reduce the equity within the Company’s capital structure, including PIA Staff’s recommended 500 basis point reduction from the level deemed appropriate by this Commission just three years ago. Such unprecedented and severe changes would significantly impair the Company’s financial integrity, which is necessary to ensure its ability to raise capital at reasonable cost and upon reasonable terms for the benefit of customers.
PIA Staff has also asserted that the Company can arbitrarily reduce its Operations and Maintenance (“O&M”) expense, as well as substantially reduce or eliminate transmission and distribution investments required to support the long-term reliability and resiliency of our electric system, without considering the long-term impacts to the performance of the electric system for our customers and the state. The Commission must consider the cost of electric service when evaluating Georgia Power’s cost to serve our 2.7 million customers, and we have thoughtfully and appropriately balanced our request in this proceeding with both the financial impacts to, and needs of, our customers.
The changes that PIA Staff and many of the intervening parties propose are ill-advised and fail to consider the impact of their proposals on the Company’s ability to access capital markets on reasonable terms and to attract the substantial capital necessary to continue providing our customers with the clean, safe and reliable electric service they deserve and expect. Therefore, the Commission should approve the rate adjustment and capital structure Georgia Power proposes in this case so that the Company remains positioned to continue meeting the energy needs of our customers and the communities we serve.
Q.HOW IS THE REMAINDER OF YOUR TESTIMONY STRUCTURED?
A.Our testimony is organized as follows:
Section II (pages 5 to 14) discusses financial integrity and access to capital;
Section III (pages 15 to 19) discusses proposed modifications to the traditional alternate rate plan; 
Section IV (pages 19 to 21) discusses coal combustion residuals asset retirement obligations (“CCR AROs”); 
Section V (pages 21 to 23) discusses electric transportation;
Section VI (pages 23 to 36) discusses O&M adjustments; 
Section VII (pages 36 to 39) discusses other issues, including the revenue forecast, compliance filing requirements, and depreciation;
Section VIII (pages 39 to 59) discusses the Grid Investment Plan, DERMS, and Rules and Regulations Section G; and 
Section IX (pages 59-61) discusses recent tax legislation. 
In addition to our rebuttal testimony, the Company is filing the rebuttal testimony of: (1) Mr. James M. Coyne to address the ROE recommendations and analyses of PIA Staff Witness Gorman and Intervenor Witnesses Pollock, Reno, and Ellis; (2) Mr. Steven Fetter to explain the credit rating impacts of PIA Staff and Intervenor recommendations; and (3) Mr. Larry T. Legg and Mr. Lee Evans to rebut rate design and cost of service issues raised by PIA Staff and Intervenors. 
II.FINANCIAL INTEGRITY AND ACCESS TO CAPITAL
Q.WOULD ADOPTION OF PIA STAFF AND INTERVENOR’S COST OF CAPITAL POSITIONS UNDO THIS COMMISSION’S HISTORY OF BALANCED, CONSISTENT AND PREDICTABLE REGULATION AND HARM THE COMPANY’S FINANCIAL INTEGRITY?
A.Yes.
Q.PLEASE EXPLAIN THE IMPORTANCE OF CONSISTENT AND PREDICTABLE REGULATION WITHIN THE CONTEXT OF INVESTOR AND CREDIT PERCEPTIONS AND THE FINANCIAL INTEGRITY OF THE COMPANY.
A.As Mr. Fetter discusses, regulation is a key factor in assessing the future credit risk profile of a utility because, among other things, a state public utility commission determines the nature, amount, and timing of recovery of a utility’s cost of service to its customers. Accordingly, the constructive practices and policies of regulatory commissions play a critical role in the overall credit risk profiles of regulated utilities and, ultimately, investors’ decisions. As such, it is imperative that state utility commissions understand the financial, economic, and operational elements of an evolving industry, and engage in a fair decision-making process that demonstrates predictability and consistency. As Mr. Fetter has testified, these qualitative factors can represent as much as half of the Company’s overall credit rating.
For these reasons, rating agencies look for consistent application of sound economic regulatory principles by utility regulators. If a regulatory body allowed a utility the opportunity to earn a fair return on utility assets and subsequently applied cost recovery principles in a manner inconsistent with the regulatory compact, investors’ interest in providing funds to that utility would be altered, the utility’s risk profile would deteriorate, and the utility’s access to capital at reasonable terms would suffer – all to the detriment of customers. 
Q.HOW IS THE COMMISSION CURRENTLY VIEWED BY THE FINANCIAL COMMUNITY? 
A.S&P Global Market Intelligence (formerly Regulatory Research Associates or “RRA”), a respected commentator on U.S. regulatory policy, rates the Georgia regulatory environment (which goes beyond the Commission to also include state legislative and executive branch policies) as Above Average 2. This places Georgia among the top five of the 53 regulatory jurisdictions that S&P Global Market Intelligence currently evaluates. The beneficial aspect, for both Georgia Power investors and customers, of this ranking is its positive reflection in the credit rating process, which provides the rating agencies with a degree of confidence that the outcome in this rate case will be supportive of the Company’s financial integrity. As the rating agencies have stated: 
S&P: In a November 18, 2021 report, S&P stated that Georgia Power’s multiyear rate plan effective since the 2019 rate case has been “supportive of GPC’s credit quality.”
Moody’s: In an October 26, 2022 report, Moody’s stated, “The stable outlook also considers our expectation that the overall regulatory framework in Georgia will remain credit supportive including a constructive outcome on the utility’s pending rate case this December;” 
Fitch: As stated in an October 4, 2022 report, “Fitch expects a constructive resolution to Georgia Power’s pending rate case filing, which proposes a three-year rate plan with rate increases of $852 million, $107 million, and $45 million effective Jan. 1 of 2023, 2024, and 2025, respectively.”
Q.HOW DO CUSTOMERS BENEFIT WHEN THE COMPANY BALANCES THE INTERESTS OF ALL STAKEHOLDERS?
A.Customers benefit when the Company balances the interests of all stakeholders because a financially healthy utility can meet the needs of its customers and is more likely to achieve high levels of reliability and customer satisfaction. Customers also benefit from the Company’s operating efficiencies in its efforts to meet investor expectations. The efficiencies gained and the corresponding cost reductions that result from those efforts are provided to customers through savings in rates. 
Q.WHAT ELEMENTS OF PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATIONS WOULD THREATEN THE COMPANY’S FINANCIAL INTEGRITY?
A.Virtually all of PIA Staff’s recommendations would severely harm the Company’s financial integrity. Taken individually, Mr. Gorman’s recommended 9.45% ROE set point and capital structure of 51% equity, beginning in year 2024, would critically threaten the Company’s financial health. The cumulative negative impact of Mr. Gorman’s recommendations coupled with those of Messrs. Smith and Trokey would be even more dramatic.
Q.IS THERE A WAY TO EVALUATE THE IMPACTS ON FUNDS FROM OPERATIONS (“FFO”) TO DEBT AS A RESULT OF PIA STAFF’S AGGREGATE RECOMMENDATIONS?
A.Yes, if one were to recalculate Georgia Power’s FFO to debt assuming Mr. Gorman’s recommended ROE of 9.45% and an equity ratio of 51% in 2024-2025, the results are extremely negative to the financial integrity of Georgia Power. Further, incorporating the detrimental effects of Messrs. Smith and Trokey’s recommendations, the 2023-2025 average FFO to debt would deteriorate by roughly 500 basis points. The resulting Georgia Power credit profile, assuming the collective recommendations by PIA Staff, would place the Company’s financial integrity at or below the lowest acceptable level recommended by Mr. Fetter. PIA Staff’s unreasonable recommendations would harm the Company’s credit profile, disturb the constructive regulatory environment that has benefitted Georgia Power’s customers, and harm customers by restricting the Company’s access to debt capital at reasonable terms and costs. 
Q.WHAT ARE THE CREDIT RATING AGENCIES’ CURRENT EXPECTATIONS REGARDING THE COMPANY’S FINANCIAL INTEGRITY?
A.Based on the latest Moody’s Credit Opinion issued October 26, 2022, Moody’s states:
Georgia Power operates within a credit supportive Georgia regulatory framework which generally provides timely recovery of prudent costs and investments and allows for the company to maintain a typically stable financial profile.
Moody’s goes on to state:

Georgia Power is currently in a pending rate case and will look to address the deferred fuel costs in a filing next February. Depending on resolution of these regulatory filings, we expect Georgia Power's financial profile to improve including a ratio of CFO pre-W/C to debt of about 20% in 2023. Once the new nuclear units are in service and depending on the ultimate investment recovery allowed by regulators as result of the cost prudency review, the majority of which will be completed when Unit 4 is in service, we expect a substantial increase in Georgia Power’s cash flow which should translate into considerable improvement in credit metrics.
The punitive adjustments that Mr. Gorman and Messrs. Smith and Trokey recommend would limit the FFO to debt metric to approximately 20% for the foreseeable future. However, the rating agencies discuss in their publications the expectation that, beginning in 2024, Georgia Power will remedy its credit profile such that it assumes a position of bolstered strength and adequate credit quality. 
Q.WOULD PIA STAFF’S PROPOSED ADJUSTMENTS IMPAIR THE COMPANY’S ABILITY TO IMPROVE ITS CREDIT METRICS OVER TIME?
A.Yes.
Q.WILLMR. GORMAN’S PROPOSED ADJUSTMENTS PRODUCE THE NECESSARY CASH FLOW TO YIELD AN APPROPRIATE CREDIT RATING GOING FORWARD?
A.No. The Company has identified an omission in Mr. Gorman’s calculation of expected FFO to debt. Mr. Gorman does not include the correct debt imputations for purposes of the FFO to debt ratio that S&P prescribes in its most recent guidance related to the application of S&P Global Ratings’ “Corporate Methodology: Ratios And Adjustments” criteria published April 1, 2019. By excluding more than $5 billion of debt imputations, Mr. Gorman’s credit metric expectation is significantly overstated and fails to illustrate the harmful effects of his recommended ROE and equity ratio. 
Q.WHAT WAS THE OMISSION IN MR. GORMAN’S FFO TO DEBT CREDIT METRIC CALCULATION?
A.In his recalculation of one of S&P’s most important credit metrics, FFO to debt, Mr. Gorman omitted the important debt imputation methodology S&P has directed in previously issued authoritative publications. These debt imputations are essential adjustments that must be made to arrive at a properly recalculated S&P credit metric. 
Q.WHAT ARE DEBT IMPUTATIONS AND WHY DO RATING AGENCIES REQUIRE THEM FOR PURPOSES OF CALCULATING THE CREDIT METRIC “FFO TO DEBT”?
A.Debt imputation is the act of adding debt-like fixed obligations to a utility’s “traditional” debt balance when calculating FFO to debt. For many years, S&P has viewed certain components on the balance sheet, including pension obligations, lease liabilities, and asset retirement obligations as similar to debt and therefore adds them to the debt balance when calculating credit metrics.
Q.HOW DOES S&P OBTAIN THE INFORMATION TO PERFORM SUCH CALCULATIONS?
A.S&P uses the information included on the Company’s publicly available balance sheet and accompanying financial disclosures to obtain pertinent information for the proper debt imputation calculation. Georgia Power has no off-balance sheet debt, or any other off-balance sheet components, that the rating agencies use when calculating adjusted debt. 
Q.DID MR. GORMAN INCLUDE THIS DEBT IMPUTATION IN HIS FFO TO DEBT CREDIT METRIC CALCULATION?
A.No. In his recalculation of S&P’s credit metric, FFO to debt, Mr. Gorman omitted the debt imputation methodology directed by S&P in previously issued authoritative publications. These debt imputations are essential adjustments that must be made to recalculate S&P’s credit metric. 
In response to the question posed by PIA Staff and Mr. Gorman in data request STF-BAI-1-17, the Company responded as follows:
Question:
Please state whether GPC’s regulated retail operations have any offbalance sheet debt such as purchased power agreements and operating leases. If the answer is “yes,” provide the amount of each off-balance sheet debt item and estimate the related imputed interest and amortization expense associated with these off-balance sheet debt equivalents specific to GPC’s jurisdictional regulated retail electric operations. 
Response:
No. Georgia Power does not have any off-balance sheet debt. In 2019, Georgia Power adopted the provisions of Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB) Accounting Standards Codification (“ASC”) Topic 842 (as amended), Leases (ASC 842), which requires lessees to recognize leases with a term of greater than 12 months on the balance sheet as lease obligations. With the adoption of the standard, the operating leases historically representing off-balance sheet financing arrangements are now reflected on Georgia Power’s balance sheet.
In responding to this  question, the Company correctly answered that it does not have any off-balance sheet debt. The Company also clarified that obligations such as purchased power agreements and operating leases are now found on the Company’s balance sheet. Mr. Gorman and the Company agree that these debt-like obligations are the type of debt imputations that are appropriate to include in estimating S&P’s FFO to debt calculation. However, due to the confusion around where these debt-like components are identified, these components were omitted from Mr. Gorman’s calculations. Mr. Gorman should have included the debt imputations related to these on-balance sheet debt-like components in his estimated recalculation of S&P credit metrics, as he did in the 2019 rate case. 
Q.WHAT AMOUNT OF DEBT IMPUTATION SHOULD HAVE BEEN INCLUDED IN MR. GORMAN’S CALCULATIONS?
A.Based upon the relevant balance sheet accounts, similar to those assessed in S&P’s November 18, 2021, Ratings Direct report on Georgia Power, over $5.5 billion of debt imputations should have been adjusted for purposes of Mr. Gorman’s estimated recalculation of S&P credit metrics. Had Mr. Gorman’s recalculation included the proper S&P debt imputations, it would have shown that the Company’s metrics, assuming Staff’s adjustments were accepted by the Commission, are insufficient to maintain sufficient access to capital at reasonable rates.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH MR. GORMAN’S CONCLUSION THAT ITS EQUITY RATIO SHOULD BE REDUCED TO 51% BEGINNING IN 2024?
A.No. Although Mr. Gorman acknowledges that the Company’s authorized equity ratio was previously increased due to the negative effects of the 2017 Tax Cuts and Jobs Act (“TCJA”), he completely ignores that these negative effects continue today. Instead, he proposes that the Company’s equity ratio should be reduced to 51% once Vogtle Unit 4 is placed in service, wrongly assuming that the construction of Vogtle Units 3 and 4 , and not the TCJA, was the catalyst to increase the Company’s equity ratio to 56% and should, therefore, be the impetus to reduce it back to 51%.
Q.HOW WAS GEORGIA POWER’S CURRENT 56% EQUITY RATIO DETERMINED IN THE COMPANY’S PRIOR 2019 RATE CASE?
A.The Settlement Agreement approved in the 2019 Rate Case left it to the Commission to determine the Company’s appropriate capital structure. Based on the evidence presented in that case, the Commission found the appropriate capital structure to be 56% equity and 44% debt. As noted in the Commission’s Short Order Adopting Settlement as Modified in the Company’s 2019 Rate Case in Docket No. 42516, the Company’s equity ratio was adjusted from 51% to 55% as a part of the TCJA Settlement in Docket No. 36989 (“Tax Reform Settlement”) to address the negative implications of tax reform and provide support for maintaining the Company’s credit profile. The Commission further determined that the “proposed capital structure of 56% common equity level is just and reasonable considering all of the evidence presented.” 
Q.HAVE THERE BEEN ANY CHANGES TO THE TCJA OR ITS NEGATIVE IMPACTS ON THE COMPANY SINCE THE COMMISSION DEEMED THE APPROPRIATE CAPITAL STRUCTURE FOR THE COMPANY TO BE 56% EQUITY AND 44% DEBT?
A.No.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY ANTICIPATE ANY CHANGES TO THE TCJA OR ITS NEGATIVE IMPLICATIONS ON THE COMPANY DURING 2023-2025 THAT WOULD JUSTIFY LOWERING ITS EQUITY RATIO?
A.No.
Q.DOES ANY OTHER WITNESS RECOMMEND A SPECIFIC REDUCTION IN THE EQUITY LEVEL IN THE COMPANY’S CAPITAL STRUCTURE?
A.Yes. Georgia Association of Manufacturers (“GAM”) Witness LaConte recommends that the Company’s equity ratio should be more in line with the average equity ratio for the companies included in Witness Coyne’s proxy group that have similar credit ratings as Georgia Power or should not exceed 53%. Ms. LaConte’s proposal of 53% equity is not based on a careful and accurate analysis of the credit rating implications of her recommendation. If a 53% equity level were adopted by the Commission, that decrease would cause over 200 basis points of deterioration in FFO to debt, harming the Company’s financial integrity. Additionally, as mentioned earlier, the 56% equity level set in the 2019 rate case appropriately mitigates the negative credit impacts of the TCJA. The impacts of the TCJA still exist; therefore, the Company must maintain its 56% equity ratio as carefully reviewed and approved by the Commission in its 2019 rate case.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH MR. GORMAN’S RECOMMENDATION TO REDUCE THE COMPANY’S ROE SET POINT 105 BASIS POINTS TO 9.45%, MRS. LACONTE’S RECOMMENDATION TO REDUCE THE COMPANY’S ROE SET POINT 67 BASIS POINTS TO 9.83%, OR MS. RENO’S RECOMMENDATION TO REDUCE THE COMPANY’S ROE SET POINT 103 BASIS POINTS TO 9.47%?
A.No. Mr. Coyne will address the errors in Mr. Gorman’s and Ms. Reno’s calculations in this regard. Notwithstanding these errors, their recommendations do not represent current market conditions and would not result in full recovery of the Company’s cost of equity. Further, there is no justification for such severe reductions in the Company’s cost of equity since the last base rate case. For these reasons, Mr. Gorman and Ms. Reno’s recommendations are not supported and are punitive when considering Georgia Power’s record of strong performance. The ROE reductions proposed by Mr. Gorman and Ms. Reno are a dramatic departure from the approach employed by this Commission over the past several rate cases and would surely be viewed negatively by the capital markets, as Mr. Fetter has stated in his testimony. A reduction of this magnitude would be an unprecedented change from the consistent and historically constructive regulation for which this Commission has a reputation – one where excellence is recognized and rewarded, not punished.
III.MODIFICATIONS TO TRADITIONAL ALTERNATE RATE PLAN
Q.ARE PIA STAFF’S PROPOSED CHANGES TO THE COMPANY’S ARP CONSISTENT WITH THE CONSTRUCTIVE REGULATORY ENVIRONMENT TYPICAL OF PRIOR ARPS APPROVED BY THIS COMMISSION?
A.No. PIA Staff’s collective recommendations radically depart from the previously accepted ARP structure and have the effect of removing the benefits to customers resulting from the Company agreeing to enter an ARP in the first place. Three-year ARPs avoid the potential for more frequent base rate cases, provide rate stability and predictability to customers, provide for customer revenue sharing for earnings above the approved earnings band, encourage the Company to operate efficiently, and signal the type of regulatory consistency viewed favorably by credit rating agencies. 
Q.IN WHAT WAY DOES THE COMPANY ASSUME RISK DURING THE THREE-YEAR ACCOUNTING PERIOD? 
A.Because the Company surrenders its right to file a full rate case during the three-year term when the ROE is within the authorized earnings band, the Company assumes the risks of changing conditions during the term of the ARP. For example, the level of inflation may increase more than projected, the cost of debt or equity might increase, sales may be less than forecasted, or the Company’s overall cost of service may be more than forecasted. A three-year rate plan holds the Company accountable for inefficiencies or cost overruns due to rates being set at a predetermined level for the duration of the three-year period.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATION TO USE THE TEST PERIOD REVENUE REQUIREMENT FOR PURPOSES OF ESTABLISHING THE FIRST YEAR REVENUE REQUIREMENT OF AN ARP?
A. No. The revenue deficiency calculated for the calendar year ending December 31, 2023, rather than the deficiency for the split test year, reflects a more accurate depiction of the costs the Company will incur in its first year under a new ARP when the new rates are effective. Determining the revenue deficiency using the calendar year reduces regulatory lag. In the Company’s Errata Filing, the projected revenue deficiency based on the traditional test period and the 2023 calendar year is $459.8 million and $612.3 million, respectively, resulting in a difference of $152.5 million. This difference illustrates that basing the year one rate increase on the split test period’s revenue deficiency would create a revenue shortfall of $152.5 million in the very first year of the three-year period. Therefore, setting rates based on the test period would not permit the Company to timely recover its prudently incurred costs in serving customers. 
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATION TO DEFER AND USE ANY REVENUE SUFFICIENCY BASED ON PIA STAFF’S CALCULATED TEST YEAR AND 2023 REVENUE REQUIREMENT FOR ACCELERATED RECOGNITION OF DEFERRED COSTS?
A. No. Based on the Company’s proposal, the Company does not have a projected revenue sufficiency in 2023 to apply to the deferred costs. As stated previously, the Company’s revenue deficiency should be based on the calendar year 2023 revenue requirement rather than the test period, if the Company’s ARP is adopted. However, the Company generally agrees with PIA Staff’s concept of applying revenue sufficiency in a rate case, in the event there is any, to certain regulatory assets to maintain rate stability for the customers.
Q.WHY IS A LEVELIZED APPROACH MORE APPROPRIATE THAN THE STEP INCREASE APPROACH RECOMMENDED BY PIA STAFF?
A.The levelized approach results in $43 million less being billed to customers than a step increase approach over the three-year ARP. It also balances the needs of both customers and the Company, allowing customers to benefit from three years of stable and predictable rates while allowing the Company to recover its projected revenue deficiency. Rate stability provides customers with a beneficial tool when budgeting for their utility costs and provides a predictable revenue stream for the Company. 
The step increase approach would increase customer rates by an increasing amount during each of the next three years. Although an annual increase would result in a lower than requested projected revenue requirement for 2023 and 2024, the increase for 2025 would be greater than the levelized amount, and rates would increase more overall during the ARP. In addition, the fluctuation in projected costs year-to-year creates uncertainty for customers. Although the Company shares PIA Staff’s interest in rate stability, the Company maintains that levelizing the proposed increases over the three-year ARP is the more appropriate approach for customers.
Q.ARE CUSTOMERS FAIRLY COMPENSATED FOR THE PROJECTED OVER-COLLECTION IN THE FIRST TWO YEARS OF THE LEVELIZATION?
A.Yes. Based on the Company’s projections under the levelized revenue requirement, the Company would over-collect in 2023 and 2024 and under-collect in 2025. For the projected advancement of revenues in 2023 and 2024, the Company proposes to defer the amount as a regulatory liability, reducing retail rate base and giving customers credit on the advanced amount based on the Company’s full weighted average cost of capital (“WACC”). This proposed deferral reduces the levelized revenue requirement requested by the Company and fully amortizes the advancement by the end of 2025.
Q.DO YOU AGREE WITH PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATION THAT 100% OF EARNINGS ABOVE THE TOP END OF THE EARNINGS BAND SHOULD BE USED TO ACCELERATE DEFERRED COSTS?
A.No. PIA Staff’s proposal amounts to an earnings cap by eliminating any retained revenues earned above the earnings band. This removes any incentive for the Company to exceed the top end of the band and pr does not encourage the Company to maximize efficiencies during the ARP period. In addition, the top end of the earnings band proposed by PIA Staff is set artificially low, at a level below even the Company’s cost of equity. 
Q. HAS THE EARNINGS SHARING MECHANISM WORKED WELL FOR THE COMPANY AND ITS CUSTOMERS?
A.Yes. As presented in the table on page 130 of the testimony of PIA Staff Witnesses Smith and Trokey, and as stated in our direct testimony, the Company returned approximately $297 million in benefits to customers as a result of the sharing arrangements approved by the Commission in the 2013 and 2019 base rate cases. As the table illustrates, this represents an average of approximately 78% of the earnings above the top of the band being applied to the benefit of customers and 22% of the earnings being retained by the Company since 2014. These benefits have been achieved without compromising superior customer satisfaction or reliability. Notably, the amount customers pay through rates does not increase or decrease when the Company’s earnings are within the band, because rates are set on the ROE set point. As previously mentioned, the sharing mechanism is a critical component of the ARP and appropriately encourages the Company to look for efficiencies and process improvements, which can lead to higher earnings that benefit both customers and the Company.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH COMMERCIAL GROUP WITNESS CHRISS’ RECOMMENDATION TO ADJUST THE EARNINGS BAND COMMENSURATE WITH ANY ADJUSTMENT TO THE REQUESTED 11% RETURN ON EQUITY SET POINT?
A.No. If the Commission were to authorize a set point ROE less than the Company’s requested 11%, the bottom end of the earnings band should remain set at 9.5% with the top end of the earnings band set at 12.0%. 
Q. DO YOU AGREE WITH DOD WITNESS BLANK’S PROPOSAL TO NARROW THE ROE EARNINGS BAND AND REDUCE THE AMOUNT OF EARNINGS ABOVE THE BAND RETAINED BY THE COMPANY?
A.No. It appears that Mr. Blank is only recommending an ROE earnings band of 100 basis points above the ROE if the Commission authorizes an ROE below the Company’s requested 11%. This would result in the top of the earnings band being lower than the 12% proposed by the Company. Otherwise, Mr. Blank reiterates his recommendation from the 2019 rate case that the earnings band be narrowed to +/- 50 basis points above and below the target ROE. Narrowing the earnings band may increase risks and uncertainty to customers. Second, Mr. Blank also recommends reducing the Company’s portion of any shared earnings above the top of the band, which is not acceptable because it caps the Company’s earnings incentive. 
IV. CCR AROS
Q.IS THE APPROVAL OF THE COMPANY’S CCR COMPLIANCE PLAN AT ISSUE IN THIS PROCEEDING?
A.No. The Commission-approved 2022 IRP Stipulation in Docket No. 44160 approved the Company’s ECS and plan to comply with federal and state CCR regulations, but reserved CCR cost recovery for determination in this rate case. This includes the method of recovery and the appropriate period over which the Company should recover costs.
Q.DO ANY PARTIES DISAGREE WITH THE COMPANY’S PROPOSAL TO CONTINUE THE CURRENT RECOVERY METHODOLOGY REGARDING CCR ARO COMPLIANCE COSTS APPROVED IN THE COMPANY’S 2019 RATE CASE?
A.All parties generally agree with the current CCR ARO cost recovery methodology except for GAM Witness Pollock, who proposes that the Company should not earn an equity return on the CCR ARO regulatory asset balance. The Company’s CCR ARO compliance costs are required to comply with federal and state environmental regulations and are appropriately included in rate base. As such, the Company should be able to timely recover its prudently incurred costs and have the opportunity to recover all of its financing costs, including equity. Witness Pollock’s recommendation prevents the Company’s ability to recover its actual costs of financing these required investments. In addition, his proposed ratemaking treatment contradicts past treatment of ARO compliance costs and such departure from past precedent is unjustified.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH MR. POLLOCK’S ASSERTION THAT IT SHOULD NOT EARN A RETURN ON EQUITY ON UNRECOVERED CCR COSTS UNTIL SECURITIZATION IS AUTHORIZED?
A.No. Denying the Company the opportunity to recover all of its financing costs on any unrecovered CCR ARO compliance costs amounts to a disallowance and inappropriately and prematurely assumes that such costs were not prudently incurred. As Witness Pollock admitted upon cross examination, securitization is not currently an option in Georgia and would require a change in state law to implement. It would be inappropriate to deny the Company recovery of currently incurred financing costs based solely on the potential for future securitization legislation, over which neither the Company nor the Commission have control, and which might never happen. Securitization is not needed as this Commission already has all the tools necessary to appropriately balance the needs of our customers and the Company.
V.ELECTRIC TRANSPORTATION
Q.WHAT IS GEORGIA POWER’S ROLE IN SUPPORTING THE DEVELOPMENT OF GEORGIA’S ELECTRIC TRANSPORTATION (“ET”) ECOSYSTEM?
A.Georgia Power must be prepared to meet the energy needs of all customers within its service territory. Therefore, we continue to make appropriate investments in the infrastructure and technology needed to support the growth of electric transportation in Georgia that is being driven by customer interest and market demand. These investments include the buildout and upgrade of infrastructure from grid to charger that will enable charging across the state in public, at work, and at home. In limited circumstances, Georgia Power will continue to offer EV charging services as a provider of last resort in underserved areas through the Community Charging Program. As such, Georgia Power will continue to support our state’s developing ET ecosystem, which continues to experience economic growth as evidenced by two major EV manufacturing announcements this year.
Q.WHAT IS THE MAKE READY PROGRAM?
A.Georgia Power’s Make Ready program helps to remove the biggest financial hurdle of installing electric chargers at a qualifying customer’s location by installing, owning, and maintaining the electrical infrastructure behind the meter up to the charger, which typically includes the panel, switchgear, wiring, conduits, and disconnects. The customer is then responsible for the procurement, installation, maintenance, and energy to serve the EV charging equipment. 
Q.WHAT IS GEORGIA POWER’S REQUEST REGARDING THE MAKE READY PROGRAM IN THIS CASE?
A. The Company has requested to invest $27 million per year for each year of the ARP (2023-2025) for infrastructure upgrades in the Make Ready program. As stated in the Company’s response to STF-LA-6-19, these capital expenditures include wires, panels, conduit, transformers, and labor. 
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE GEORGIA POWER’S COMMUNITY CHARGING PROGRAM.
A.Georgia Power’s Community Charging Program expands the growth of electric vehicle direct current (“DC”) fast charging locations by partnering with businesses to host an EV fast charging site where the charging will be made available to the public. Georgia Power installs, operates, and maintains the chargers in this program, which is designed to partner with customers to help grow critical public EV infrastructure across Georgia. The Company primarily operates this program as a provider of last resort to provide public DC fast charging options for EV drivers in areas that lack investment in public DC fast charging. In the 2019 Rate Case, the Commission approved $2 million per year (2020-2022) in spending for the Community Charging program. The Company’s request in this case for $3 million per year for each year of the ARP (2023-2025) is intended to maintain the development and deployment of the Community Charging program. The increase in funding requested reflects inflation and the increased cost of labor and equipment.
Q.WILL CONTINUED INVESTMENT IN ET INFRASTRUCTURE AT THE LEVELS GEORGIA POWER REQUESTS PROVIDE ECONOMIC BENEFITS TO CUSTOMERS?
A.Yes. The Company’s Rate Impact Measure (“RIM”) analysis of the Make Ready program indicates these investments will put downward pressure on rates over the life of the technology, which benefits all customers. The Community Charging program enables greater adoption of these technologies by providing public charging along travel corridors throughout the state, especially in rural and underserved communities that have not received infrastructure investments from the private sector. 
VI.O&M ADJUSTMENTS
Q.PLEASE SUMMARIZE MR. MUGRACE’S RECOMMENDED O&M ADJUSTMENTS.
A.Mr. Mugrace recommends reducing the Company’s projected O&M expenses by $192 million, $147 million, $137 million, and $169 million in the test year and 2023, 2024, and 2025 rate years, respectively. Mr. Mugrace recommends the Company adjust its projected functional O&M expenses for certain FERC accounts based upon prior historical levels of O&M expenses. Mr. Mugrace also recommends removing certain expenses historically allowed by the Commission from the retail cost of service, such as certain employee labor costs, employee retention costs, Innovation Center costs, and miscellaneous general expenses. Furthermore, Mr. Mugrace recommends the Company reduce its labor expenses based on vacancy rate ratio.
Q.WHY IS A SIMPLE AVERAGE OF HISTORICAL COSTS NOT ALWAYS A GOOD DETERMINANT OF PROJECTED COSTS?
A.The Company’s O&M expenses are complex and driven by many factors such as timing and scope of outages, scheduled maintenance cycles, repair work, medical claims, labor market, and contractor rates. These conditions in the historical period are not identical to the test period and the Company and its business units consider all these factors in the development of their respective budgets for the upcoming three-year period. In addition, the historical years used by Mr. Mugrace includes years 2020 and 2021, which were greatly impacted by the COVID pandemic and are not representative of a “normal” year that should be relied upon as reflective of costs to be incurred in the forecasted periods.
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE COMPANY’S O&M BUDGET PROCESS?
A.Annually, the Company utilizes input from all areas of the business to plan and budget the resources needed in support of expected workload for the upcoming five-year forecasted period. The Company considers planned outage schedules, expected maintenance activities, asset and vegetation management programs, customer service needs, technology, fleet, materials, staffing levels, contractor rates, and expected inflation when developing the budget. Budget assumptions are vetted and approved to ensure consistency across business units. 
During this process, Georgia Power reviews expenses and adjusts budgeted amounts based on known increases or decreases, accordingly. The Company does not create its budget by simply applying an inflation factor to all the actual, or historic year expenses. Based on the type of expenditure, inflation rates may be applied to derive projected expenses. 
Q.DOES THE COMPANY’S O&M REMAIN STATIC OVER TIME?
A.No. The Company has seen significant price increases in nearly all areas of its business. For example, transformer prices have increased over 48% since 2020, wire and cable costs increased 33%, pole line hardware is up 27%, and pole costs have increased 26%. Despite cost increases, budgets may fluctuate over a given period of time due to multiple factors, including unit outages, changes in workforce needs for both Company labor and contract labor, Company benefit costs, and Commission-approved items such as Storm Recovery and demand side management (“DSM”). These are only a few of the items that make up the Company’s O&M request. The Company is constantly focused on managing its O&M expenditures, emphasizing cost containment as part of its corporate culture, as reasonable and necessary cost containment is in the best interest of both the Company and its customers. The Company has identified efficiencies in nearly every part of its business while still providing superior customer satisfaction and reliable service to customers.
Q.AS AN EXAMPLE OF WHY SIMPLY ESCALATING HISTORIC COSTS IS INAPPROPRIATE, WHAT IS DRIVING THE INCREASE IN O&M EXPENSES IN CUSTOMER SERVICE AND INFORMATION FOR THE TEST PERIOD AND YEARS 2023 THROUGH 2025?
A.The primary driver for the increase in Customer Service and Information expenses is related to the increase in DSM expenses as a result of the DSM programs approved by the Commission in the 2022 DSM Certification in Docket No. 44161. Because the projected level of DSM expenses are directly attributed to the DSM programs approved by the Commission, historical costs are clearly not a good indicator of the projected expenses for the ARP years reflected in the filing.
Q.ARE WITNESS MUGRACE’S RECOMMENDATIONS REGARDING DSM PROGRAM EXPENSES APPROPRIATE? 
A.No. In fact, PIA Staff’s panel of Barber, Deitchman, Watkins does not recommend any adjustments to the DSM program expenses.
Q.WHY IS IT INAPPROPRIATE TO REMOVE THE COSTS ASSOCIATED WITH THE INNOVATION CENTER FROM RETAIL COST OF SERVICE AS PROPOSED BY PIA STAFF?
A.The Company’s Energy Innovation Center (“EIC”) is related to but distinct from Research and Development in that the EIC is focused on education and exchanging ideas and information with all customer segments and stakeholders. The EIC is a platform where Georgia Power can share with customers general educational materials on innovative technologies, discuss end-use applications that will be beneficial for customers, bring stakeholders together, and establish a foundation for future partnerships between the utility and customers. Establishing a forum to circulate these ideas around technology innovation, and their market milestones, is fundamentally beneficial to Georgia Power’s customers because it has led to project activities that deliver cost saving efficiency and sustainability outcomes, with the potential to deliver grid-level benefits that improve reliability. The EIC is a resource for customers and stakeholders to learn about new energy technologies and how they may participate in their deployment, where applicable and appropriate. 
Q.HAVE THE COSTS ASSOCIATED WITH THE ENERGY INNOVATION CENTER PREVIOUSLY BEEN APPROVED BY THE COMMISSION IN RETAIL COST OF SERVICE?
A.Yes.
Q.WHY IS IT INAPPROPRIATE TO REMOVE THE SERVICE AWARDS AND RECREATION COSTS FROM RETAIL COST OF SERVICE?
A.The service award program is a part of the overall compensation package offered to all full-time and part-time active employees, which helps motivate and retain the talent needed to efficiently operate the Company’s business. It demonstrates recognition and appreciation to employees for company service at specific service intervals. As stated in STF-PCMG-2-12, the costs listed as recreation costs are actually meals and travel expenses that were inadvertently budgeted to FERC account 926 instead of FERC account 921. These are not a “new” set of costs as the Company incurred similar expenses in prior periods, which were correctly recorded to FERC account 921.
Q.HAVE THE SERVICE AWARDS AND MEALS AND TRAVEL EXPENSES PREVIOUSLY BEEN APPROVED BY THE COMMISSION IN RETAIL COST OF SERVICE?
A.Yes.
Q.WHY IS IT INAPPROPRIATE TO REMOVE MISCELLANEOUS GENERAL EXPENSES INCLUDING ASSOCIATION DUES, COMMUNITY SERVICE, AND EXPERIMENTAL COSTS FROM RETAIL COST OF SERVICE?
A.Contrary to the assertion of PIA Staff Witness Mugrace, these items are necessary costs incurred in operating the business and our customers benefit from these activities. Through these efforts, the Company develops technology efficiencies that reduce O&M expenses and equipment improvements that improve the reliability of the grid. As a member of certain associations, the Company and its employees learn, grow, and contribute to the sustainability of the electric industry. Costs associated with community organizations, community development, economic development and other business issues benefit the communities we serve. Investments in these areas help the Company engage with customers and communities, which allows for growth and sustainability across the state. 
Q.HAVE MISCELLANEOUS GENERAL EXPENSES INCLUDING ASSOCIATION DUES, COMMUNITY SERVICE, AND EXPERIMENTAL COSTS PREVIOUSLY BEEN APPROVED BY THE COMMISSION IN RETAIL COST OF SERVICE?
A.Yes.
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE HOW THE COMPANY CALCULATED THE INCREMENTAL INFLATION ASSOCIATED WITH ITS O&M EXPENSES?
A.The Company began to see significant price increases across all aspects of its business consistent with the rising inflation factors found in the Employment Cost Index (“ECI”) and Gross Domestic Product (“GDP”) deflator. In response to these signals, the Company adjusted its projected O&M expenses to account for these cost increases in this filing by identifying labor and non-labor costs that typically would fluctuate based on inflation factors and applying the incremental increase in ECI and GDP inflation rates to the appropriate cost types. The adjustments were recorded at a summary account level across several FERC accounts as provided in STF-PCMG-1-5. Items such as benefits, incentive pay, insurance, and parity were excluded from the adjustment.
Q.WHY IS STAFF’S COMPARISON AND THE RESULTING RECOMMENDED ADJUSTMENT OF INCREMENTAL INFLATION INAPPROPRIATE?
A.Although Mr. Mugrace states that the ECI is an appropriate source of inflation rates for labor costs, he ultimately used only the lower GDP index to escalate total non-Production O&M expenses, including labor and non-labor costs, to calculate his incremental inflation adjustment. In order to appropriately calculate the adjustment, the O&M expenses need to be separated into labor and non-labor charges and the ECI and GDP rates need to be applied to the respective costs, as the Company did in performing its calculation of the incremental inflation adjustment. In addition, Mr. Mugrace did not carry forward the incremental inflation adjustment calculated in Year 1 to the subsequent years, which caused the impact in future years to be understated. Since the escalation indexes are increases from the prior year, any incremental inflation adjustments from the prior year need to be accounted for in the calculation of incremental inflation in a given year. 
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE COMPANY’S BUDGETED HEADCOUNT IN THE FORECASTED PERIODS?
A.The Company budgeted its headcount and total labor costs, including internal and contract labor as well as overtime, at the level needed to operate its business and, therefore, there is no headcount vacancy reflected in the Company’s forecast.
Q.IF THE COMPANY IS UNABLE TO HIRE THE FULL NUMBER OF EMPLOYEES BUDGETED TO PERFORM ITS OPERATIONS, HOW DOES THE COMPANY CONTINUE TO MANAGE ITS BUSINESS?
A.The Company fills open positions with overtime labor from the existing workforce or hires additional contract labor to perform the necessary work if it is unable to hire the full number of employees budgeted. In this example, while internal labor costs decrease, overtime and contract labor increase to offset the reduction.
Q.WHAT INCORRECT LABOR BUDGET ASSUMPTIONS OR ERRORS WERE INCLUDED IN MR. MUGRACE’S TESTIMONY?
A.Mr. Mugrace makes several errors based on flawed assumptions about how and where labor dollars are budgeted. The labor dollars referenced by Mr. Mugrace throughout his testimony only capture contract labor dollars and not internal labor dollars. There are internal labor dollars included within the functional O&M expenses from which Mr. Mugrace attempted to remove all labor dollars to determine non-labor expenses. The results of Mr. Mugrace’s adjustments are significantly flawed because he failed to remove the internal labor dollars in his analysis. Additionally, the costs associated with the Performance Pay Program (“PPP”) and payroll taxes are removed from contract labor although the Company provides neither PPP nor payroll taxes for contract labor. In some instances, there also appear to be inconsistencies where Mr. Mugrace only applied adjustments to the test year, while for other years, the adjustments were only applied to years 2023 through 2025. For example, Mr. Mugrace recommends reducing the Company’s proposed incremental inflation expenses for years 2023 through 2025 but does not recommend a corresponding adjustment to the test year, which shows an increase in O&M expenses resulting from his analysis.
Q.WHY IS IT INAPPROPRIATE TO REMOVE CERTAIN EMPLOYEE LABOR COSTS FROM RETAIL COST OF SERVICE?
A.To provide its award-winning service to customers, Georgia Power must attract and retain a high level of skilled and professional employees, who are paid competitively and appropriately based on their role. The Company's costs related to Supplemental Executive Retirement Plan (“SERP”) and non-SERP benefit costs as well as employee retention are necessary expenses to ensure the attraction and retention of our highly skilled workforce. 
The SERP and non-SERP programs are used to attract and retain senior management level employees across the various business units of the Company who have key roles, experiences, and skills in managing employees to ensure Georgia Power continues to provide reliable customer service and maintain our standards of safety. These types of programs are common for other companies who provide qualified pension plan benefits and are regularly evaluated to ensure that the total compensation for our employees including these pension benefit programs is market competitive. Furthermore, these non-qualified pension programs are not funded and therefore, are not covered by corporate-owned life insurance benefits as stated by Mr. Mugrace in his testimony.
Employee retention agreements are utilized as targeted, strategic compensation tools to retain high-performing employees in critical roles throughout the Company who offer deep experience, knowledge, and expertise. These agreements are entered into in direct response to specifically identified market pressures. Such pressures often include recruitment by Company competitors and peers, within and outside of the Company’s industry, as well as within and outside of the Company’s geographic footprint. Employee retention costs allow the Company to continue providing excellent service for our customer base and further maintain the resiliency of our infrastructure. Further, these costs offset the potential future expenses that would be incurred to recruit, develop and train substitute employees for these key roles.
Q.HAVE THESE EMPLOYEE LABOR COSTS PREVIOUSLY BEEN APPROVED BY THE COMMISSION IN RETAIL COST OF SERVICE?
A.Yes.
Q.WHAT IS THE GOAL OF GEORGIA POWER’S EMPLOYEE COMPENSATION PROGRAM?
A.To provide its award-winning service to customers, Georgia Power must attract and retain highly skilled employees. Therefore, Georgia Power’s employee compensation program is designed to provide competitive compensation at levels targeting the 50th percentile of the appropriate markets. The Company’s well-balanced and market competitive compensation program provides different types of compensation that are commonly known and recognized by compensation professionals to be market appropriate to retain and attract talent, which includes (i) fixed based compensation, (ii) variable short-term compensation, and (iii) long-term stock-based compensation. The goal of providing different types of compensation is to create a balanced incentive system to ensure that employees are providing the best service to customers at the least cost. Evaluating compensation as a whole allows Georgia Power to maximize the effective use of resources essential to efficiently serve its customers by linking a portion of the employees’ compensation package to operational and financial performance, thus aligning the compensation programs with Georgia Power’s need to attract, engage, retain, and motivate its highly skilled workforce.
Q.WHAT IS PIA STAFF’S CONCERN WITH THE COMPANY’S PROPOSED EMPLOYEE COMPENSATION PROGRAM?
A.According to the testimony of Messrs. Smith, Trokey, and Mugrace, it appears that PIA Staff believes certain portions of the Company’s total compensation program – the long-term incentive stock-based compensation plan and the amounts of the PPP attributed to financial goals – do not benefit customers and should therefore be borne by shareholders instead of customers.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH THE DISALLOWANCES FOR THE EMPLOYEE COMPENSATION PROGRAM PROPOSED BY PIA STAFF?
A. No. The Company’s existing compensation policy is necessary to attract, motivate, and retain a highly skilled, dedicated workforce, and to provide customers with clean, safe, and reliable service while efficiently managing costs. If “at-risk” components of the employee compensation program which includes the long-term incentive stock-based compensation plan and the amounts of the PPP attributed to financial goals was eliminated or significantly reduced, the Company would have to commensurately increase other components of its total compensation package – such as direct salary costs – to maintain its competitiveness as an employer within the industry and to continue to attract a skilled workforce. It would result in higher fixed costs and poor alignment of all stakeholders’ interests, including customers.
PIA Staff’s proposed disallowance of these portions of the employee compensation program are not based upon any supporting data or market analysis that the Company’s expenses are excessive or that the programs are not competitive with the external market. There is also no basis for Mr. Mugrace’s assertion that, “[t]hese types of incentive costs benefit the Shareholders of the Company, and of SCS and SNC, and do not directly benefit the ratepayers in the area of safe and reliable utility service.”
The budgeted costs to attract, retain, and engage employees are expenses that arise in the ordinary course of the Company’s business and should be included in the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail rates.
Further, the payroll tax expenses associated with stock-based compensation are also a prudently incurred cost of doing business and, therefore, should be recovered from customers. However, Messrs. Smith and Trokey applied both the Federal Insurance Contributions Act (“FICA”) and Medicare portions of payroll tax to the stock-based compensation adjustment when calculating their payroll tax expense adjustment to be excluded from retail cost of service. As they noted on schedule E-5 in PIA Staff’s Exhibit_(RS-RT-2), it is likely that employees who are eligible for the Company’s stock-based compensation programs would have annual salaries above the projected FICA threshold. Therefore, it is appropriate to apply only the Medicare portion of payroll tax to the stock-based compensation expense when calculating the applicable payroll tax expense adjustment.
Q.WHY DOES THE COMPANY PROVIDE LONG-TERM INCENTIVE BASED COMPENSATION? 
A.The long-term incentive based at-risk compensation program incentivizes employees to make decisions that are in the long-term best interest of customers and the Company. Customers benefit from a financially healthy company, which should be measured over the short- and long-term to ensure that the decisions made by the Company are optimized for both short- and long-term benefits. This is especially true in the utility industry, where decisions related to infrastructure and other major capital projects may have long-lasting financial consequences to all stakeholders including customers. Customers would be harmed if the Company were to drive its employees to sacrifice long-term financial health for short-term benefits. By designing the at-risk portion of the total compensation plan to include longer-term goals, the Company achieves an appropriate incentive balance that motivates employees to deliver safe, clean, and reliable electric service to our customers in a manner that is economically efficient both now and in the years that follow. 
WHY IS IT IMPORTANT THAT AN INCENTIVE COMPENSATION PLAN INCLUDE BOTH OPERATIONAL AND FINANCIAL GOALS?
Georgia Power seeks to create a balanced incentive system to ensure that employees are providing the best service to customers at the least cost. Evaluating compensation as a whole allows Georgia Power to maximize the effective use of resources essential to efficiently serving its customers by linking a portion of the employees’ compensation package to operational and financial performance and allows for the alignment of the compensation programs with Georgia Power’s need to attract, engage, retain, and motivate its highly skilled workforce. A well-designed total compensation program using sound compensation practices and principles provides a balance between operational focus and financial focus for both the short term and longer term to drive employee behavior in ways that balance the interests of customers and shareholders alike. A compensation plan that contains only operational goals might inappropriately drive employees to use more financial resources than necessary to achieve operational success, while a plan that contains only financial goals might inappropriately drive employees to make decisions that adversely impact operational success. A balanced program of incentive compensation is a powerful tool that drives employees to put the customer at the center of all we do while sustaining the financial integrity of the Company.
Q.HOW DO THE ELEMENTS OF GEORGIA POWER’S COMPENSATION PROGRAM THAT ARE BASED ON FINANCIAL OBJECTIVES PROVIDE BENEFITS TO CUSTOMERS? 
A.Goals based on financial metrics – when included in an incentive compensation plan designed to balance financial and operational goals– provide benefits to customers by reducing expenses, creating greater efficiencies in operations, ensuring a financially strong company, and allowing access to the capital markets at a low cost. 
WHAT MIGHT GEORGIA POWER AND ITS CUSTOMERS EXPERIENCE IF THE COMPANY IS UNABLE TO RECOVER THESE COSTS?
Disallowing recovery of a financial component for a level of standard market pay forces a utility to consider modifying the entire incentive design to be based on operational measures alone (which are recoverable) and eliminate financial components of the incentive program design. However, making this change to an operational-based incentive program design would not be in the best interests of customers, as it would encourage employees to focus solely on operational performance with no balance or accountability to cost (causing employees to improve operational performance and customer satisfaction regardless of the financial cost to do so). Georgia Power strongly believes this imbalance is not in the best interests of customers, and also believes this will lead to higher rate increases for customers in the future with little to no benefit or improvement to operations.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH MESSRS. SMITH AND TROKEY’S statement that “the objectives of maximizing shareholder value on the one hand and minimizing costs to ratepayers on the other hand, are generally opposed to each other”?
A.No. To deliver value to shareholders, the Company must constantly find ways to run its business as efficiently as possible without sacrificing customer satisfaction and reliability. The efficiencies gained and the cost reductions resulting from those efforts are returned to customers through lower revenue requirements. Therefore, many of the Company’s actions to increase shareholder value will result in lower costs and increased value to customers.
VII.OTHER ISSUES
Revenue Forecast
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDED REDUCTION TO THE COMPANY’S PROJECTED REVENUE FORECAST?
A.No. While the Company appreciates that differences of opinion may exist in the intricacies of forecast modeling, the Company believes the projected revenue forecast included in its filing continues to be the most reasonable and appropriate method to determine the Company’s revenue requirement at present rates. In addition, PIA Staff agreed that the Company’s method was reasonable and did not identify any errors or corrections in the Company’s filing. PIA Staff simply offered an alternative for Commission consideration.
Q.WHAT IS THE COMPANY’S POSITION REGARDING PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATIONS TO ESTABLISH A REQUIREMENT FOR FILING DETAILED REVENUE FORECAST TESTIMONY AND WORKPAPERS IN FUTURE RATE CASES?
A.Should the Commission believe a modification to the Base Rate Case Minimum Filing Requirements (“MFRs”) is warranted to address the revenue forecast, the Company would support working with the Staff to make the necessary revisions.
Compliance Filing Requirements
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDED DATA REVISIONS TO THE CURRENT ANNUAL COMPLIANCE FILING REQUIREMENTS?
A.No. The data elements currently updated in the annual Compliance Filings established in the Settlement Agreement approved in the Company’s 2019 Rate Case provide benefits to customers and equity to all parties. The additional elements that Staff proposes to incorporate into the annual Compliance Filings risk transforming this established, efficient, and effective process into a burdensome, mini rate case proceeding. More importantly, as the number of data elements to be re-estimated and readdressed on an annual basis via the Compliance Filings increases, the more the benefits and value of the three-year ARP are undermined. 
Depreciation 
Q.PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR CONCERNS WITH PIA STAFF’S WITNESS KAUFMAN’S DEPRECIATION RECOMENDATION. 
A.Although the Company agrees with Mr. Kaufman’s proposed depreciation life extension of Plant Hatch, there are some important areas of disagreement with other aspects of his testimony, as described below: 
Contingencies should not be removed from production dismantling costs. 
The lives for hydro, transmission, distribution, and general plant should not be extended. 
Mr. Kaufman fails to update certain components of the calculations used for extending the lives of certain generating units and Transmission, Distribution, and General plant. 
In addition, PIA Staff incorrectly applies Mr. Kaufman’s proposed depreciation rates for Transmission, Distribution and General plant easements to the appropriate plant in service balances. 
Q. CAN YOU BRIEFLY DESCRIBE THE CONCERNS YOU HAVE REGARDING PIA STAFF’S DISMANTLEMENT COST ASSUMPTIONS? 
A.PIA Staff Witness Kaufman removes contingency from plant dismantlement cost estimates for coal and gas production facilities. He concludes that contingency represents “unexpected outcomes” and therefore, should be removed from the depreciation expense calculations for the Company’s coal and gas plants. 
When developing cost estimates, contingency is a fundamental element of a comprehensive and transparent estimate. Contingency is necessary to acknowledge and mitigate the risk of inherent cost uncertainties that accompany capital-intensive projects such as dismantlement of a coal or gas fired electric generation facility. 
Q. ARE THERE FACTORS THAT THE COMPANY MUST CONSIDER FOR CURRENT AND FUTURE PLANT DISMANTLEMENTS THAT HAVE NOT AFFECTED HISTORIC PLANT DISMANTLEMENTS?
A.Yes. Prior to the retirement of Plant Hammond in 2019, historic retired facilities did not have air environmental controls (e.g., baghouses, scrubbers, Selective Catalytic Reduction Systems). These environmental controls significantly increase the footprint and dismantlement cost of the remaining coal facilities. 
Q.HAVE HISTORIC DISMANTLEMENT COST ESTIMATES, INCLUDING CONTINGENCY, PREVIOUSLY BEEN APPROVED BY THE COMMISSION IN RETAIL COST OF SERVICE?
A.Yes. 
 VIII.GRID INVESTMENT PLAN, DERMS, AND RULES AND REGULATIONS SECTION G
Transmission Grid Investment
Q.HAS THE COMPANY USED OBJECTIVE APPROACHES TO DETERMINE WHICH EQUIPMENT SHOULD BE REPLACED AS PART OF THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN AS RECOMMENDED BY STAFF WITNESSES ALVAREZ, STEPHENS, AND RANA?
A.Yes, the Company relied on its long history of robust maintenance practices, internal experience, and historical operational knowledge to determine which transmission equipment will be replaced as part of the Grid Investment Plan. The Company incorporated this experience into its recommendation to replace transmission equipment that will be greater than the Company expected life by the end of the Grid Investment Plan period. While the industry expected life for transformers and breakers is 35 years and 25 years, respectively, the Company has been able to extend the life of equipment beyond its industry expected life through its existing comprehensive maintenance practices. The Company’s expected life for transformers and breakers is 50 years and 35 years, respectively. 
Contrary to Staff Witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana’s overreliance on inspection and performance testing for determining when equipment should be replaced, routine diagnostic testing and inspections cannot always accurately predict when equipment failure will occur on the system. Over the last ten years, 196 substation transformers failed in-service at an average age of 43 years, which is lower than the Company expected life for this asset. Of these 196 substation transformers, 108 failed (55%) after passing such diagnostic tests and inspections. Therefore, the Company’s experience to date informed its recommendation to replace transmission assets through the Grid Investment Program that have or will exceed their Company expected life by 2031 rather than solely relying on the results of the Company’s routine diagnostic tests and inspections. 
Q.WILL TRANSMISSION INVESTMENTS IN THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN PREVENT SYSTEM RELIABILITY DETERIORATION?
A.Yes. Transmission investments pursuant to the Company’s Grid Investment Plan are intended to replace assets that have or will have exceeded their Company expected life by the end of the plan. These assets are being replaced to prevent reliability challenges associated with aged transmission assets. Based on the Company’s operational experience and common understanding, the likelihood of failure for transmission assets included in the plan increases as the assets age beyond their expected life. Due to the large percentage of transmission equipment that is beyond or nearing its Company expected life, the Company designed the Grid Investment Plan to target the reasonable replacement of such assets to prevent transmission system reliability deterioration across the system. If these replacements are not undertaken, system reliability will be negatively impacted due to increased failures of aged equipment. Such failures can be catastrophic and simultaneous in nature. Replacing these aged assets will reduce the number of emergency failures, mitigate system operation reliability concerns, avoid higher maintenance and replacement costs, and ensure the safety of the general public and the Company’s employees. 
Q.WOULD IT BE PRACTICAL FOR THE COMPANY TO CONDUCT AND RELY ON A COST-BENEFIT ANALYSIS FOR TRANSMISSION INVESTMENTS INCLUDED IN THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN AS RECOMMENDED BY PIA STAFF WITNESSES?
A.No, it is impractical to perform a cost-benefit analysis for transmission investments included in the Grid Investment Plan due to the dynamic and networked nature of the transmission system and the number of variables that could change suddenly in such a study. The transmission system has a wide variety of components and factors that impact its operation, such as system loading and flows, facility and generation outages, and weather. The ability to conduct a meaningful and accurate cost-benefit analysis of transmission investments in the Grid Investment Plan is further complicated by the type of failure that could occur (controlled or catastrophic), impacts to other capital projects, lead times for replacement equipment, and the cost of generation redispatch due to loss of transmission elements. The constant change in these variables would produce a cost-benefit analysis with unreliable conclusions that could be rendered obsolete or invalid at any given time. The results of such an analysis would be questionable, at best. For these reasons, the Company used asset age to determine capital investment in each transmission facility to mitigate the risk of outages associated with a significant and growing number of assets that are approaching or have exceeded their Company expected lives.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY’S DATA SUPPORT STAFF WITNESS STEPHENS’ ASSERTION THAT TRANSMISSION EQUIPMENT CAN OPERATE SAFELY AND RELIABLY PAST EXPECTED LIFE FOR UP TO 100 YEARS?
A.No. Witness Stephens’ example of a single 99-year-old transformer on Georgia Power’s system should not be used as a basis for the assumption that the Company’s entire transformer fleet can or should operate that far beyond Company expected life. The tables below represent transmission and distribution substation equipment within 10 years of the oldest vintages for transmission and distribution substation equipment on Georgia Power’s system. The vintages shown in Tables 2 and 3 represent a significantly small portion of each equipment category, with the highest vintage percentage being only 2% of the entire population for that equipment type. More importantly, power transformers are less than 0.2% for both substation types.
