This data indicates that while a very small portion of substation equipment can operate well past Company expected life, Georgia Power should not expect the overwhelming majority of its transmission and distribution assets to operate safely and reliably for this long. As mentioned previously, the Company’s data over the last ten years shows that substation transformers have failed at an average age of 43 years, which is well below the timeframe that Mr. Stephens suggests that equipment can remain in operation. Furthermore, the average age of failure for transformers over the last ten years (43 years) is past industry expected life (35 years), but earlier than the age (50 years) at which this equipment will be replaced within the Grid Investment Plan. Given the large number of transmission assets that will exceed their Company expected life in the near future, replacement within the Grid Investment Plan, as designed, is a better solution for the transmission system than simply operating these assets for an unknown period of time until they fail. If the Company does not continue with these investments, the risk of catastrophic equipment failure along with facility and customer outages will increase.
Q.IS THE COMPANY PERFORMING PREEMPTIVE REPLACEMENTS FOR TRANSMISSION EQUIPMENT INCLUDED IN THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN?
A.No. The Company disagrees with Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens’ characterization of the Company’s Grid Investment Plan strategy for transmission assets as “preemptive replacement.” The Company relied on its extensive operational and maintenance experience, outage and failure history, and ability to extend asset expected life to determine the age at which it should replace transmission assets within the Grid Investment Plan. In addition, the Company reviewed, analyzed, and compared its replacement assumptions against industry expected life ages for the transmission equipment to be replaced in the Grid Investment Plan. 
As the Company testified during direct hearings, the bathtub curve is utilized to determine the variation of equipment failure rate with respect to time. The bathtub curve presented in Witness Alvarez and Stephens’ testimony is based upon the estimated useful life of a hypothetical piece of equipment. In contrast, the bathtub curve, as illustrated in Figure 1 below, is based on the expected life of an asset, which is defined as the life expectancy of the Company’s equipment. The life expectancy of equipment is supported by internal maintenance procedures, past experiences, equipment specifications, and monitoring of equipment. It is important to note that estimated useful life and expected life are two very separate and distinct terms.
FIGURE 1: Bathtub Curve for Expected Life

Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens mistakenly point to the middle of their estimated useful life bathtub curve and misrepresent the point at which the Company is replacing transmission equipment as part of the Grid Investment Plan. However, the Company is replacing equipment in the “end-of-life period” where failures occur at an exponential rate due to age-related degradation of equipment. The Company is strategically targeting transmission projects within this “end-of-life period” in a controlled and planned manner to prevent customer interruptions, catastrophic equipment failure, and better manage replacement costs for customers. As mentioned in several discovery requests, the cost of replacing transmission equipment that has catastrophically failed is 2 to 13 times the cost of replacing this equipment in a planned manner. Within this context and background, the Company strongly maintains that it is not performing preemptive replacement on its transmission equipment as part of its Grid Investment Plan. 
Q.WHEN FAILURES OCCUR FOR AGED EQUIPMENT ON THE SYSTEM, SHOULD THE COMPANY SOLELY DEPEND ON SPARE EQUIPMENT BEING AVAILABLE TO REPLACE THE FAILED EQUIPMENT AS SUGGESTED BY STAFF WITNESSES? 
A.No. Solely depending on spare equipment to supplement failed equipment would present both operational and supply chain challenges if failures occur. The Company’s equipment stock levels are aimed to address emergency replacements based on historical failure rates and other factors. The current stock levels are agreed upon by Integrated Transmission System (“ITS”) Participants, which along with customers, bear the cost of the spare equipment pool. If the Grid Investment Plan is not continued, 44% of substation transformers and 37% of substation breakers will have exceeded their Company expected life by 2031. With having such a large amount of equipment at or nearing the “end-of-life” period at the same time, the Company is concerned that the existing spare equipment stock will not be able to keep up with the replacement demand for failed equipment. For example, the Company’s current stock levels for substation transformers represents 3% of the total population transformers on the system. Furthermore, there are 171 different types of transformers on Georgia Power’s system. The Company currently only has spare transformers available for 38 of the 171 transformer types, with many of the available transformer spare counts being limited to only one unit on hand. 
Due to industry supply chain challenges, the lead times for all substation equipment are longer than the Company has seen in many years, with average substation transformer lead times increasing from approximately six months to three years. These lead times continue to grow as other utilities invest in their system and global pressures on the supply-chain for grid infrastructure continue. As transmission equipment gets older and failure rates increase, the Company and customers will be negatively impacted given the number of spares in existing inventory. The Company could choose to increase its spare equipment pool, but this would also negatively impact customers, as keeping too much spare inventory would lead to higher expenditures and higher carrying costs. Depending on spare equipment means that the Company would wait for equipment to fail before it acts. The Company is strongly opposed to such an approach. The Grid Investment Plan offers a controlled, programmatic, multi-year approach to replacing aging transmission assets in a way that reasonably accounts for these operational and supply chain considerations.
Distribution Grid Investment
Q. DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH STAFF’S PROPOSED REDUCTIONS TO THE DISTRIBUTION PORTION OF THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN, AND THE OVERALL ASSERTION “THERE IS NO PENDING RELIABILITY EMERGENCY”?
A. No, the Company does not agree that the Basic Insulation Level (“BIL”) and Undergrounding investment packages, which make up approximately 85% of Distribution Grid investments, should be removed from the program. Nor does the Company agree with the statement that “there is no pending reliability emergency.” Georgia Power’s system reliability will continue to worsen every year that investments are not made. Between 2013 and 2021, the Company’s system average interruption duration index (“SAIDI”) worsened by approximately 44% and system average interruption frequency index (“SAIFI”) worsened by approximately 10%. For a subset of feeders (also referred to as circuits) in the system, and the customers on those feeders, the decline in reliability is even more pronounced. This worsening reliability has significant negative impacts on customer experience due to more frequent and longer outages, as well as increases in the economic cost incurred by customers.
It is within this context, and with extreme care, that the Company created the Distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan, which is designed to ensure lasting reliability improvements while minimizing the total cost to the customer. Removing the BIL and Undergrounding investment packages from the Grid Investment Plan would result in significantly longer and more frequent outages across the system, as these packages are the best way to permanently address the root causes of outages on these feeders. In the absence of these investments, the Company would achieve an end-state SAIDI of approximately 127 by 2031, essentially equal to the program starting point SAIDI in 2019, compared to the Grid Investment Plan’s current projected end-state of approximately 80. Therefore, the Staff’s recommendations would maintain the system-level SAIDI at third quartile levels.
Q. IS THE REVISION TO THE COMPANY’S END-STATE SAIDI FOR THE DISTRIBUTION PORTION OF THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN A REFLECTION OF REDUCED RELIABILITY EXPECTATIONS, AS CLAIMED BY STAFF?
A. No. The revision to the end-state SAIDI targeted in the Company’s Distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan is a result of lessons learned from the first three years of implementation and not a reflection of changed reliability expectations. In fact, the Company has seen better than expected reliability improvements from the set of investments that have completed construction. Initial results indicate an improvement of approximately 65% in both SAIDI and SAIFI for Grid projects that have been completed for over a year and cycled through both a winter and summer peak since completion. The investments are working and are delivering improved reliability for Georgia Power’s customers. 
As outlined in our direct testimony, the Company refined certain components of the Grid Investment Plan based on insights and lessons learned in the first three years of the program:
Georgia Power confirmed that the overall strategy and economic rationale supporting the Grid Investment Plan is optimal for customers. The specific investment packages deployed are producing expected benefits and delivering positive results.
The total cost required to deliver individual investments is higher than the Company originally projected because of increases in scope (e.g., additional automation devices required) and in the cost of work (e.g., inflation, COVID shortages). 
Undergrounding remains the most effective solution for improving customer reliability but, as investment costs have increased across the program, the use of Undergrounding should be carefully evaluated to ensure it is used only where it is the most efficient solution. 
Given the learnings above, the Company reassessed and refreshed the Distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan, using the same methodology used in the 2019 Rate Case, to ensure that the investments being made continue to result in the lowest total cost to customers. As a result of this evaluation, the Company determined that the set of distribution investments in the best interests of customers are projected to deliver an 80 end-state SAIDI as compared to the 65 end-state SAIDI included in the 2019 rate case plan, which is still first quartile results compared to industry peers. The Company considers this intermittent refinement of the 10-year Grid Investment Plan to be essential for ensuring that customer capital is managed responsibly. 
Q. WHY DID THE COMPANY ELECT TO OPTIMIZE THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN AT THE SYSTEM RATHER THAN FEEDER LEVEL? 
A.The Grid Investment Plan optimizes benefits at the system level rather than the feeder level because the cost of system reliability is borne by all customers served by Georgia Power. In other words, the explicit cost for capital investment and ongoing O&M expense for the grid is paid for by all customers, regardless of each customer’s reliability experience and regardless of whether the customer’s individual feeder is included within the Grid Investment Plan. The Company intentionally chose to optimize benefits across the system to ensure the investments made were in the best interest of all customers instead of just the subset of customers served by an individual feeder. 
Q.DOES THE COMPANY’S DISTRIBUTION PORTION OF THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN IGNORE A FEEDER-BY-FEEDER ANALYSIS AS SUGGESTED BY STAFF WITNESSES ALVAREZ AND STEPHENS?
A.No. Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens propose that the Company conduct a root cause analysis for every feeder on the Company’s system, which implies that the Company has not done such analysis. However, this is a misconception. In fact, each and every distribution investment within the Grid Investment Plan is evaluated on a feeder-by-feeder basis with a root cause analysis. The Company does exactly the level of analysis suggested by Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens. 
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE FEEDER-LEVEL ROOT CAUSE ANALYSIS THE COMPANY CONDUCTS FOR POTENTIAL GRID INVESTMENTS.
A.To evaluate a distribution feeder for the Grid Investment Plan, first, the Company aggregates all the pertinent and available data across the system. This data begins with five years of total reliability performance for each feeder as measured by SAIDI, SAIFI, customer minutes of interruption (CMI), and frequency of different outage event causes. The initial ‘hurdle’ for any feeder to qualify for investment is that the feeder has had sustained poor performance over the past five years. 
The Company then undertakes a second evaluation to determine the root causes of outages on each feeder. In this step, the Company disaggregates every feeder’s reliability performance to understand the events that caused those outages. For example, the Company evaluates what percentage of a feeder’s outages were the result of vegetation, vehicles, animals, or weather and how that percentage compares to the rest of the system. An evaluated feeder qualifies for a distribution investment package that would permanently resolve the outage cause(s) only if it experiences a higher percentage of outages driven by that particular cause(s) than the system average. In this way, the Company deploys standard, repeatable criteria systematically across all feeders to identify outliers in each category of outage cause. 
Therefore, prior to optimizing the plan, the Company has completed a feeder-by-feeder, root cause analysis on the entire Distribution system. Through this analysis, the Company determines whether a feeder should be considered for any investment and, if so, which of the six possible investment options specifically would address the root causes of reliability issues on that feeder. 
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE HOW THE COMPANY SELECTS FEEDERS FOR INVESTMENT AFTER CONDUCTING THE ROOT CAUSE ANALYSIS.
A. For each of the possible investment packages identified during the root cause analysis, the Company projects the costs and the reliability benefits specific to deploying qualified investment package(s) on each feeder. The cost and benefit for each investment will vary depending on unique characteristics of each feeder, such as starting reliability performance and number of line miles to be worked.
The Company then evaluates the impact of all possible combinations of feeder level investments to determine the final investment plan that provides the best reliability and the lowest total cost to customers. To do this, the Company evaluates the total cost to all Georgia Power customers of each potential investment plan and selects the plan that minimizes total cost to customers across the system– inclusive of the cost of investment, the cost of remaining outages, and the cost of O&M that customers bear. In this fashion, the Company considers the direct cost of investments as well as the costs customers bear from increased outages related to not investing in some potential investments. The Company acknowledges that it has an obligation to provide reliable electric service and minimize outages for all customers and, therefore, must evaluate the total cost to deliver the best plan for all of its 2.7 million customers. 
Q. DID STAFF WITNESSES ALVAREZ AND STEPHENS PROVIDE ADEQUATE SUPPORT FOR THEIR ASSERTION THAT BIL AND UNDERGROUNDING ARE NOT COST EFFECTIVE?
A. No, the Company fervently disagrees with Staff’s assertions that the BIL and Undergrounding packages are not cost-effective. In executing their cost effectiveness analysis, Staff systematically underestimated investment benefits by not accurately representing the cost to customers of foregoing BIL and Undergrounding:
First, Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens did not account for the continued reliability degradation that customers will experience if feeders selected for BIL or Undergrounding are denied investment, underestimating the economic cost to customers of the increased outages.
Second, Staff did not quantify the outage repair-related and vegetation-related O&M expenses that will be incurred if these feeders are removed from the Grid Investment Plan.
In contrast, the Company has taken extreme care to account for all costs and benefits that will be experienced by customers during the formulation of the Grid Investment Plan. The Company has the utmost confidence that the distribution investments that are part of the Grid Investment Plan are in the best interests of customers, resulting in the greatest reliability benefits for the lowest total cost to customers.
Q. DID STAFF WITNESSES ALVAREZ AND STEPHENS PROVIDE ADEQUATE SUPPORT FOR THEIR RECOMMENDATION THAT THE COMPANY INCREASE VEGETATION MANAGEMENT IN LIEU OF THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN?
A. No. Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens offered no independent analysis to validate the assertion that increased vegetation management is an effective alternative to the BIL or Undergrounding Grid Investment Plan packages. Though vegetation management has an important place in managing overall system reliability, it is a temporary solution that cannot address a variety of other outage drivers, such as vehicles, wildlife, and lightning. During cross examination, Mr. Stephens himself acknowledged that he had made an assumption about vegetation management as a cost-effective alternative. 
Staff did not explain how vegetation management could generate the same expected level of reliability improvement as the dedicated investment packages included in the Grid Investment Plan. Georgia Power’s current vegetation management program is designed to maintain reliability, not improve it. In addition, Mr. Stephens confirmed during cross examination that no analysis had been done to determine how much it would cost to actually improve reliability through vegetation management. Further, many activities fall within the category of ‘vegetation management,’ and Mr. Stephens did not complete any analysis to determine which activities specifically would need to be added or increased to obtain reliability improvements through vegetation management. 
Finally, Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens proposed a legislated expansion of utility rights of way as a potential approach to improving reliability. However, Mr. Alvarez confirmed at the hearing that he had not undertaken any analysis of how long it would take or how much it would cost to pass such legislation. As such, PIA Staff failed to demonstrate how its proposed alternatives to the Grid Investment Plan were either less costly or more beneficial than the Company’s proposal. 
Q. DID STAFF PROVIDE ADEQUATE SUPPORT FOR THEIR RECOMMENDATION THAT THE COMPANY IMPROVE ITS WORST PERFORMING FEEDER PROGRAM IN LIEU OF THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN?
A.No. As with its vegetation management recommendation, Staff failed to conduct the analysis required to validate the recommendation that the Company should focus its attention only on feeders that are performing 2.5 times worse than average as part of its ‘Worst Performing Feeder’ program. This lack of analysis is inconsistent with Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens’ own assertions that grid investments should only ever be made if they are cost effective at the feeder level.
Staff did not consider the type of investment that would be required to address the root cause of outages on feeders that are performing 2.5 times worse than average. For many of these feeders, BIL and Undergrounding would be the best solution that would address root causes – yet both of these are investment packages within the Grid Investment Program that Staff has proposed for removal. Moreover, Staff did not consider the economic or O&M cost for outages that will continue to be experienced on feeders that do not meet PIA Staff’s ‘worst performing’ criteria. 
Q. PLEASE EXPLAIN WHY A MORE ROBUST WORST PERFORMING FEEDER PROGRAM WOULD NOT BE A DIFFERENTIATED ALTERNATIVE TO THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN. 
A.The Distribution portion of the Company’s Grid Investment Plan already encompasses the key qualities that Staff indicates should be part of a more robust ‘Worst Performing Feeder’ program. This includes clear and repeatable criteria to identify poor-performing feeders, a dedicated budget and associated personnel to administer the program, and standard tracking and reporting on the program’s progress. 
First, the Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens recommend establishing “clear criteria for identifying circuits for additional attention,” and that the Company “compute one or more reliability metrics for each circuit to enable the identification of poor performers.” As is presented in the Customer Benefit Analysis, the Company has clear and specific criteria for each Distribution investment package in the Grid Investment Program. This includes reliability-specific criteria (e.g., SAIDI, SAIFI), physical attribute-related criteria (e.g., overhead vs. underground), and criteria that identify the root causes of the outages (e.g., outages due to vegetation or vehicles, feeder load). Where the root cause is unclear, not addressable, or cost prohibitive, an investment is not made.
Second, Staff recommends “that specific budgets for the program be established,” and that there be designated “personnel / organizational accountabilities for program administration.” The Distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan has a designated budget and designated personnel. In the 2019 Rate Case, the first three years of the Distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan were approved by the Commission. Moreover, since 2019, the Company has established a team solely devoted to the Grid Investment Plan – from program and project managers, to engineers, supply chain personnel, and construction workers on the ground.
Last, Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens recommend “standards for tracking spending, projects, and results.” The Company rigorously tracks all spending for the Grid Investment Plan (at both the project and program level), as well as the reliability performance of each Distribution feeder – before, during, and after investment. The Company reports on the Grid Investment Program performance during the Company’s rate case and makes itself available to respond to any data requests on the Grid Investment Program, as evidenced by the substantial volume of Grid-related data requests that were responded to in the current case. The fact that the Company has robust standards for tracking spending, projects, and results is evidenced by the adjustments made by the Company in the overall plan and optimal range for investment.
The Distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan encompasses the very qualities that Staff recommends should be part of the Company’s ‘Worst Performing Feeder’ program and should be approved as is without modification.
DERMS and Rules and Regulations Section G Recommendation
Q.ARE CURRENT SOFTWARE SYSTEMS SUFFICIENT ENOUGH TO EFFICIENTLY MANAGE THE GROWING AMOUNT OF DISTRIBUTED ENERGY RESOURCES (“DER”) THAT WILL BE INTERCONNECTED ON THE SYSTEM?
A.No. While current software systems allow for the monitoring of power flows and equipment statuses, they lack the enhanced monitoring and operational capabilities necessary to allow for the optimization of an electric system that takes full advantage of a proliferation of DER. The applications currently in place are not capable of managing the substantial number of DERs expected to connect to the grid in the coming years. As more DER are added to the system, it is essential for the Company to have visibility, predictability, and control (where applicable) of these resources. Failure to do so could compromise the Company’s ability to accurately forecast and optimize the full array of generation resources, and also negatively impact power quality and reliability. Further, a system operator will not be able to process the significant amount of data coming in and make informed decisions quickly enough to optimize grid operation if using current software systems. 
In contrast, a DERMS fully integrated with the Company’s other grid control systems unlocks the potential to continually optimize larger portions of the grid and solve for a variety of constraints, such as economic dispatch, Volt/VAR optimization, and reliability. Additionally, DERMS will allow the Company to consider demand side resources alongside supply side resources in future IRPs, as ordered by the Commission in the 2022 IRP. The Company’s implementation of DERMS will allow for maturation of the solution while thousands or tens of thousands of additional discrete DER are connected to the grid. If DER growth is unrestricted without the installation of DERMS, the Company risks a loss of grid control, which would compromise grid reliability and stability. A DERMS solution is a natural, logical, and necessary step for the Company to prepare for the growth and integration of variable and distributed energy resources. 
Q.COULD THE COMPANY DELAY ITS DERMS PROGRAM UNTIL DER ADOPTION IS HIGHER?
A.No. There are significant risks to grid stability if the Company were to delay implementing DERMS. Beginning implementation of a DERMS solution now allows for the full DERMS capabilities envisioned by the Company to be developed in parallel with increasing DER penetration across the electric grid. Further, the complexity of integrating DERMS with the Company’s existing critical software systems will require a significant amount of time. Undertaking DERMS over the next three years reduces the risk of restricting DER interconnections in the future because of a lack of DER forecasting, control, and optimization capability. 
Q.  IS THE INTENT BEHIND THE COMPANY’S PROPOSED EDITS TO SECTION G OF THE RULES AND REGULATIONS TO REQUIRE UNCONDITIONAL ACCESS AND CONTROL OF CUSTOMER GENERATORS, AS STAFF SUGGESTS?
A.No. The Company proposed requiring that the customer generation facilities interconnecting to the Company’s electric system include a communications interface, installed and maintained by the customer, that supports information exchange with, and control by, Georgia Power so that the Company may continue to operate the electric system in a safe, reliable, and efficient manner. The proposed requirement to allow for communication and increased visibility to DERs operating in parallel with the system is critical to maintaining the system’s safety and reliability. As the number of distributed generators grows, the ability to communicate and potentially interact with customer generation will allow for additional growth that might not otherwise be possible. Notably, the Company has only requested this change for customer generation facilities sized 250 kW and above that operate in parallel with the Company’s electric system for more than 100 milliseconds. 
Q.   DID THE COMPANY INTEND FOR THIS PROPOSED CHANGE TO BE CONSISTENT WITH THE REQUIREMENTS OF IEEE-1547? 
A.  Yes. The Company intends for the proposed changes to be consistent with the requirements of IEEE-1547, as they may be amended from time to time. 
Q.WHAT IS THE JUSTIFICATION FOR THE COMPANY’S REQUEST TO REQUIRE EXISTING CUSTOMER GENERATORS TO INSTALL A LOCAL INTERFACE FOR COMMUNICATION AND CONTROL?
A.Utility visibility and control of customer generation facilities becomes an essential function as the adoption of DER increases. Without visibility, it is impossible for a utility to ascertain the status, availability, real time actual output, and forecast availability of a resource. Without control, it is impossible for a utility to update functional settings such as active power setpoint, frequency response, and reactive power modes in response to real time grid conditions. Both visibility and control are necessary functions to enable real time economic optimization of the electric grid. The Company is requesting that all existing and future customer generation facilities be equipped with a local communications interface so that these facilities will be available to the Company’s eventual DERMS solution. Without visibility and control capability on existing customer generation facilities, gaps will be present in the Company’s ability to be fully aware of the interaction of those facilities with other generation facilities and the rest of the electric grid.
Revenue Requirement Impacts
Q.WITH RESPECT TO THE RECOMMENDED CAPITAL REDUCTIONS FOR THE GRID INVESTMENT PROGRAM, DID PIA STAFF CORRECTLY CALCULATE ITS REVENUE REQUIREMENT IMPACT?
A.No. PIA Staff assumed the projected capital expenditures for the Grid Investment Program will close to plant-in-service as they are incurred, thus incorrectly assuming the capital expenditures are the same as closures in its calculation of the revenue requirement impact. Since the capital expenditures are projected to close to plant in service later than when they are incurred in the Company’s filing, assuming closures are the same as capital expenditures artificially inflates the reduction of average plant in service balances based on their recommendation. This results in a significantly overstated revenue requirement reduction for the test year and years 2023 through 2025. In addition, PIA Staff did not consider the impact of removing the related accumulated deferred income taxes (ADITs) in their proposed Grid Investment Program capital reduction, which also caused the revenue requirement reduction to be overstated.
IX.RECENT TAX LEGISLATION
Q.IS THE COMPANY ACTIVELY ANALYZING AND PURSUING THE BENEFITS PROVIDED IN THE RECENTLY PASSED INFLATION REDUCTION ACT (“IRA”) AND INFRASTRUCTURE AND INVESTMENT JOBS ACT (“IIJA”) LEGISLATION, AND DOES THE COMPANY PLAN TO PASS THOSE BENEFITS ON TO CUSTOMERS?
Yes. The Company is in the process of assessing the various potential impacts of the IRA and IIJA. The Company expects the IRA and IIJA will benefit customers through reduced project costs and lower income taxes. The Company has already identified several benefits from the IRA, with the details of those benefits provided in response to data request STF-LA-5-14. The Company continues to seek out additional opportunities under the new legislation that will benefit customers; however, there are many components of the IRA and IIJA that will take time to evaluate, and significant guidance from the IRS and Treasury will be necessary to administer the tax law changes in both Acts. The Company fully intends to pass any tax benefits related to the IRA and the IIJA on to customers.
Q.BASED ON THE COMPANY’S INITIAL ANALYSIS OF THE IRA, WHAT OPPORTUNITIES FOR TAX CREDITS WERE IDENTIFIED?
The Company’s initial analysis of the IRA identified approximately $44 million of additional tax credit benefits related to projected, qualified renewable energy and electric vehicle investments. The largest benefit identified in the Company’s initial analysis relates to investment tax credits (“ITC”) associated with the Mossy Branch battery storage project. The Mossy Branch project, which is projected to go into service in 2023, is a project that the Company currently believes would qualify for a 30% ITC. Additionally, with the Commission’s approval, the Company can opt-out of the traditional ITC normalization structure which would result in giving customers all the ITC benefit in the year the project goes into service instead of over the life of the asset. The Company also expects to generate ITCs on the McGrau Ford battery facility; however, those credits are not expected to be generated until after 2025 when the facility is expected to be placed into service. In addition, the Company’s initial analysis shows the IRA will provide more options to pursue and compete for lower cost solar energy investments as a result of expanded tax credits. Through the IRA, the Company now has an option to choose between either the traditional ITC or a new Production Tax Credit (“PTC”) for Company-owned solar projects; however, the Company is still not able to claim ITC or PTCs on solar resources procured through purchased power agreements, which account for the majority of the Company’s new solar resources expected to come online in years 2023 through 2025. For new Company-owned solar facilities projected to come online in those years, the Company estimates the PTC option will provide approximately $0.4 million of additional benefits for customers in 2025 related to projected ash pond solar investments at Plant McIntosh, Plant McDonough, and Plant Hammond.
Since the filing of direct testimony, the Company identified IRA benefits for which it may qualify. Georgia Power estimates that the Hydro Modernization projects approved by the Commission in the 2019 and 2022 IRP is currently estimated to provide approximately $3 million of tax benefits for the three years 2023 through 2025. The Company also anticipates qualifying for credits on the Integrated Hydrogen Microgrid project that was approved by the Commission in the 2022 IRP.
Q.HAS THE COMPANY PERFORMED AN INITIAL ANALYSIS OF QUALIFICATIONS FOR PTCs ON EXISTING NUCLEAR FACILITIES AVAILABLE THROUGH THE IRA?
The Company has performed initial analysis on the zero-emission nuclear production tax credits on existing nuclear facilities currently in service. Based on current guidelines in the IRA, it does not appear the Company will be eligible for the zero emission PTCs on the Company’s nuclear facilities currently in operation; however, the Company is hopeful additional guidance from the Treasury will be issued on the gross receipts calculation used to calculate the credits that may allow the Company to qualify for these credits for the benefit of customers. 
Q.HOW DOES THE COMPANY PROPOSE TO COMMUNICATE RESULTS OF THE IRA AND IIJA ANALYSIS TO THE COMMISSION? 
A.The Company proposes to use existing mechanisms for data communication and discovery for this and future rate proceedings to provide tax and other savings resulting from the IRA and IIJA, noting that there should be benefits associated with the IRA and IIJA well beyond the period of this three-year ARP. The Company can agree to record these tax benefits to a regulatory liability for future consideration by this Commission to be used for the benefit of customers as soon as possible.
Q.DOES THIS CONCLUDE YOUR TESTIMONY?
A.Yes.

===== 2022 Rate Case Rebuttal Testimony Evans_Legg FINAL.docx =====
REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF 
LEE EVANS AND LARRY LEGG
ON BEHALF OF
GEORGIA POWER COMPANY

DOCKET NO. 44280

INTRODUCTION
Q.PLEASE STATE YOUR NAMES, TITLES, AND BUSINESS ADDRESSES.
A.Lee Evans, Director of Demand Planning & Analysis for Southern Company Services, Inc. (“SCS”). My business address is 241 Ralph McGill Boulevard N.E., Atlanta, Georgia 30308.
A.Larry T. Legg, Director of Pricing and Rates for Georgia Power Company (“Georgia Power” or the “Company”), 241 Ralph McGill Boulevard N.E., Atlanta, Georgia 30308.
Q.MR. LEGG AND MR. EVANS, DID YOU PREVIOUSLY PRESENT DIRECT AND SUPPLEMENTAL DIRECT TESTIMONY AND EXHIBITS ON BEHALF OF GEORGIA POWER IN THIS PROCEEDING?
A.Yes. 
Q.WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF YOUR TESTIMONY?
A.The purpose of our testimony is to respond to recommendations made by the Georgia Public Service Commission’s (“Commission”) Public Interest Advocacy (“PIA”) Staff and Intervenors as they relate to rate design and cost-of-service. The fact that we may not specifically address a particular recommendation made by PIA Staff or an Intervenor should not be construed as support for such position.
Q.ARE YOU PROVIDING ANY EXHIBITS TO YOUR TESTIMONY?
A.No. 
Q.PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR TESTIMONY.
A.As part of our longstanding commitment to meet our customers’ energy needs, Georgia Power offers our customers an extensive portfolio of more than 40 individual electric service base rate options from which to choose, with multiple options available for customers of differing sizes and classes. These rate offerings, including the changes proposed by Georgia Power in this rate case, will enable the Company to continue providing customers with clean, safe, and reliable electricity at prices that are below the national average. While a more thorough discussion of the issues we address on rebuttal appears in the following sections of our testimony, here we provide a general summary of our testimony with respect to a number of key issues. 
Residential Rates
Notably, and for the avoidance of doubt, the Company is not proposing to discontinue the Residential Service (“R”) tariff; rather, the Company proposes to make the rate unavailable to new residential premises that establish service after December 31, 2022. Georgia Power also opposes the structural changes to the R rate proposed by PIA Staff, as these changes would fundamentally alter the rate for the more than 1.8 million customers currently taking service under the rate without due consideration of this impact. 
The Company’s proposal to close the R rate for new residential premises effective January 1, 2023, should be approved. This proposal is a logical step in the transition to more modern rate structures, such as Time of Use Residential Demand (“TOU-RD”), with price signals that more accurately reflect the cost of electric service for customers. To be clear, if the R rate is closed to new premises, customers will still be able to choose from among any of the Company’s residential rate options for which they qualify, in addition to TOU-RD as the default residential rate. Contrary to the assertions of PIA Staff and certain intervenors, the TOU-RD rate is designed to be revenue neutral and not cost customers more than the R rate. Because the TOU-RD rate provides better price signals, customers are able to make more informed decisions about their energy use and can save on their electric bills by shifting usage or staggering the use of key appliances.
Commercial and Industrial Rates
The Company’s commercial and industrial rates continue to be appropriately structured, and the Company does not agree with the proposed changes to these rates. PIA Staff’s recommendation that customers on marginal rates should be assessed the fully embedded cost of the Environmental Compliance Cost Recovery (“ECCR”) tariff is fundamentally inconsistent with how marginal rates like Real-Time Pricing (“RTP”) are designed to operate and should be denied. Fully embedded costs are recovered from the Customer Baseline Load (“CBL”) portion of the marginally priced rates, while marginal costs are recovered from the marginal portion of the rates. To include fully embedded costs such as ECCR in the marginal portion of the rates like RTP would undermine the rate structure and significantly decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future customers. Altogether, PIA Staff’s recommendation to include ECCR in marginal RTP prices, as well as their recommendation to recover ECCR costs on a per kWh basis rather than as a percent of base revenue, results in an overall increase to the RTP tariffs of $150 million. The Commission should reject both recommendations. The Commission should also reject Department of Defense Witness Blank’s recommendation to modify the Company’s criteria for the reduction of CBLs for customers who install behind the meter generation because customer generation does not permanently remove the customer’s load and does not justify the reduction of a customer’s CBL.
Community Solar
The Commission should approve the Company’s requested increase to the Community Solar block prices as agreed to by PIA Staff, since the purpose of this program is to support the development of renewable energy but does not guarantee bill savings. In addition, the Georgia Coalition of Local Government’s (“Coalition”) proposed price reduction is premised on a misunderstanding of how the program operates and should be rejected. 
Charge It Electric Vehicle Tariff
The Commission should also approve the Charge It Electric Vehicle (“CIEV”) tariff, as agreed to by PIA Staff, as an appropriately structured and time limited offering to encourage the growth of electric vehicles (“EVs”) in Georgia. 
Cost-of-Service Study
The Company’s cost-of-service study (“COSS”) continues to reasonably allocate the Company’s revenue requirement across retail rates based on principles of cost causation. Georgia Power’s continued use of a 12 coincident peak (“12-CP”) methodology to allocate production costs is reasonable and appropriately recognizes the Company’s need to plan and operate its system in every month of the year. Staff and Intervenor proposals to replace the 12-CP method with an energy-based allocation method or summer-focused allocation method are neither compelling nor justified. 
Parity Adjustments
Finally, the Company is supportive of general movement towards parity but not the rate specific adjustments proposed by PIA Staff or Georgia Association of Manufacturers (“GAM”) Witness Pollock. Instead, the Company supports parity adjustments rooted in the Company’s COSS that are made at the rate group rather than individual rate level. 
Q.HOW IS THE REMAINDER OF YOUR TESTIMONY STRUCTURED?
A.Our testimony is organized as follows:
Section II (pages 5 to 11) discusses proposed changes to residential rates;
Section III (pages 11 to 14) discusses proposed modifications to commercial rates;
Section IV (pages 14 to 18) discusses proposed changes to the Community Solar and CIEV tariffs;
Section V (pages 18 to 19) discusses the Company’s proposed Interconnection Application Fee;
Section VI (pages 19 to 20) discusses hourly interval data;
Section VII (pages 20 to 22) discusses proposed changes to the COSS; and 
Section VIII (page 23) discusses proposed parity adjustments.
RESIDENTIAL RATES
Residential Service “R” Tariff
Q.PLEASE EXPLAIN THE COMPANY’S PROPOSED CHANGES TO THE R RATE. 
A.As Mr. Legg states in his Direct Testimony, the Company proposes to update the R tariff to make the rate unavailable to newly constructed residential premises that establish service on Georgia Power’s electric system after December 31, 2022. This update will encourage residential customers to adopt more modern rate structures with price signals that more accurately and appropriately reflect the cost of electric service. However, the R tariff will remain available to residential premises for which electric service is established prior to January 1, 2023.
As part of its commitment to customer satisfaction, Georgia Power has actively promoted the variety of residential rate options it offers to help customers select the rate that best fits their needs. As a result, customer adoption of residential rate options such as the TOU-RD (also known as “Smart Usage”), TOU-PEV, Pay by Day (“PBD”), and FlatBill tariffs has increased significantly. Indeed, the number of residential customers who have chosen to participate in a time-of-use rate has increased by more than 250%. Many customers have demonstrated a preference for these rate plans, which are designed to provide customers with options that best meet their needs. By closing the R rate to new premises, the Company will continue to gradually transition new residential accounts onto the other rates within the Domestic Group that better align with the cost to serve customers. 
Q.IS PIA STAFF PROPOSING ANY MODIFICATIONS TO THE R RATE?
A.Yes. PIA Staff proposes to eliminate the declining block rates during non-summer months and move to a flat rate for all kilowatt hour (“kWh”) usage. 
Q.DO YOU AGREE WITH PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATION TO REPLACE THE DECLINING WINTER BLOCK RATE STRUCTURE WITH A FLAT ENERGY RATE?
A.No. Notably, the Commission has declined to adopt this recommendation in each of the last two rate cases and should continue to do so in this case. Although the price signal the R rate sends is not as strong as more recent tariff designs, the Company originally designed the R rate to reflect the seasonality of costs to serve residential customers. The inclining price blocks in June through September appropriately reflect the fact that the Company’s costs are higher in the summer months. Importantly, the inclining price structure provides incentives for energy efficiency and conservation. The declining block structure in the winter months recognizes that off-peak costs are generally lower and, therefore, provides price signals to reflect this reality. The declining block structure provides an economical option for customers choosing to use electric heating. The prices are set to more than cover the Company’s marginal costs, and resulting revenues place downward pressure on rates for all customers. 
Q.ARE THERE OTHER CONCERNS WITH PIA STAFF’S PROPOSAL TO SIGNIFICANTLY MODIFY THE R RATE?
A.Yes. PIA Staff is proposing significant modifications to the R rate without providing any analysis or discussion of the impacts these changes would have on the large number of customers who are currently on that rate. Currently, more than 1.8 million customers take service on the R rate, and it is not clear that PIA Staff has considered how their proposed modifications will impact these customers or what steps should be taken to educate customers prior to the implementation of such changes. PIA Staff is proposing to fundamentally alter the rate through which Georgia Power’s largest number of customers are being served, without due consideration of the impact to those customers. Further, the R rate design is antiquated and sends poor price signals to customers about the cost of the electricity they consume, as compared to the Company’s newer rate designs. Thus, it makes no sense to fundamentally alter the longstanding R tariff while customers are transitioning to more modern rates that more appropriately reflect the cost of electric service for customers.
Q.IS THE COMPANY PROPOSING TO ELIMINATE THE R RATE?
A.No. The Company is not proposing to eliminate the R rate. Under the Company’s proposal, customers currently on the R rate will be able to remain on the rate. Customers currently on the R rate who subsequently move to another existing premises will also be able to remain on the rate. The Company is only proposing to close the R rate for new residential premises that initially establish service with Georgia Power after December 31, 2022. This incremental change in the availability of the R rate is a measured and logical step in Georgia Power’s gradual movement towards more modern rate designs that send price signals that more appropriately reflect the cost of electric service for customers. 
Q. ASIDE FROM THE R RATE, WHAT OTHER RATE OPTIONS ARE AVAILABLE FOR CUSTOMERS AT A NEW PREMISES?
A.If the Company’s proposal is adopted, customers at new residential premises can choose from the Company’s other residential rate options, which include TOU-RD, TOU-PEV, or PrePay. Additionally, once those premises have twelve months of service history, customers can also select the Company’s FlatBill or Pay by Day rates. 
Q.DOES THE COMPANY HAVE ANY FEEDBACK FROM CUSTOMERS REGARDING THEIR EXPERIENCE ON TOU-RD? 
A.Yes. Customer surveys have shown that, as compared to customers on the R rate, TOU-RD customers are more satisfied with their rate and feel more knowledgeable regarding the behaviors that can help them capitalize on their rate plan and reduce their energy bills. These same surveys showed that TOU-RD customers had an overall higher satisfaction score for Georgia Power as compared to R rate customers. 
Q.IS THE TOU-RD RATE INHERENTLY MORE COSTLY FOR CUSTOMERS THAN THE R RATE, AS CLAIMED BY PIA STAFF AND INTERVENORS?
A.No. The TOU-RD rate is not designed to cost customers more than the R rate; rather, the TOU-RD rate was designed to be revenue neutral for the average customer – meaning that the average customer would pay the same annual bill amount on the TOU-RD rate as they would on the R rate. In fact, because TOU-RD provides better price signals to customers, they can make more informed decisions about their energy use and save on their electric bills by shifting usage or staggering the use of key appliances. As a result, customers receive more efficient economic signals regarding their usage and energy choices. 
Q.IS THE BILL COMPARISON NOTED IN PIA STAFF’S TESTIMONY AN APPROPRIATE REPRESENTATION OF THE PRICE IMPACTS OF THE TOU-RD RATE AS COMPARED TO THE R RATE?
A.No. As new premises are added to the Georgia Power system, it can take anywhere from one to two years for the premises’ monthly usage to normalize. This ramp-up period could be due to numerous factors, including the timing of home construction, installation of appliances, or occupancy. Because the bill comparisons that Staff highlight in their testimony represent premises that are new to our system, the average monthly usage for those premises is far below the level to which the usage will ultimately mature (see Figure 1). Therefore, the bill comparisons used by Staff are heavily skewed by extremely low usage and therefore fail to tell a complete story. 
Figure 1

As Figure 1 above demonstrates, a customer’s average monthly usage will increase continually for the first 24 months of existence on Georgia Power’s system. At least one intervening witness commented during their direct testimony that the TOU-RD rate is an effective rate for customers that demonstrate a “good load factor.” By allowing the new premises to mature towards a normal usage level, customers at these new premises will greatly improve their load factors and have a better bill experience when comparing TOU-RD to the R rate.
Q. AS COMPARED TO THE R RATE, WHY ARE THREE-PART RATES – LIKE TOU-RD – MORE COST-REFLECTIVE? 
A.Costs to serve our customers are divided into three components: (1) customer-related costs; (2) demand-related costs; and (3) energy-related costs. To ensure our rates most accurately reflect the cost-of-service, all three cost components should be accounted for within the design of a rate. As such, three-part rates, which include customer, demand, and energy related components, are the most cost-reflective rates because they appropriately account for each of the three cost categories. Conversely, when a rate, such as the R rate, lacks a demand component, the demand-related costs of electric service must be recovered through the rate’s energy component, which does not appropriately match the collection of revenues with how the Company incurs costs. The result is a less efficient price signal that results in less efficient use of the electric system by customers and higher prices overall.
Q.WHY IS THE TOU-RD RATE A BETTER DEFAULT RATE THAN TOU - RESIDENTIAL ENERGY ONLY (“TOU-REO”) AS PROPOSED BY GSEIA, SEIA, AND VOTE SOLAR? 
A.As described above, TOU-RD is a modern, three-part rate that sends price signals that more accurately and appropriately reflect the cost of electric service. In addition, the TOU-RD rate is more stable than the TOU-REO rate from a monthly billing standpoint, meaning that it has a smaller margin between minimum and maximum bills during an annual period. Figure 2 below provides an example of the bill volatility between the R, TOU-REO and TOU-RD rates for the average residential customer and shows that TOU-RD is much less volatile than either the TOU-REO or the R Rate when comparing the difference between minimum and maximum bills over a given year.
Figure 2


Q.WHY IS IT APPROPRIATE TO INCREASE THE BASIC SERVICE CHARGE AS PART OF THE COMPANY’S REQUEST?
A.The increased basic service charge better aligns with the Company’s costs to serve customers. As stated by Mr. Legg during the Company’s direct hearings, Georgia Power has proposed to increase all traditional base rates on an equal percentage basis, including rate elements. This includes the residential basic service charge, which recovers the customer-related costs the Company incurs to serve a customer, regardless of the amount of energy that customer may use. As proposed, the monthly increase to the basic service charge would be approximately $2.27 for a residential customer.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH STAFF’S PROPOSAL TO INCREASE THE INCOME QUALIFIED SENIOR CITIZEN DISCOUNT BY THE AMOUNT OF ANY INCREASE TO THE BASIC SERVICE CHARGE? 
A.Yes.
COMMERCIAL RATES
Q.DO YOU AGREE WITH PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATION THAT CUSTOMERS ON MARGINAL RATES SHOULD BE ASSESSED THE FULL EMBEDDED COST OF THE ENVIRONMENTAL COMPLIANCE COST RECOVERY (“ECCR”) TARIFF?
A.No. Staff’s recommendation is fundamentally inconsistent with how marginal rates like RTP are designed to operate. For RTP as designed, the Company’s fully embedded costs are recovered from the CBL portion of the rate, while marginal costs are recovered from the marginal portion of the rate. The ECCR costs include coal combustion residual asset retirement obligations (“CCR ARO”) that are fully embedded costs incurred by the Company independent of a customer’s kWh usage. As such, they are appropriately recovered through the CBL portion of marginally priced rates. To include fully embedded costs such as ECCR in the marginal portion of the RTP rate would not only undermine the rate structure, but also prevent these rates from actually functioning as marginal rates. The hourly prices would no longer reflect marginal costs or real-time prices.
Q.WHAT WOULD HAPPEN IF ECCR COSTS WERE ALLOCATED TO THE MARGINAL PORTION OF RTP AND OTHER INCREMENTAL RATES?
A.If ECCR costs, or other fully embedded costs, were allocated to the marginal portion of RTP rates, RTP costs would increase by approximately $150 million overall. A cost shift of this magnitude would significantly decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future customers. The RTP rate is a critical rate for large, economic development customers that are considering locating, relocating, or expanding operations in Georgia. PIA Staff’s proposal undermines the principles of marginal rate design by dramatically altering the allocation of ECCR costs within the RTP rate and would have a significant negative impact on the Company’s ability to help attract economic development opportunities to the state.
Q.DOES PIA STAFF’S PROPOSED 50% MOVEMENT OF ECCR IN INCREMENTAL RATES MITIGATE THE IMPACT OF STAFF’S RECOMMENDATION?
A.No. As described above, even an incremental move of 50% would have a dramatic and negative impact on RTP customers by fundamentally changing the rate and undermining the core principles of a marginal rate structure.
Q.DOES GEORGIA POWER AGREE WITH DEPARTMENT OF DEFENSE WITNESS BLANK’S RECOMMENDATION TO MODIFY THE CBL REDUCTION CRITERIA?
A.No. CBLs may be reduced when load originally represented in the CBL has been permanently removed. Mr. Blank proposes to reduce CBLs when customers install generation that runs “at least 6,000 hours” (68%) of the year, even though no load has been removed. For almost one third of the year, the customer’s usage on the system would be the same as it was before the installation of the generation. Generation does not permanently remove load, and therefore does not justify the reduction of the customer’s CBL. 
Second, Mr. Blank criticizes the Company’s requirement that a CBL reduction assessment based on historical load is not permitted unless the customer stops taking service under the RTP tariff for two years. The Company supports the requirement that a customer remain off the RTP rate for two years before returning or requesting a CBL reduction, because short term changes to a customer’s usage should not result in the permanent conversion of embedded load into marginal load on the RTP rate. The RTP tariffs include provisions to revise the CBL as the result of actions like permanent equipment removal and energy efficiency improvements, but the tariffs specifically prohibit CBL revisions due to temporary factors such as economic downturns. Requiring customers who have not removed load to stay off RTP for 24 months in order to set a new CBL preserves the integrity of the RTP program and protects other customers from potential cost shifts that might result from the premature revision of the CBL. 
Q.DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH WITNESS BLANK’S RECOMMENDATION TO ALLOW RTP CUSTOMERS TO ALSO TAKE SERVICE ON THE BACKUP (“BU”) TARIFF?
A.No. The purpose of the BU tariff is to enable customers who are not currently receiving hourly marginal pricing to avoid yearly demand ratcheting, if their generation operates as expected. Customers may receive this benefit or they may benefit from marginal pricing, but not both. 
Q.DOES THIS MEAN RTP CUSTOMERS ARE NOT ABLE TO BENEFIT FROM INSTALLING GENERATION?
A.No. RTP customers that install and use generation receive the benefit of the Company’s marginal price for that hour. This is true when the customer avoids paying the marginal price while operating above their CBL level, or when the customer receives a credit at the marginal price when operating below their CBL level.
Q.DOES THE COMPANY SUPPORT MODIFYING THE TERMS AND CONDITIONS OF THE FIXED PRICE ALTERNATIVE (“FPA”) RATE AND PRICE PROTECTION PRODUCT (“PPP”) OFFERING TO REMOVE COMPANY DISCRETION REGARDING WHEN TO OFFER THESE RATES? 
A.No. The Company considers multiple factors when deciding whether to offer the FPA rate and PPP offerings to customers. These factors include, but are not limited to, uncertainty around environmental regulation, fuel markets, and other potential factors that could impact the pricing of these offerings. Additionally, it is important for the Company to maintain flexibility to act quickly and update PPP offerings to best reflect market conditions, especially during times of fuel market volatility as have been experienced for the last year. As a result, removing the Company’s discretion regarding when to offer these products adds unnecessary risk that could be borne by other customers, since delays in acting during volatile markets could result in the development of product offerings that do not properly reflect pricing impacts. 
Q.DOES GEORGIA POWER SUPPORT MARTA’S REQUEST TO NOT APPLY ANY INCREASES TO THE ELECTRIC TRANSPORTATION (“ET”) RATE?
A. No. Georgia Power does not support MARTA’s request because it would result in a revenue deficiency being spread to other customers. Georgia Power is sympathetic to MARTA’s ridership challenges during the pandemic; however, these challenges are not unique to MARTA and many other businesses have dealt with similar challenges. 
COMMUNITY SOLAR AND CHARGE IT ELECTRIC VEHICLE TARIFFS
Community Solar
Q.WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF THE COMMUNITY SOLAR TARIFF?
A.The Community Solar tariff is a program that offers qualifying customers the opportunity to support renewable energy and the development of solar power in Georgia by subscribing to a portion of the output from local solar facilities. Every month, participating customers pay a fee for a subscription block of the energy produced by Community Solar facilities, and in return receive energy credits on their monthly bill that are based on the production from those facilities. Additionally, the equivalent renewable energy credits (“RECs”) are retired on the customer’s behalf. As noted in this Commission’s 2017 Order approving the program, the Community Solar tariff is not intended to create bill savings for participating customers, but rather, is targeted toward customers who are interested in supporting solar but either do not want or are unable to install solar panels at their premises. The Company currently operates three Community Solar facilities located near Athens, Augusta, and Savannah. 
Q.ARE GUARANTEED BILL SAVINGS PART OF THIS PROGRAM?
A.No. The Company does not guarantee or advertise the Community Solar program as a bill savings opportunity. In some months throughout the year, the credit that participating customers receive on their bill based on the solar facility’s production may exceed the charge to participate, resulting in bill savings. However, as noted above, the purpose of the program is to support renewable energy and the development of solar power in Georgia and not to achieve bill savings.
Q. WHAT DID THE COMMISSION APPROVE IN THE 2022 INTEGRATED RESOURCE PLAN REGARDING THE COMMUNITY SOLAR TARIFF?
A.The Commission approved updates to the Community Solar program to make it more accessible to customers. More specifically, the Commission approved certain changes as proposed by the Company, including the Income Qualified Community Solar Pilot. The Commission did not approve the Company’s requested pricing change “to increase the residential block charge to $27.99 and to set the commercial option at $29.99/month,” electing instead to reserve the pricing decision for consideration in this rate case. 
Q.WHAT ARE THE PROPOSED CHANGES TO THE COMMUNITY SOLAR TARIFF THAT ARE TO BE ADDRESSED IN THIS CASE? 
A.The Company proposes to update the pricing for the existing residential Community Solar Charge from $24.99 to $27.99 per one kilowatt block in order to ensure that participants are not subsidized by non-participants. In addition, following the Commission’s approval to expand the opportunity to participate in Community Solar to customers on the General Service (“GS”) tariff, the Company has proposed that the pricing for GS Community Solar participants be set at $29.99 per one kilowatt block. 
Q.WHY SHOULD THE COMMISSION REJECT PIA STAFF’S AND THE COALITION’S RECOMMENDATIONS TO DECREASE THE PER KILOWATT (“KW”) BLOCK PRICE FOR A COMMUNITY SOLAR SUBSCRIPTION?
A.The Community Solar subscription price is designed to ensure that participating customers do not shift costs to other customers when kilowatt hours reduce the monthly bills of participating customers. As such, the Company has requested an increase in its subscription rates for residential and commercial customers participating in Community Solar. Based on the approved structure of the Community Solar program, a reduction in the price per kW block of energy would leave costs unrecovered and, therefore, shift cost recovery to non-participants. 
In addition, the Coalition bases their reduction on the misguided premise that Community Solar participants pay for rate riders like NCCR, ECCR, and fuel more than once. Customers participating on the Community Solar rate still receive and pay for service on their normal retail electric service rate and are still served from all energy resources generating into or otherwise supplying the Company’s system. When a participating customer pays the subscription rate to participate in the Community Solar program, this is a voluntary premium the customer opts to pay to support the addition of solar resources in Georgia. This subscription affords the participating customer the opportunity to offset their purchase of energy from Georgia Power by the amount of energy produced at the Community Solar facilities. In turn, this energy credit offsets the purchase of delivered energy applied to the customer’s bill, including with respect to the components for ECCR, NCCR, and fuel. Therefore, savings on the riders are achieved when the participating customers’ kWh usage is reduced by the Community Solar production amount.
The Company includes the rate riders as part of the calculation in setting the Community Solar monthly charge because (i) that is consistent with how the R rate works and (ii) the Company must ensure that the riders are not under recovered simply because the participating customer’s bill reflects fewer kWhs as a result of the energy credit applied under the Community Solar program.
Finally, because the Community Solar participant still takes service on their normal retail rate, participation in the Community Solar tariff does not automatically excuse the customer from paying their share of NCCR, ECCR, and fuel.
Q.WHY IS IT INAPPROPRIATE TO LOCK IN A COMMUNITY SOLAR PARTICIPANT’S RATE FOR THE DURATION OF PROGRAM PARTICIPATION AS PROPOSED BY THE COALITION?
A.The Company designed program pricing to prevent cost shifting to non-participating customers. The Company’s costs and revenues are constantly changing. To hold a customer’s cost of participation static for the duration of their participation would lead to inappropriate cost shifting to other participating and non-participating customers. 
Charge Electric Vehicle (“CIEV”) Rider
Q.PLEASE DESCRIBE THE CIEV RIDER. 
A.Georgia Power is supporting the rapidly expanding EV market with a rider that is tailored to provide affordably priced electric service and to encourage the growth of EVs in our state. The CIEV rider will modify how the Power & Light tariffs price commercial EV charging loads. Specifically, the new CIEV rider will modify the monthly billing demands by using Billing Demand Adjustment Factors as specified in the tariff. CIEV is a temporary rider that will expire either at the end of 48 months or when the rolling 12-month load factor for the account reaches 15% or greater – whichever comes first. Participation is available to new, separately metered charging loads that are served on Georgia Power’s system after January 1, 2023. 
Q.WHY IS IT INAPPROPRIATE TO OPEN THE CIEV RIDER TO EXISTING LOCATIONS AS RECOMMENDED BY SEVERAL INTERVENORS?
A.First, the CIEV rider was designed to incentivize the installation of new EV charging stations and opening this rate to existing customers would defeat the purpose of incentivizing new installations. Second, by limiting this tariff to new, separately metered premises, the Company designed the CIEV rider to avoid a cost shift among existing customers. If existing customers were permitted to participate on the CIEV rider, the reduced billing demand allocation in each year would necessarily shift demand costs to other customers. The Company does not support this type of cost shift and supports keeping the CIEV rider limited to new, separately metered premises in order to appropriately incentivize new EV charging installations without doing harm to existing customers. 
INTERCONNECTION APPLICATION FEE 
Q.PLEASE EXPLAIN WHY THE COMPANY PROPOSED AN INTERCONNECTION APPLICATION FEE IN SECTION G OF THE COMPANY’S RULES AND REGULATIONS FOR ELECTRIC SERVICE. 
A.The Company proposed to introduce a one-time $200 interconnection application fee that will be required for all customers seeking to interconnect a customer generating facility to the Georgia Power system. This fee is designed to recover costs associated with supporting the safe and reliable interconnection of a customer generator to the system and will help ensure that such costs are not borne by all other customers. 
Q.WOULD THE COMPANY CONSIDER MAINTAINING THE EXISTING $5/KW WITNESS TESTING FEE AS PROPOSED BY GSEIA, SEIA, AND VOTE SOLAR?
A.No. The current $5/kW witness testing fee was agreed to by settlement in 2017 as part of the application of the RCB Framework to behind the meter solar. As settled to, this fee was not implemented to fully recover the Company’s cost to witness test new customer generators smaller than 250 kW let alone the administrative costs to review, process, and update the customer’s account for the installation of generation at its premises. In contrast, the Company’s proposed $200 one-time fee is designed to cover the application, administrative costs, impact study fees and witness testing fees (where required) for new customer generation. The cost to provide these services does not vary significantly based on the size of the facility and the Company believes a flat, one-time fee is more appropriate than the outdated $5/kW witness testing fee. 
HOURLY INTERVAL DATA
Q.DID THE COMPANY COMPLY WITH THE COMMISSION’S DIRECTION TO EVALUATE CUSTOMER ACCESS TO HOURLY INTERVAL DATA FOLLOWING THE 2019 RATE CASE?
A.Yes. Consistent with the Commission’s direction following the 2019 rate case, Georgia Power worked with Staff to develop options for customer access to usage data and then submitted recommendations to the Commission for approval. In addition, the cost to provide additional options for access to customer usage data was directed to be deferred as a regulatory asset and recovered in a future base rate proceeding, which the Company has requested for recovery in this case.
Q.IS GEORGIA POWER REQUIRED TO PROVIDE HOURLY USAGE DATA TO ALL CUSTOMERS FREE OF CHARGE AS ASSERTED BY THE COALITION?
A.No. 
Q.DO THE VAST MAJORITY OF CUSTOMERS HAVE ACCESS TO THEIR HOURLY USAGE DATA?
A.Yes. The Company made hourly usage data available to customers via My Power Usage (“MPU”) in January 2022. This was the result of the hourly usage project approved by the Commission. A limited group of customers are unable to use MPU; these include customers who opt-out of an AMI meter and certain unmetered accounts. Special Service Account and RTP customers are also not eligible for daily or hourly usage via MPU because they are on more complex rates and not on AMI meters, but these customers can access hourly interval usage data, along with additional features and options, by subscribing to the Company’s Energy Direct website. 
Q.ARE ADDITIONAL CHANGES NEEDED TO THE PROVISION OF HOURLY INTERVAL USAGE DATA?
A.No.
PROPOSED CHANGES TO THE COSS
Q. DO YOU BELIEVE THAT OTHER ALLOCATION METHODOLOGIES IN ADDITION TO THE COMPANY’S FILED 12-CP METHODOLOGY SHOULD BE CONSIDERED BY THIS COMMISSION FOR THE ALLOCATION OF THE CAPACITY COMPONENT OF THE PRODUCTION FUNCTION?
A.No. When preparing for a rate case filing, Georgia Power considers other allocation methodologies in addition to its historical preference of 12-CP. For this rate case, the Company determined that 12-CP remains the most accurate and appropriate methodology. As stated in Mr. Evans’ direct testimony, the 12-CP methodology incorporates the fact that Georgia Power’s system is planned and operated for the purpose of serving load requests every month of the year. Additionally, PIA Staff’s proposed methodologies not only impact the allocation of costs in base rate cases, but also may impact the allocation of fuel costs in the Fuel Cost Recovery docket. The trade-off between the level of Production fixed investment and the variable cost of producing a unit of output is one primarily made between base capital costs and fuel costs. Altering the allocation of Production capital costs with such a complex calculation would necessitate a similar alteration to the allocation in the Fuel Cost Recovery docket. Recognizing the current, approved allocation of fuel costs in the Fuel Cost Recovery docket, the Company continues to support the 12-CP methodology as the most appropriate Production allocator. It has been employed by Georgia Power for allocating production capacity-related costs since 1989, and as such, has therefore influenced present rates. 
Q.ARE PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATIONS CONSISTENT WITH THEIR 2019 RECOMMENDATIONS TO CHANGE HOW THE COMPANY ALLOCATES GENERATION PLANT PRODUCTION COSTS?
A.No. Staff is recommending energy-based allocation methods in this case, which are substantially different from the “up to” 8-CP method proposed by PIA Staff in the last case. If PIA Staff’s recommendation had been adopted in the last case, it would be wholly inappropriate to pivot to the other end of the spectrum so drastically in this case. Adopting PIA Staff’s vacillating recommendations in the 2013 and 2019 cases, and now this current rate case, would have resulted in great swings in parity results from case-to-case. To depart from using 12-CP as an allocator for Production cost, especially without compelling evidence to do so, could cause significant and unexpected disruptions to customer bills. 
Q.WOULD THE COMPANY CONSIDER MOVING TO A 4-CP OR SUMMER PEAK PRODUCTION COST ALLOCATION METHOD AS RECOMMENDED BY INTERVENORS?
A.No. The generation planning for Georgia Power’s system is driven and influenced by non-summer periods in addition to summer peaks. Georgia Power utilizes Expected Unserved Energy (“EUE”) in its determination of the optimal reserve margin for both Summer and Winter seasons. A pure summer peak or 4-CP allocator is an oversimplification of the Company’s generation planning process. If one were to use a pure summer peak allocator, this could create unstable, unbalanced, and inequitable cost allocation in which some rate groups receive little if any Production demand cost allocation whatsoever. An example of this could be if the pure summer peak allocator for that year occurred in daytime hours such that there was no subsequent cost allocation to outdoor lighting. The ongoing capacity needs of Georgia Power are heavily driven by winter needs and to not recognize or allocate costs based on winter loads would, therefore, be inappropriate.
Q.WHY IS THE 12-CP METHOD THE MOST APPROPRIATE WAY TO ALLOCATE THE COMPANY’S PRODUCTION COSTS?
A.A 12-CP demand is defined as the sum of the highest kW load for the Company in each month of the 12-month period divided by twelve. At the jurisdictional, class, or rate group level, 12-CP is the respective coincident demands at the time of the monthly system peaks. Using this allocator ensures that the coincident load burden placed on the system by a jurisdiction, class, or rate group is allocated an appropriate share of the costs of the system. 
The Company’s adoption of seasonal planning further reinforces the appropriateness of the 12-CP approach. The Company must plan to have sufficient generation to serve its customers in all twelve months of the year and the 12-CP method appropriately captures the summer and winter peaks targeted through seasonal planning. The summer and winter reliability planning of the Company further underscores that no one month or season is adequate at representing the coincident impact of customers on system capacity. Georgia Power forecasts to remain a summer peaking utility. While the IRP identified winter reliability concerns, the Company’s rate designs remain appropriate to address the forecasted peak loads and higher marginal costs that occur in summer months.
In addition, as stated in Mr. Evans’ Direct Testimony, the 12-CP allocation methodology has been used in every COSS study filed by the Company with this Commission since 1989. Further, the 12-CP method has been one of FERC’s preferred allocation techniques for determining wholesale jurisdictional obligations, and it has been used by the Company in its filings before FERC. It has proven to be a common and popular allocation technique employed in numerous retail filings before many state commissions throughout the United States. It is a straightforward methodology, has a strong cost causative relationship, and has a history of providing stable and sound results. 
PARITY ADJUSTMENTS 
Q.DOES THE COMPANY SUPPORT MOVEMENTS TOWARD PARITY AMONG RATE GROUPS? 
A.Yes. The Company has made movements toward parity in previous rate cases based on PIA Staff’s recommendation. In fact, since 2010, all of Georgia Power’s rate groups have moved toward parity or maintained relative parity. While the Company supports movement towards parity, the Company does not support the parity adjustments outlined in the testimony of Mr. Pollock on behalf of GAM or the testimony of the Barber, Deitchman, Watkins panel for PIA Staff. Any adjustments toward parity should be based upon the COSS presented in Exhibit LPE-1 filed by the Company in the rate case filing. The precision of a COSS and the accuracy of the results are greatly impacted by the number of customers and the amount of load in each rate. The smaller the number of customers and the smaller the amount of load in a particular rate, the lesser the potential accuracy of the results associated with that rate. Some rates have very limited participation and may produce volatile rates of return and parity results in cost-of-service studies for different time periods. With this in mind, the Commission should consider the cost-of-service to the eight rate groups instead of by individual rates. 
CONCLUSION 
Q.DOES THIS CONCLUDE YOUR TESTIMONY?
A.Yes.

 1                      II. FINANCIAL INTEGRITY AND ACCESS TO CAPITAL

 2   Q.      WOULD ADOPTION OF PIA STAFF AND INTERVENOR’S COST OF
 3           CAPITAL POSITIONS UNDO THIS COMMISSION’S HISTORY OF
 4           BALANCED, CONSISTENT AND PREDICTABLE REGULATION AND
 5           HARM THE COMPANY’S FINANCIAL INTEGRITY?

 6   A.      Yes.

 7   Q.      PLEASE EXPLAIN THE IMPORTANCE OF CONSISTENT AND
 8           PREDICTABLE REGULATION WITHIN THE CONTEXT OF INVESTOR
 9           AND CREDIT PERCEPTIONS AND THE FINANCIAL INTEGRITY OF
10           THE COMPANY.

11   A.      As Mr. Fetter discusses, regulation is a key factor in assessing the future credit risk
12           profile of a utility because, among other things, a state public utility commission
13           determines the nature, amount, and timing of recovery of a utility’s cost of service
14           to its customers. Accordingly, the constructive practices and policies of regulatory
15           commissions play a critical role in the overall credit risk profiles of regulated
16           utilities and, ultimately, investors’ decisions. As such, it is imperative that state
17           utility commissions understand the financial, economic, and operational elements
18           of an evolving industry, and engage in a fair decision-making process that
19           demonstrates predictability and consistency. As Mr. Fetter has testified, these
20           qualitative factors can represent as much as half of the Company’s overall credit
21           rating.

22           For these reasons, rating agencies look for consistent application of sound
23           economic regulatory principles by utility regulators. If a regulatory body allowed a
24           utility the opportunity to earn a fair return on utility assets and subsequently applied
25           cost recovery principles in a manner inconsistent with the regulatory compact,
26           investors’ interest in providing funds to that utility would be altered, the utility’s


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                       Page 5 of 61

 1             risk profile would deteriorate, and the utility’s access to capital at reasonable terms
 2             would suffer – all to the detriment of customers.

 3   Q.         HOW DO CUSTOMERS BENEFIT WHEN THE COMPANY BALANCES
 4              THE INTERESTS OF ALL STAKEHOLDERS?

 5   A.         Customers benefit when the Company balances the interests of all stakeholders
 6              because a financially healthy utility can meet the needs of its customers and is more
 7              likely to achieve high levels of reliability and customer satisfaction. Customers also
 8              benefit from the Company’s operating efficiencies in its efforts to meet investor
 9              expectations. The efficiencies gained and the corresponding cost reductions that
10              result from those efforts are provided to customers through savings in rates.

11   Q.         WHAT ELEMENTS OF PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATIONS WOULD
