26           Mr. Gorman omitted the important debt imputation methodology S&P has directed

          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                       Page 9 of 61

 1           in previously issued authoritative publications. These debt imputations are essential
 2           adjustments that must be made to arrive at a properly recalculated S&P credit
 3           metric.

 4   Q.      WHAT ARE DEBT IMPUTATIONS AND WHY DO RATING AGENCIES
 5           REQUIRE THEM FOR PURPOSES OF CALCULATING THE CREDIT
 6           METRIC “FFO TO DEBT”?

 7   A.      Debt imputation is the act of adding debt-like fixed obligations to a utility’s
 8           “traditional” debt balance when calculating FFO to debt. For many years, S&P has
 9           viewed certain components on the balance sheet, including pension obligations,
10           lease liabilities, and asset retirement obligations as similar to debt and therefore
11           adds them to the debt balance when calculating credit metrics.

12   Q.      HOW DOES S&P OBTAIN THE INFORMATION TO PERFORM SUCH
13           CALCULATIONS?

14   A.      S&P uses the information included on the Company’s publicly available balance
15           sheet and accompanying financial disclosures to obtain pertinent information for
16           the proper debt imputation calculation. Georgia Power has no off-balance sheet
17           debt, or any other off-balance sheet components, that the rating agencies use when
18           calculating adjusted debt.

19   Q.      DID MR. GORMAN INCLUDE THIS DEBT IMPUTATION IN HIS FFO TO
20           DEBT CREDIT METRIC CALCULATION?

21   A.      No. In his recalculation of S&P’s credit metric, FFO to debt, Mr. Gorman omitted
22           the debt imputation methodology directed by S&P in previously issued
23           authoritative publications. These debt imputations are essential adjustments that
24           must be made to recalculate S&P’s credit metric.

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 10 of 61

 1      In response to the question posed by PIA Staff and Mr. Gorman in data request
 2      STF-BAI-1-17, the Company responded as follows:

 3               Question:

 4               Please state whether GPC’s regulated retail operations have any
 5               off-balance sheet debt such as purchased power agreements and
 6               operating leases. If the answer is “yes,” provide the amount of each
 7               off-balance sheet debt item and estimate the related imputed interest
 8               and amortization expense associated with these off-balance sheet
 9               debt equivalents specific to GPC’s jurisdictional regulated retail
10               electric operations.

11               Response:

12               No. Georgia Power does not have any off-balance sheet debt. In
13               2019, Georgia Power adopted the provisions of Financial
14               Accounting Standards Board (FASB) Accounting Standards
15               Codification (“ASC”) Topic 842 (as amended), Leases (ASC 842),
16               which requires lessees to recognize leases with a term of greater than
17               12 months on the balance sheet as lease obligations. With the
18               adoption of the standard, the operating leases historically
19               representing off-balance sheet financing arrangements are now
20               reflected on Georgia Power’s balance sheet.

21      In responding to this question, the Company correctly answered that it does not
22      have any off-balance sheet debt. The Company also clarified that obligations such
23      as purchased power agreements and operating leases are now found on the
24      Company’s balance sheet. Mr. Gorman and the Company agree that these debt-like
25      obligations are the type of debt imputations that are appropriate to include in
26      estimating S&P’s FFO to debt calculation. However, due to the confusion around
27      where these debt-like components are identified, these components were omitted
28      from Mr. Gorman’s calculations. Mr. Gorman should have included the debt
29      imputations related to these on-balance sheet debt-like components in his estimated
30      recalculation of S&P credit metrics, as he did in the 2019 rate case.

 1           III.     MODIFICATIONS TO TRADITIONAL ALTERNATE RATE PLAN

 2   Q.      ARE PIA STAFF’S PROPOSED CHANGES TO THE COMPANY’S ARP
 3           CONSISTENT               WITH         THE         CONSTRUCTIVE                 REGULATORY
 4           ENVIRONMENT TYPICAL OF PRIOR ARPS APPROVED BY THIS
 5           COMMISSION?

 6   A.      No. PIA Staff’s collective recommendations radically depart from the previously
 7           accepted ARP structure and have the effect of removing the benefits to customers
 8           resulting from the Company agreeing to enter an ARP in the first place. Three-year
 9           ARPs avoid the potential for more frequent base rate cases, provide rate stability
10           and predictability to customers, provide for customer revenue sharing for earnings
11           above the approved earnings band, encourage the Company to operate efficiently,
12           and signal the type of regulatory consistency viewed favorably by credit rating
13           agencies.

14   Q.      IN WHAT WAY DOES THE COMPANY ASSUME RISK DURING THE
15           THREE-YEAR ACCOUNTING PERIOD?

16   A.      Because the Company surrenders its right to file a full rate case during the three-
17           year term when the ROE is within the authorized earnings band, the Company
18           assumes the risks of changing conditions during the term of the ARP. For example,
19           the level of inflation may increase more than projected, the cost of debt or equity
20           might increase, sales may be less than forecasted, or the Company’s overall cost of
21           service may be more than forecasted. A three-year rate plan holds the Company
22           accountable for inefficiencies or cost overruns due to rates being set at a
23           predetermined level for the duration of the three-year period.


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 15 of 61

 1   Q.      DOES          THE         COMPANY              AGREE            WITH          PIA       STAFF’S
 2           RECOMMENDATION                    TO      USE      THE      TEST       PERIOD REVENUE
 3           REQUIREMENT FOR PURPOSES OF ESTABLISHING THE FIRST
 4           YEAR REVENUE REQUIREMENT OF AN ARP?

 5   A.      No. The revenue deficiency calculated for the calendar year ending December 31,
 6           2023, rather than the deficiency for the split test year, reflects a more accurate
 7           depiction of the costs the Company will incur in its first year under a new ARP
 8           when the new rates are effective. Determining the revenue deficiency using the
 9           calendar year reduces regulatory lag. In the Company’s Errata Filing, the projected
10           revenue deficiency based on the traditional test period and the 2023 calendar year
11           is $459.8 million and $612.3 million, respectively, resulting in a difference of
12           $152.5 million. This difference illustrates that basing the year one rate increase on
13           the split test period’s revenue deficiency would create a revenue shortfall of $152.5
14           million in the very first year of the three-year period. Therefore, setting rates based
15           on the test period would not permit the Company to timely recover its prudently
16           incurred costs in serving customers.

17   Q.      DOES          THE         COMPANY              AGREE            WITH          PIA       STAFF’S
18           RECOMMENDATION                     TO      DEFER        AND       USE       ANY       REVENUE
19           SUFFICIENCY BASED ON PIA STAFF’S CALCULATED TEST YEAR
20           AND        2023      REVENUE            REQUIREMENT                FOR       ACCELERATED
21           RECOGNITION OF DEFERRED COSTS?

22   A.      No. Based on the Company’s proposal, the Company does not have a projected
23           revenue sufficiency in 2023 to apply to the deferred costs. As stated previously, the
24           Company’s revenue deficiency should be based on the calendar year 2023 revenue
25           requirement rather than the test period, if the Company’s ARP is adopted. However,
26           the Company generally agrees with PIA Staff’s concept of applying revenue
27           sufficiency in a rate case, in the event there is any, to certain regulatory assets to
28           maintain rate stability for the customers.

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 16 of 61

 1   Q.      WHY IS A LEVELIZED APPROACH MORE APPROPRIATE THAN THE
 2           STEP INCREASE APPROACH RECOMMENDED BY PIA STAFF?

 3   A.      The levelized approach results in $43 million less being billed to customers than a
 4           step increase approach over the three-year ARP. It also balances the needs of both
 5           customers and the Company, allowing customers to benefit from three years of
 6           stable and predictable rates while allowing the Company to recover its projected
 7           revenue deficiency. Rate stability provides customers with a beneficial tool when
 8           budgeting for their utility costs and provides a predictable revenue stream for the
 9           Company.

10           The step increase approach would increase customer rates by an increasing amount
11           during each of the next three years. Although an annual increase would result in a
12           lower than requested projected revenue requirement for 2023 and 2024, the increase
13           for 2025 would be greater than the levelized amount, and rates would increase more
14           overall during the ARP. In addition, the fluctuation in projected costs year-to-year
15           creates uncertainty for customers. Although the Company shares PIA Staff’s
16           interest in rate stability, the Company maintains that levelizing the proposed
17           increases over the three-year ARP is the more appropriate approach for customers.

18   Q.      ARE CUSTOMERS FAIRLY COMPENSATED FOR THE PROJECTED
19           OVER-COLLECTION                   IN     THE      FIRST        TWO       YEARS         OF     THE
20           LEVELIZATION?

21   A.      Yes. Based on the Company’s projections under the levelized revenue requirement,
22           the Company would over-collect in 2023 and 2024 and under-collect in 2025. For
23           the projected advancement of revenues in 2023 and 2024, the Company proposes
24           to defer the amount as a regulatory liability, reducing retail rate base and giving
25           customers credit on the advanced amount based on the Company’s full weighted
26           average cost of capital (“WACC”). This proposed deferral reduces the levelized


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 17 of 61

 1           revenue requirement requested by the Company and fully amortizes the
 2           advancement by the end of 2025.

 3   Q.      DO YOU AGREE WITH PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATION THAT 100%
 4           OF EARNINGS ABOVE THE TOP END OF THE EARNINGS BAND
 5           SHOULD BE USED TO ACCELERATE DEFERRED COSTS?

 6   A.      No. PIA Staff’s proposal amounts to an earnings cap by eliminating any retained
 7           revenues earned above the earnings band. This removes any incentive for the
 8           Company to exceed the top end of the band and pr does not encourage the Company
 9           to maximize efficiencies during the ARP period. In addition, the top end of the
10           earnings band proposed by PIA Staff is set artificially low, at a level below even
11           the Company’s cost of equity.

12   Q.      HAS THE EARNINGS SHARING MECHANISM WORKED WELL FOR
13           THE COMPANY AND ITS CUSTOMERS?

14   A.      Yes. As presented in the table on page 130 of the testimony of PIA Staff Witnesses
15           Smith and Trokey, and as stated in our direct testimony, the Company returned
16           approximately $297 million in benefits to customers as a result of the sharing
17           arrangements approved by the Commission in the 2013 and 2019 base rate cases.
18           As the table illustrates, this represents an average of approximately 78% of the
19           earnings above the top of the band being applied to the benefit of customers and
20           22% of the earnings being retained by the Company since 2014. These benefits
21           have been achieved without compromising superior customer satisfaction or
22           reliability. Notably, the amount customers pay through rates does not increase or
23           decrease when the Company’s earnings are within the band, because rates are set
24           on the ROE set point. As previously mentioned, the sharing mechanism is a critical
25           component of the ARP and appropriately encourages the Company to look for
26           efficiencies and process improvements, which can lead to higher earnings that
27           benefit both customers and the Company.

14           lower than the 12% proposed by the Company. Otherwise, Mr. Blank reiterates his

17           increase risks and uncertainty to customers. Second, Mr. Blank also recommends
18           reducing the Company’s portion of any shared earnings above the top of the band,

 6                                     V. ELECTRIC TRANSPORTATION

 7   Q.      WHAT IS GEORGIA POWER’S ROLE IN SUPPORTING THE
 8           DEVELOPMENT OF GEORGIA’S ELECTRIC TRANSPORTATION
 9           (“ET”) ECOSYSTEM?

10   A.      Georgia Power must be prepared to meet the energy needs of all customers within
11           its service territory. Therefore, we continue to make appropriate investments in the
12           infrastructure and technology needed to support the growth of electric
13           transportation in Georgia that is being driven by customer interest and market
14           demand. These investments include the buildout and upgrade of infrastructure from
15           grid to charger that will enable charging across the state in public, at work, and at
16           home. In limited circumstances, Georgia Power will continue to offer EV charging
17           services as a provider of last resort in underserved areas through the Community
18           Charging Program. As such, Georgia Power will continue to support our state’s
19           developing ET ecosystem, which continues to experience economic growth as
20           evidenced by two major EV manufacturing announcements this year.

21   Q.      WHAT IS THE MAKE READY PROGRAM?

22   A.      Georgia Power’s Make Ready program helps to remove the biggest financial hurdle
23           of installing electric chargers at a qualifying customer’s location by installing,
24           owning, and maintaining the electrical infrastructure behind the meter up to the
25           charger, which typically includes the panel, switchgear, wiring, conduits, and


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 21 of 61

 1           disconnects. The customer is then responsible for the procurement, installation,
 2           maintenance, and energy to serve the EV charging equipment.

 3   Q.      WHAT IS GEORGIA POWER’S REQUEST REGARDING THE MAKE
 4           READY PROGRAM IN THIS CASE?

 5   A.      The Company has requested to invest $27 million per year for each year of the ARP
 6           (2023-2025) for infrastructure upgrades in the Make Ready program. As stated in
 7           the Company’s response to STF-LA-6-19, these capital expenditures include wires,
 8           panels, conduit, transformers, and labor.

 9   Q.      PLEASE DESCRIBE GEORGIA POWER’S COMMUNITY CHARGING
10           PROGRAM.

11   A.      Georgia Power’s Community Charging Program expands the growth of electric
12           vehicle direct current (“DC”) fast charging locations by partnering with businesses
13           to host an EV fast charging site where the charging will be made available to the
14           public. Georgia Power installs, operates, and maintains the chargers in this
15           program, which is designed to partner with customers to help grow critical public
16           EV infrastructure across Georgia. The Company primarily operates this program as
17           a provider of last resort to provide public DC fast charging options for EV drivers
18           in areas that lack investment in public DC fast charging. In the 2019 Rate Case, the
19           Commission approved $2 million per year (2020-2022) in spending for the
20           Community Charging program. The Company’s request in this case for $3 million
21           per year for each year of the ARP (2023-2025) is intended to maintain the
22           development and deployment of the Community Charging program. The increase
23           in funding requested reflects inflation and the increased cost of labor and
24           equipment.

 1   Q.      WILL CONTINUED INVESTMENT IN ET INFRASTRUCTURE AT THE

 4   A.      Yes. The Company’s Rate Impact Measure (“RIM”) analysis of the Make Ready

 7           program enables greater adoption of these technologies by providing public

11                                         VI.      O&M ADJUSTMENTS

12   Q.      PLEASE SUMMARIZE MR. MUGRACE’S RECOMMENDED O&M
13           ADJUSTMENTS.

14   A.      Mr. Mugrace recommends reducing the Company’s projected O&M expenses by
15           $192 million, $147 million, $137 million, and $169 million in the test year and
16           2023, 2024, and 2025 rate years, respectively. Mr. Mugrace recommends the
17           Company adjust its projected functional O&M expenses for certain FERC accounts
18           based upon prior historical levels of O&M expenses. Mr. Mugrace also
19           recommends removing certain expenses historically allowed by the Commission
20           from the retail cost of service, such as certain employee labor costs, employee
21           retention costs, Innovation Center costs, and miscellaneous general expenses.
22           Furthermore, Mr. Mugrace recommends the Company reduce its labor expenses
23           based on vacancy rate ratio.


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 23 of 61

 1   Q.      WHY IS A SIMPLE AVERAGE OF HISTORICAL COSTS NOT ALWAYS
 2           A GOOD DETERMINANT OF PROJECTED COSTS?

 3   A.      The Company’s O&M expenses are complex and driven by many factors such as
 4           timing and scope of outages, scheduled maintenance cycles, repair work, medical
 5           claims, labor market, and contractor rates. These conditions in the historical period
 6           are not identical to the test period and the Company and its business units consider
 7           all these factors in the development of their respective budgets for the upcoming
 8           three-year period. In addition, the historical years used by Mr. Mugrace includes
 9           years 2020 and 2021, which were greatly impacted by the COVID pandemic and
10           are not representative of a “normal” year that should be relied upon as reflective of
11           costs to be incurred in the forecasted periods.

12   Q.      PLEASE DESCRIBE THE COMPANY’S O&M BUDGET PROCESS?

13   A.      Annually, the Company utilizes input from all areas of the business to plan and
14           budget the resources needed in support of expected workload for the upcoming
15           five-year forecasted period. The Company considers planned outage schedules,
16           expected maintenance activities, asset and vegetation management programs,
17           customer service needs, technology, fleet, materials, staffing levels, contractor
18           rates, and expected inflation when developing the budget. Budget assumptions are
19           vetted and approved to ensure consistency across business units.

20           During this process, Georgia Power reviews expenses and adjusts budgeted
21           amounts based on known increases or decreases, accordingly. The Company does
22           not create its budget by simply applying an inflation factor to all the actual, or
23           historic year expenses. Based on the type of expenditure, inflation rates may be
24           applied to derive projected expenses.


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 24 of 61

 1   Q.      DOES THE COMPANY’S O&M REMAIN STATIC OVER TIME?

 2   A.      No. The Company has seen significant price increases in nearly all areas of its
 3           business. For example, transformer prices have increased over 48% since 2020,
 4           wire and cable costs increased 33%, pole line hardware is up 27%, and pole costs
 5           have increased 26%. Despite cost increases, budgets may fluctuate over a given
 6           period of time due to multiple factors, including unit outages, changes in workforce
 7           needs for both Company labor and contract labor, Company benefit costs, and
 8           Commission-approved items such as Storm Recovery and demand side
 9           management (“DSM”). These are only a few of the items that make up the
10           Company’s O&M request. The Company is constantly focused on managing its
11           O&M expenditures, emphasizing cost containment as part of its corporate culture,
12           as reasonable and necessary cost containment is in the best interest of both the
13           Company and its customers. The Company has identified efficiencies in nearly
14           every part of its business while still providing superior customer satisfaction and
15           reliable service to customers.

16   Q.      AS AN EXAMPLE OF WHY SIMPLY ESCALATING HISTORIC COSTS
17           IS INAPPROPRIATE, WHAT IS DRIVING THE INCREASE IN O&M
18           EXPENSES IN CUSTOMER SERVICE AND INFORMATION FOR THE
19           TEST PERIOD AND YEARS 2023 THROUGH 2025?

20   A.      The primary driver for the increase in Customer Service and Information expenses
21           is related to the increase in DSM expenses as a result of the DSM programs
22           approved by the Commission in the 2022 DSM Certification in Docket No. 44161.
23           Because the projected level of DSM expenses are directly attributed to the DSM
24           programs approved by the Commission, historical costs are clearly not a good
25           indicator of the projected expenses for the ARP years reflected in the filing.

 9   Q.      PLEASE DESCRIBE HOW THE COMPANY CALCULATED THE
10           INCREMENTAL                INFLATION            ASSOCIATED              WITH        ITS      O&M
11           EXPENSES?

12   A.      The Company began to see significant price increases across all aspects of its
13           business consistent with the rising inflation factors found in the Employment Cost
14           Index (“ECI”) and Gross Domestic Product (“GDP”) deflator. In response to these
15           signals, the Company adjusted its projected O&M expenses to account for these
16           cost increases in this filing by identifying labor and non-labor costs that typically
17           would fluctuate based on inflation factors and applying the incremental increase in
18           ECI and GDP inflation rates to the appropriate cost types. The adjustments were
19           recorded at a summary account level across several FERC accounts as provided in
20           STF-PCMG-1-5. Items such as benefits, incentive pay, insurance, and parity were
21           excluded from the adjustment.

22   Q.      WHY         IS     STAFF’S          COMPARISON               AND        THE        RESULTING
23           RECOMMENDED ADJUSTMENT OF INCREMENTAL INFLATION
24           INAPPROPRIATE?

25   A.      Although Mr. Mugrace states that the ECI is an appropriate source of inflation rates
26           for labor costs, he ultimately used only the lower GDP index to escalate total non-

          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 28 of 61

 1           Production O&M expenses, including labor and non-labor costs, to calculate his
 2           incremental inflation adjustment. In order to appropriately calculate the adjustment,
 3           the O&M expenses need to be separated into labor and non-labor charges and the
 4           ECI and GDP rates need to be applied to the respective costs, as the Company did
 5           in performing its calculation of the incremental inflation adjustment. In addition,
 6           Mr. Mugrace did not carry forward the incremental inflation adjustment calculated
 7           in Year 1 to the subsequent years, which caused the impact in future years to be
 8           understated. Since the escalation indexes are increases from the prior year, any
 9           incremental inflation adjustments from the prior year need to be accounted for in
10           the calculation of incremental inflation in a given year.

11   Q.      PLEASE DESCRIBE THE COMPANY’S BUDGETED HEADCOUNT IN
12           THE FORECASTED PERIODS?

13   A.      The Company budgeted its headcount and total labor costs, including internal and

16   Q.      IF THE COMPANY IS UNABLE TO HIRE THE FULL NUMBER OF
17           EMPLOYEES BUDGETED TO PERFORM ITS OPERATIONS, HOW
18           DOES THE COMPANY CONTINUE TO MANAGE ITS BUSINESS?

19   A.      The Company fills open positions with overtime labor from the existing workforce
20           or hires additional contract labor to perform the necessary work if it is unable to
21           hire the full number of employees budgeted. In this example, while internal labor
22           costs decrease, overtime and contract labor increase to offset the reduction.

21   A.      To provide its award-winning service to customers, Georgia Power must attract and
22           retain highly skilled employees. Therefore, Georgia Power’s employee
23           compensation program is designed to provide competitive compensation at levels
24           targeting the 50th percentile of the appropriate markets. The Company’s well-
25           balanced and market competitive compensation program provides different types
26           of compensation that are commonly known and recognized by compensation

 1           professionals to be market appropriate to retain and attract talent, which includes

24           components of the employee compensation program which includes the long-term

27           to commensurately increase other components of its total compensation package –

 1      such as direct salary costs – to maintain its competitiveness as an employer within

 4      PIA Staff’s proposed disallowance of these portions of the employee compensation

11      The budgeted costs to attract, retain, and engage employees are expenses that arise
12      in the ordinary course of the Company’s business and should be included in the
13      Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail rates.

14      Further, the payroll tax expenses associated with stock-based compensation are also
15      a prudently incurred cost of doing business and, therefore, should be recovered
16      from customers. However, Messrs. Smith and Trokey applied both the Federal

18      stock-based compensation adjustment when calculating their payroll tax expense
19      adjustment to be excluded from retail cost of service. As they noted on schedule E-
20      5 in PIA Staff’s Exhibit_(RS-RT-2), it is likely that employees who are eligible for
21      the Company’s stock-based compensation programs would have annual salaries
22      above the projected FICA threshold. Therefore, it is appropriate to apply only the
23      Medicare portion of payroll tax to the stock-based compensation expense when
24      calculating the applicable payroll tax expense adjustment.

     Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                     on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                               Docket No. 44280
                                                 Page 33 of 61

 1   Q.      WHY DOES THE COMPANY PROVIDE LONG-TERM INCENTIVE
 2           BASED COMPENSATION?

 3   A.      The long-term incentive based at-risk compensation program incentivizes
 4           employees to make decisions that are in the long-term best interest of customers
 5           and the Company. Customers benefit from a financially healthy company, which
 6           should be measured over the short- and long-term to ensure that the decisions made
 7           by the Company are optimized for both short- and long-term benefits. This is
 8           especially true in the utility industry, where decisions related to infrastructure and
 9           other major capital projects may have long-lasting financial consequences to all
10           stakeholders including customers. Customers would be harmed if the Company
11           were to drive its employees to sacrifice long-term financial health for short-term
12           benefits. By designing the at-risk portion of the total compensation plan to include
13           longer-term goals, the Company achieves an appropriate incentive balance that
14           motivates employees to deliver safe, clean, and reliable electric service to our
15           customers in a manner that is economically efficient both now and in the years that
16           follow.

17   Q.      WHY IS IT IMPORTANT THAT AN INCENTIVE COMPENSATION
18           PLAN INCLUDE BOTH OPERATIONAL AND FINANCIAL GOALS?

19           Georgia Power seeks to create a balanced incentive system to ensure that employees
20           are providing the best service to customers at the least cost. Evaluating
21           compensation as a whole allows Georgia Power to maximize the effective use of
22           resources essential to efficiently serving its customers by linking a portion of the
23           employees’ compensation package to operational and financial performance and
24           allows for the alignment of the compensation programs with Georgia Power’s need
25           to attract, engage, retain, and motivate its highly skilled workforce. A well-designed
26           total compensation program using sound compensation practices and principles
27           provides a balance between operational focus and financial focus for both the short
28           term and longer term to drive employee behavior in ways that balance the interests

          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 34 of 61

 1           of customers and shareholders alike. A compensation plan that contains only
 2           operational goals might inappropriately drive employees to use more financial
 3           resources than necessary to achieve operational success, while a plan that contains
 4           only financial goals might inappropriately drive employees to make decisions that
 5           adversely impact operational success. A balanced program of incentive
 6           compensation is a powerful tool that drives employees to put the customer at the
 7           center of all we do while sustaining the financial integrity of the Company.

 8   Q.      HOW DO THE ELEMENTS OF GEORGIA POWER’S COMPENSATION
 9           PROGRAM THAT ARE BASED ON FINANCIAL OBJECTIVES PROVIDE
10           BENEFITS TO CUSTOMERS?

11   A.      Goals based on financial metrics – when included in an incentive compensation
12           plan designed to balance financial and operational goals– provide benefits to
13           customers by reducing expenses, creating greater efficiencies in operations,
14           ensuring a financially strong company, and allowing access to the capital markets
15           at a low cost.

16   Q.      WHAT          MIGHT         GEORGIA            POWER          AND        ITS      CUSTOMERS
17           EXPERIENCE IF THE COMPANY IS UNABLE TO RECOVER THESE
18           COSTS?

19   A.      Disallowing recovery of a financial component for a level of standard market pay
20           forces a utility to consider modifying the entire incentive design to be based on
21           operational measures alone (which are recoverable) and eliminate financial
22           components of the incentive program design. However, making this change to an
23           operational-based incentive program design would not be in the best interests of
24           customers, as it would encourage employees to focus solely on operational
25           performance with no balance or accountability to cost (causing employees to
26           improve operational performance and customer satisfaction regardless of the
27           financial cost to do so). Georgia Power strongly believes this imbalance is not in

          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 35 of 61

 1           the best interests of customers, and also believes this will lead to higher rate
 2           increases for customers in the future with little to no benefit or improvement to
 3           operations.

 4   Q.      DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH MESSRS. SMITH AND TROKEY’S
 5           STATEMENT               THAT        “THE         OBJECTIVES             OF       MAXIMIZING
 6           SHAREHOLDER VALUE ON THE ONE HAND AND MINIMIZING
 7           COSTS TO RATEPAYERS ON THE OTHER HAND, ARE GENERALLY
 8           OPPOSED TO EACH OTHER”?

 9   A.      No. To deliver value to shareholders, the Company must constantly find ways to
10           run its business as efficiently as possible without sacrificing customer satisfaction
11           and reliability. The efficiencies gained and the cost reductions resulting from those
12           efforts are returned to customers through lower revenue requirements. Therefore,
13           many of the Company’s actions to increase shareholder value will result in lower
14           costs and increased value to customers.

10   Q.      CAN YOU BRIEFLY DESCRIBE THE CONCERNS YOU HAVE
11           REGARDING PIA STAFF’S DISMANTLEMENT COST ASSUMPTIONS?

12   A.      PIA Staff Witness Kaufman removes contingency from plant dismantlement cost
13           estimates for coal and gas production facilities. He concludes that contingency
14           represents “unexpected outcomes” and therefore, should be removed from the
15           depreciation expense calculations for the Company’s coal and gas plants.

16           When developing cost estimates, contingency is a fundamental element of a
17           comprehensive and transparent estimate. Contingency is necessary to acknowledge
18           and mitigate the risk of inherent cost uncertainties that accompany capital-intensive
19           projects such as dismantlement of a coal or gas fired electric generation facility.

24           did not have air environmental controls (e.g., baghouses, scrubbers, Selective

 7                   VIII. GRID INVESTMENT PLAN, DERMS, AND RULES AND
 8                                 REGULATIONS SECTION G

 9   Transmission Grid Investment

10   Q.      HAS       THE       COMPANY            USED        OBJECTIVE            APPROACHES                 TO
11           DETERMINE WHICH EQUIPMENT SHOULD BE REPLACED AS PART
12           OF THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN AS RECOMMENDED BY STAFF
13           WITNESSES ALVAREZ, STEPHENS, AND RANA?

14   A.      Yes, the Company relied on its long history of robust maintenance practices,
15           internal experience, and historical operational knowledge to determine which
16           transmission equipment will be replaced as part of the Grid Investment Plan. The
17           Company incorporated this experience into its recommendation to replace
18           transmission equipment that will be greater than the Company expected life by the
19           end of the Grid Investment Plan period. While the industry expected life for
20           transformers and breakers is 35 years and 25 years, respectively, the Company has
21           been able to extend the life of equipment beyond its industry expected life through
22           its existing comprehensive maintenance practices. The Company’s expected life for
23           transformers and breakers is 50 years and 35 years, respectively.

24           Contrary to Staff Witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana’s overreliance on
25           inspection and performance testing for determining when equipment should be
26           replaced, routine diagnostic testing and inspections cannot always accurately

          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 39 of 61

 1           predict when equipment failure will occur on the system. Over the last ten years,
 2           196 substation transformers failed in-service at an average age of 43 years, which
 3           is lower than the Company expected life for this asset. Of these 196 substation
 4           transformers, 108 failed (55%) after passing such diagnostic tests and inspections.
 5           Therefore, the Company’s experience to date informed its recommendation to
 6           replace transmission assets through the Grid Investment Program that have or will
 7           exceed their Company expected life by 2031 rather than solely relying on the results
 8           of the Company’s routine diagnostic tests and inspections.

 9   Q.      WILL TRANSMISSION INVESTMENTS IN THE GRID INVESTMENT
10           PLAN PREVENT SYSTEM RELIABILITY DETERIORATION?

11   A.      Yes. Transmission investments pursuant to the Company’s Grid Investment Plan
12           are intended to replace assets that have or will have exceeded their Company
13           expected life by the end of the plan. These assets are being replaced to prevent
14           reliability challenges associated with aged transmission assets. Based on the
15           Company’s operational experience and common understanding, the likelihood of
16           failure for transmission assets included in the plan increases as the assets age
17           beyond their expected life. Due to the large percentage of transmission equipment
18           that is beyond or nearing its Company expected life, the Company designed the
19           Grid Investment Plan to target the reasonable replacement of such assets to prevent
20           transmission system reliability deterioration across the system. If these
21           replacements are not undertaken, system reliability will be negatively impacted due
22           to increased failures of aged equipment. Such failures can be catastrophic and
23           simultaneous in nature. Replacing these aged assets will reduce the number of
24           emergency failures, mitigate system operation reliability concerns, avoid higher
25           maintenance and replacement costs, and ensure the safety of the general public and
26           the Company’s employees.


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 40 of 61

 1   Q.      WOULD IT BE PRACTICAL FOR THE COMPANY TO CONDUCT AND
 2           RELY ON A COST-BENEFIT ANALYSIS FOR TRANSMISSION
 3           INVESTMENTS INCLUDED IN THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN AS
 4           RECOMMENDED BY PIA STAFF WITNESSES?

 5   A.      No, it is impractical to perform a cost-benefit analysis for transmission investments
 6           included in the Grid Investment Plan due to the dynamic and networked nature of
 7           the transmission system and the number of variables that could change suddenly in
 8           such a study. The transmission system has a wide variety of components and factors
 9           that impact its operation, such as system loading and flows, facility and generation
10           outages, and weather. The ability to conduct a meaningful and accurate cost-benefit
11           analysis of transmission investments in the Grid Investment Plan is further
12           complicated by the type of failure that could occur (controlled or catastrophic),
13           impacts to other capital projects, lead times for replacement equipment, and the cost
14           of generation redispatch due to loss of transmission elements. The constant change
15           in these variables would produce a cost-benefit analysis with unreliable conclusions
16           that could be rendered obsolete or invalid at any given time. The results of such an
17           analysis would be questionable, at best. For these reasons, the Company used asset
18           age to determine capital investment in each transmission facility to mitigate the risk
19           of outages associated with a significant and growing number of assets that are
20           approaching or have exceeded their Company expected lives.

21   Q.      DOES        THE       COMPANY’S             DATA        SUPPORT           STAFF        WITNESS

27           entire transformer fleet can or should operate that far beyond Company expected
28           life. The tables below represent transmission and distribution substation equipment

 5   Q.      IS THE COMPANY PERFORMING PREEMPTIVE REPLACEMENTS
 6           FOR TRANSMISSION EQUIPMENT INCLUDED IN THE GRID
 7           INVESTMENT PLAN?

 8   A.      No. The Company disagrees with Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens’
 9           characterization of the Company’s Grid Investment Plan strategy for transmission
10           assets as “preemptive replacement.” The Company relied on its extensive
11           operational and maintenance experience, outage and failure history, and ability to
12           extend asset expected life to determine the age at which it should replace
13           transmission assets within the Grid Investment Plan. In addition, the Company
14           reviewed, analyzed, and compared its replacement assumptions against industry
15           expected life ages for the transmission equipment to be replaced in the Grid
16           Investment Plan.

17           As the Company testified during direct hearings, the bathtub curve is utilized to
18           determine the variation of equipment failure rate with respect to time. The bathtub
19           curve presented in Witness Alvarez and Stephens’ testimony is based upon the
20           estimated useful life of a hypothetical piece of equipment. In contrast, the bathtub
21           curve, as illustrated in Figure 1 below, is based on the expected life of an asset,
22           which is defined as the life expectancy of the Company’s equipment. The life
23           expectancy of equipment is supported by internal maintenance procedures, past
24           experiences, equipment specifications, and monitoring of equipment. It is important
25           to note that estimated useful life and expected life are two very separate and distinct
26           terms.


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 43 of 61

 1                           FIGURE 1: Bathtub Curve for Expected Life


 2
 3      Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens mistakenly point to the middle of their estimated
 4      useful life bathtub curve and misrepresent the point at which the Company is
 5      replacing transmission equipment as part of the Grid Investment Plan. However,
 6      the Company is replacing equipment in the “end-of-life period” where failures
 7      occur at an exponential rate due to age-related degradation of equipment. The
 8      Company is strategically targeting transmission projects within this “end-of-life
 9      period” in a controlled and planned manner to prevent customer interruptions,
10      catastrophic equipment failure, and better manage replacement costs for customers.
11      As mentioned in several discovery requests, the cost of replacing transmission
12      equipment that has catastrophically failed is 2 to 13 times the cost of replacing this
13      equipment in a planned manner. Within this context and background, the Company
14      strongly maintains that it is not performing preemptive replacement on its
15      transmission equipment as part of its Grid Investment Plan.


     ______________________________________________________________________________

     Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                     on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                               Docket No. 44280
                                                 Page 44 of 61

 1   Q.      WHEN FAILURES OCCUR FOR AGED EQUIPMENT ON THE SYSTEM,
 2           SHOULD THE COMPANY SOLELY DEPEND ON SPARE EQUIPMENT
 3           BEING AVAILABLE TO REPLACE THE FAILED EQUIPMENT AS
 4           SUGGESTED BY STAFF WITNESSES?

 5   A.      No. Solely depending on spare equipment to supplement failed equipment would
 6           present both operational and supply chain challenges if failures occur. The
 7           Company’s equipment stock levels are aimed to address emergency replacements
 8           based on historical failure rates and other factors. The current stock levels are
 9           agreed upon by Integrated Transmission System (“ITS”) Participants, which along
10           with customers, bear the cost of the spare equipment pool. If the Grid Investment
11           Plan is not continued, 44% of substation transformers and 37% of substation
12           breakers will have exceeded their Company expected life by 2031. With having
13           such a large amount of equipment at or nearing the “end-of-life” period at the same
14           time, the Company is concerned that the existing spare equipment stock will not be
15           able to keep up with the replacement demand for failed equipment. For example,
16           the Company’s current stock levels for substation transformers represents 3% of
17           the total population transformers on the system. Furthermore, there are 171
18           different types of transformers on Georgia Power’s system. The Company currently
19           only has spare transformers available for 38 of the 171 transformer types, with
20           many of the available transformer spare counts being limited to only one unit on
21           hand.

22           Due to industry supply chain challenges, the lead times for all substation equipment
23           are longer than the Company has seen in many years, with average substation
24           transformer lead times increasing from approximately six months to three years.
25           These lead times continue to grow as other utilities invest in their system and global
26           pressures on the supply-chain for grid infrastructure continue. As transmission
27           equipment gets older and failure rates increase, the Company and customers will
28           be negatively impacted given the number of spares in existing inventory. The


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 45 of 61

 1           Company could choose to increase its spare equipment pool, but this would also
 2           negatively impact customers, as keeping too much spare inventory would lead to
 3           higher expenditures and higher carrying costs. Depending on spare equipment
 4           means that the Company would wait for equipment to fail before it acts. The
 5           Company is strongly opposed to such an approach. The Grid Investment Plan offers
 6           a controlled, programmatic, multi-year approach to replacing aging transmission
 7           assets in a way that reasonably accounts for these operational and supply chain
 8           considerations.

 9   Distribution Grid Investment

10   Q.      DOES        THE       COMPANY             AGREE         WITH        STAFF’S         PROPOSED
11           REDUCTIONS TO THE DISTRIBUTION PORTION OF THE GRID
12           INVESTMENT PLAN, AND THE OVERALL ASSERTION “THERE IS NO
13           PENDING RELIABILITY EMERGENCY”?

14   A.      No, the Company does not agree that the Basic Insulation Level (“BIL”) and
15           Undergrounding investment packages, which make up approximately 85% of
16           Distribution Grid investments, should be removed from the program. Nor does the
17           Company agree with the statement that “there is no pending reliability
18           emergency.”5 Georgia Power’s system reliability will continue to worsen every
19           year that investments are not made. Between 2013 and 2021,6 the Company’s
20           system average interruption duration index (“SAIDI”) worsened by approximately
21           44% and system average interruption frequency index (“SAIFI”) worsened by
22           approximately 10%. For a subset of feeders (also referred to as circuits) in the
23           system, and the customers on those feeders, the decline in reliability is even more
24           pronounced. This worsening reliability has significant negative impacts on


     5
      Trade Secret Testimony of Paul J. Alvarez and Dennis Stephens, p. 2, lines 23 – 24.
     6
      Georgia Power reliability performance 2014 - 2021, as reported by the Energy Information
     Administration.

          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 46 of 61

 1             customer experience7 due to more frequent and longer outages, as well as increases
 2             in the economic cost incurred by customers.

 3             It is within this context, and with extreme care, that the Company created the
 4             Distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan, which is designed to ensure
 5             lasting reliability improvements while minimizing the total cost to the customer.
 6             Removing the BIL and Undergrounding investment packages from the Grid
 7             Investment Plan would result in significantly longer and more frequent outages
 8             across the system, as these packages are the best way to permanently address the
 9             root causes of outages on these feeders. In the absence of these investments, the
10             Company would achieve an end-state SAIDI of approximately 127 by 2031,
11             essentially equal to the program starting point SAIDI in 2019, compared to the Grid
12             Investment Plan’s current projected end-state of approximately 80. Therefore, the
13             Staff’s recommendations would maintain the system-level SAIDI at third quartile
14             levels.

15   Q.        IS THE REVISION TO THE COMPANY’S END-STATE SAIDI FOR THE
16             DISTRIBUTION PORTION OF THE GRID INVESTMENT PLAN A
17             REFLECTION OF REDUCED RELIABILITY EXPECTATIONS, AS
18             CLAIMED BY STAFF?

19   A.        No. The revision to the end-state SAIDI targeted in the Company’s Distribution
20             portion of the Grid Investment Plan is a result of lessons learned from the first three
21             years of implementation and not a reflection of changed reliability expectations. In
22             fact, the Company has seen better than expected reliability improvements from the
23             set of investments that have completed construction. Initial results indicate an
24             improvement of approximately 65% in both SAIDI and SAIFI for Grid projects that
25             have been completed for over a year and cycled through both a winter and summer

         Response to STF-WG-2-7(j), Docket No. 42516.

                                                      Docket No. 44280

 1             peak since completion.8 The investments are working and are delivering improved
 2             reliability for Georgia Power’s customers.

 3             As outlined in our direct testimony, the Company refined certain components of the
 4             Grid Investment Plan based on insights and lessons learned in the first three years
 5             of the program:
 6                     Georgia Power confirmed that the overall strategy and economic rationale
 7                      supporting the Grid Investment Plan is optimal for customers. The specific
 8                      investment packages deployed are producing expected benefits and
 9                      delivering positive results.

10                     The total cost required to deliver individual investments is higher than the
11                      Company originally projected because of increases in scope (e.g., additional
12                      automation devices required) and in the cost of work (e.g., inflation, COVID
13                      shortages).

23             deliver an 80 end-state SAIDI as compared to the 65 end-state SAIDI included in
24             the 2019 rate case plan, which is still first quartile results compared to industry
25             peers. The Company considers this intermittent refinement of the 10-year Grid
26             Investment Plan to be essential for ensuring that customer capital is managed
27             responsibly.

            Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                            on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                      Docket No. 44280
                                                        Page 48 of 61

 1   Q.      WHY DID THE COMPANY ELECT TO OPTIMIZE THE GRID
 2           INVESTMENT PLAN AT THE SYSTEM RATHER THAN FEEDER
 3           LEVEL?

 4   A.      The Grid Investment Plan optimizes benefits at the system level rather than the
 5           feeder level because the cost of system reliability is borne by all customers served
 6           by Georgia Power. In other words, the explicit cost for capital investment and
 7           ongoing O&M expense for the grid is paid for by all customers, regardless of each
 8           customer’s reliability experience and regardless of whether the customer’s
 9           individual feeder is included within the Grid Investment Plan. The Company
10           intentionally chose to optimize benefits across the system to ensure the investments
11           made were in the best interest of all customers instead of just the subset of
12           customers served by an individual feeder.

13   Q.      DOES THE COMPANY’S DISTRIBUTION PORTION OF THE GRID
14           INVESTMENT PLAN IGNORE A FEEDER-BY-FEEDER ANALYSIS AS
15           SUGGESTED BY STAFF WITNESSES ALVAREZ AND STEPHENS?

16   A.      No. Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens propose that the Company conduct a
17           root cause analysis for every feeder on the Company’s system, which implies that
18           the Company has not done such analysis. However, this is a misconception. In fact,
19           each and every distribution investment within the Grid Investment Plan is evaluated
20           on a feeder-by-feeder basis with a root cause analysis. The Company does exactly
21           the level of analysis suggested by Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens.

22   Q.      PLEASE DESCRIBE THE FEEDER-LEVEL ROOT CAUSE ANALYSIS
23           THE COMPANY CONDUCTS FOR POTENTIAL GRID INVESTMENTS.

24   A.      To evaluate a distribution feeder for the Grid Investment Plan, first, the Company
25           aggregates all the pertinent and available data across the system. This data begins
26           with five years of total reliability performance for each feeder as measured by


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 49 of 61

 1           SAIDI, SAIFI, customer minutes of interruption (CMI), and frequency of different
 2           outage event causes. The initial ‘hurdle’ for any feeder to qualify for investment is
 3           that the feeder has had sustained poor performance over the past five years.

 4           The Company then undertakes a second evaluation to determine the root causes of
 5           outages on each feeder. In this step, the Company disaggregates every feeder’s
 6           reliability performance to understand the events that caused those outages. For
 7           example, the Company evaluates what percentage of a feeder’s outages were the
 8           result of vegetation, vehicles, animals, or weather and how that percentage
 9           compares to the rest of the system. An evaluated feeder qualifies for a distribution
10           investment package that would permanently resolve the outage cause(s) only if it
11           experiences a higher percentage of outages driven by that particular cause(s) than
12           the system average. In this way, the Company deploys standard, repeatable criteria
13           systematically across all feeders to identify outliers in each category of outage
14           cause.

15           Therefore, prior to optimizing the plan, the Company has completed a feeder-by-
16           feeder, root cause analysis on the entire Distribution system. Through this analysis,
17           the Company determines whether a feeder should be considered for any investment
18           and, if so, which of the six possible investment options specifically would address
19           the root causes of reliability issues on that feeder.

20   Q.      PLEASE DESCRIBE HOW THE COMPANY SELECTS FEEDERS FOR
21           INVESTMENT AFTER CONDUCTING THE ROOT CAUSE ANALYSIS.

22   A.      For each of the possible investment packages identified during the root cause
23           analysis, the Company projects the costs and the reliability benefits specific to
24           deploying qualified investment package(s) on each feeder. The cost and benefit for
25           each investment will vary depending on unique characteristics of each feeder, such
26           as starting reliability performance and number of line miles to be worked.


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 50 of 61

 1              The Company then evaluates the impact of all possible combinations of feeder level
 2              investments to determine the final investment plan that provides the best reliability
 3              and the lowest total cost to customers. To do this, the Company evaluates the total
 4              cost to all Georgia Power customers of each potential investment plan and selects
 5              the plan that minimizes total cost to customers across the system– inclusive of the
 6              cost of investment, the cost of remaining outages, and the cost of O&M that
 7              customers bear. In this fashion, the Company considers the direct cost of
 8              investments as well as the costs customers bear from increased outages related to
 9              not investing in some potential investments. The Company acknowledges that it
10              has an obligation to provide reliable electric service and minimize outages for all
11              customers and, therefore, must evaluate the total cost to deliver the best plan for all
12              of its 2.7 million customers.

13   Q.         DID STAFF WITNESSES ALVAREZ AND STEPHENS PROVIDE
14              ADEQUATE SUPPORT FOR THEIR ASSERTION THAT BIL AND
15              UNDERGROUNDING ARE NOT COST EFFECTIVE?

16   A.         No, the Company fervently disagrees with Staff’s assertions that the BIL and
17              Undergrounding packages are not cost-effective.9 In executing their cost
18              effectiveness analysis, Staff systematically underestimated investment benefits by
19              not accurately representing the cost to customers of foregoing BIL and
20              Undergrounding:

21                 First, Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens did not account for the continued
22                  reliability degradation that customers will experience if feeders selected for BIL
23                  or Undergrounding are denied investment, underestimating the economic cost
24                  to customers of the increased outages.

25                 Second, Staff did not quantify the outage repair-related and vegetation-related
26                  O&M expenses that will be incurred if these feeders are removed from the Grid
27                  Investment Plan.

 3           Investment Plan. The Company has the utmost confidence that the distribution

24           determine how much it would cost to actually improve reliability through

21   DERMS and Rules and Regulations Section G Recommendation

22   Q.         ARE CURRENT SOFTWARE SYSTEMS SUFFICIENT ENOUGH TO
23              EFFICIENTLY MANAGE THE GROWING AMOUNT OF DISTRIBUTED


     18
          Trade Secret Testimony of Paul J. Alvarez and Dennis Stephens, p. 53, lines 13 – 14.
     19
          Trade Secret Testimony of Paul J. Alvarez and Dennis Stephens, p. 53, lines 12 – 13.

            ______________________________________________________________________________

             Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                             on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                       Docket No. 44280
                                                         Page 55 of 61

 1           ENERGY RESOURCES (“DER”) THAT WILL BE INTERCONNECTED
 2           ON THE SYSTEM?

 3   A.      No. While current software systems allow for the monitoring of power flows and
 4           equipment statuses, they lack the enhanced monitoring and operational capabilities
 5           necessary to allow for the optimization of an electric system that takes full
 6           advantage of a proliferation of DER. The applications currently in place are not
 7           capable of managing the substantial number of DERs expected to connect to the
 8           grid in the coming years. As more DER are added to the system, it is essential for
 9           the Company to have visibility, predictability, and control (where applicable) of
10           these resources. Failure to do so could compromise the Company’s ability to
11           accurately forecast and optimize the full array of generation resources, and also
12           negatively impact power quality and reliability. Further, a system operator will not
13           be able to process the significant amount of data coming in and make informed
14           decisions quickly enough to optimize grid operation if using current software
15           systems.

16           In contrast, a DERMS fully integrated with the Company’s other grid control
17           systems unlocks the potential to continually optimize larger portions of the grid and
18           solve for a variety of constraints, such as economic dispatch, Volt/VAR
19           optimization, and reliability. Additionally, DERMS will allow the Company to
20           consider demand side resources alongside supply side resources in future IRPs, as
21           ordered by the Commission in the 2022 IRP. The Company’s implementation of
22           DERMS will allow for maturation of the solution while thousands or tens of
23           thousands of additional discrete DER are connected to the grid. If DER growth is
24           unrestricted without the installation of DERMS, the Company risks a loss of grid
25           control, which would compromise grid reliability and stability. A DERMS solution
26           is a natural, logical, and necessary step for the Company to prepare for the growth
27           and integration of variable and distributed energy resources.


          ______________________________________________________________________________

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 56 of 61

 1   Q.      COULD THE COMPANY DELAY ITS DERMS PROGRAM UNTIL DER
 2           ADOPTION IS HIGHER?

 3   A.      No. There are significant risks to grid stability if the Company were to delay
 4           implementing DERMS. Beginning implementation of a DERMS solution now
 5           allows for the full DERMS capabilities envisioned by the Company to be developed
 6           in parallel with increasing DER penetration across the electric grid. Further, the
 7           complexity of integrating DERMS with the Company’s existing critical software
 8           systems will require a significant amount of time. Undertaking DERMS over the
 9           next three years reduces the risk of restricting DER interconnections in the future
10           because of a lack of DER forecasting, control, and optimization capability.

11   Q.      IS THE INTENT BEHIND THE COMPANY’S PROPOSED EDITS TO
12           SECTION G OF THE RULES AND REGULATIONS TO REQUIRE
13           UNCONDITIONAL                 ACCESS          AND       CONTROL            OF      CUSTOMER
14           GENERATORS, AS STAFF SUGGESTS?

15   A.      No. The Company proposed requiring that the customer generation facilities
16           interconnecting to the Company’s electric system include a communications
17           interface, installed and maintained by the customer, that supports information
18           exchange with, and control by, Georgia Power so that the Company may continue
19           to operate the electric system in a safe, reliable, and efficient manner. The proposed
20           requirement to allow for communication and increased visibility to DERs operating
21           in parallel with the system is critical to maintaining the system’s safety and
22           reliability. As the number of distributed generators grows, the ability to
23           communicate and potentially interact with customer generation will allow for
24           additional growth that might not otherwise be possible. Notably, the Company has
25           only requested this change for customer generation facilities sized 250 kW and
26           above that operate in parallel with the Company’s electric system for more than
27           100 milliseconds.

          Rebuttal Testimony of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson
                                          on behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280
                                                      Page 57 of 61

 1   Q.      DID THE COMPANY INTEND FOR THIS PROPOSED CHANGE TO BE
 2           CONSISTENT WITH THE REQUIREMENTS OF IEEE-1547?

 3   A.      Yes. The Company intends for the proposed changes to be consistent with the
 4           requirements of IEEE-1547, as they may be amended from time to time.

 5   Q.      WHAT IS THE JUSTIFICATION FOR THE COMPANY’S REQUEST TO
 6           REQUIRE EXISTING CUSTOMER GENERATORS TO INSTALL A
 7           LOCAL INTERFACE FOR COMMUNICATION AND CONTROL?

 8   A.      Utility visibility and control of customer generation facilities becomes an essential
 9           function as the adoption of DER increases. Without visibility, it is impossible for a
10           utility to ascertain the status, availability, real time actual output, and forecast
11           availability of a resource. Without control, it is impossible for a utility to update
12           functional settings such as active power setpoint, frequency response, and reactive
13           power modes in response to real time grid conditions. Both visibility and control
14           are necessary functions to enable real time economic optimization of the electric
15           grid. The Company is requesting that all existing and future customer generation
16           facilities be equipped with a local communications interface so that these facilities
17           will be available to the Company’s eventual DERMS solution. Without visibility
18           and control capability on existing customer generation facilities, gaps will be
19           present in the Company’s ability to be fully aware of the interaction of those
20           facilities with other generation facilities and the rest of the electric grid.

26           Program will close to plant-in-service as they are incurred, thus incorrectly

 1   Q.      BASED ON THE COMPANY’S INITIAL ANALYSIS OF THE IRA, WHAT
 2           OPPORTUNITIES FOR TAX CREDITS WERE IDENTIFIED?

 3   A.      The Company’s initial analysis of the IRA identified approximately $44 million of
 4           additional tax credit benefits related to projected, qualified renewable energy and
 5           electric vehicle investments. The largest benefit identified in the Company’s initial
 6           analysis relates to investment tax credits (“ITC”) associated with the Mossy Branch
 7           battery storage project. The Mossy Branch project, which is projected to go into
 8           service in 2023, is a project that the Company currently believes would qualify for
 9           a 30% ITC. Additionally, with the Commission’s approval, the Company can opt-
10           out of the traditional ITC normalization structure which would result in giving
11           customers all the ITC benefit in the year the project goes into service instead of
12           over the life of the asset. The Company also expects to generate ITCs on the
13           McGrau Ford battery facility; however, those credits are not expected to be
14           generated until after 2025 when the facility is expected to be placed into service. In
15           addition, the Company’s initial analysis shows the IRA will provide more options
16           to pursue and compete for lower cost solar energy investments as a result of
17           expanded tax credits. Through the IRA, the Company now has an option to choose
18           between either the traditional ITC or a new Production Tax Credit (“PTC”) for
19           Company-owned solar projects; however, the Company is still not able to claim
20           ITC or PTCs on solar resources procured through purchased power agreements,
21           which account for the majority of the Company’s new solar resources expected to
22           come online in years 2023 through 2025. For new Company-owned solar facilities
23           projected to come online in those years, the Company estimates the PTC option
24           will provide approximately $0.4 million of additional benefits for customers in
25           2025 related to projected ash pond solar investments at Plant McIntosh, Plant
26           McDonough, and Plant Hammond.

27           Since the filing of direct testimony, the Company identified IRA benefits for which
28           it may qualify. Georgia Power estimates that the Hydro Modernization projects

                                     I.      INTRODUCTION

 9        WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF YOUR REBUTTAL TESTIMONY?

10   A.   The purpose of my rebuttal testimony is to respond to the direct testimonies of the
11        following witnesses as they relate to the appropriate return on equity (“ROE”) and capital
12        structure for Georgia Power: Mr. Michael P. Gorman on behalf of the Commission’s Public
13        Interest Advocacy Staff (“Staff”), Ms. Billie S. LaConte on behalf of Georgia Association
