 1        ARE YOU SPONSORING ANY EXHIBITS AS PART OF YOUR REBUTTAL
 2        TESTIMONY?

 3   A.   No.

 4        HOW      IS   THE      REMAINDER             OF     YOUR          REBUTTAL   TESTIMONY
 5        ORGANIZED?

 6   A.   My rebuttal testimony is organized to respond to the Intervenor Witnesses by issue, starting
 7        in Section II with an overview and summary of the results and recommendations presented
 8        by the various ROE witnesses in this proceeding. Section III discusses economic and
 9        capital market conditions and how those conditions are affecting the various models used
10        to estimate the cost of equity for Georgia Power. In Section IV, I address the proper
11        application of the cost of equity analytical models employed by me and the Intervenor
12        Witnesses, including the Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) model, the Capital Asset Pricing
13        Model (“CAPM”), the Bond Yield Plus Risk Premium (“Risk Premium”) model and the
14        Expected Earnings model. In Section V, I respond to comments raised by certain witnesses
15        with respect to Georgia Power’s proposed capital structure and cost of debt, and I explain
16        why that capital structure is reasonable by comparison to the proxy group. In Section VI, I
17        respond to comments regarding Georgia Power’s company-specific risk factors raised by
18        the Intervenor Witnesses. Lastly, in Section VII, I summarize my key conclusions and
19        continue to recommend an 11.00 percent ROE (including flotation costs) and Georgia
20        Power’s actual capital structure of 56.0 percent common equity and 44.0 percent long-term
21        debt.

22                             II.      SUMMARY OF TESTIMONY

23        WHAT ARE YOUR CONCLUSIONS REGARDING THE ANALYSIS AND
24        RECOMMENDATIONS PROVIDED BY THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES
25        REGARDING THE APPROPRIATE ROE AND CAPITAL STRUCTURE FOR
26        GEORGIA POWER?

27   A.   My key conclusions are as follows:

                                     Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                     On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                               Docket No. 44280
                                                  Page 2 of 52

 1        (1)    The Intervenor Witnesses’ analyses contain flaws and inconsistencies that produce
 2               some results well below recent authorized returns for electric utilities, particularly
 3               those comparable to Georgia Power. Some recommendations are even 50 to 100
 4               basis points below prevailing costs of debt for utilities. Such recommendations
 5               should be given no weight as no investor would take on the perpetual risks of equity
 6               ownership when they could earn a higher return for safer debt investments that have
 7               a senior claim on cash flows.

 8        (2)    The cost of equity for regulated utility companies is being affected by several key
 9               factors in the current and prospective capital markets, including the interest rate
10               environment and central bank monetary policy, as well as current inflationary
11               pressure. Inflation has escalated to levels not seen since the early 1980s; interest
12               rates across the yield spectrum have increased; and capital market volatility remains
13               elevated. These circumstances reinforce the importance of considering the results
14               of multiple models, as I have with the DCF, CAPM, Risk Premium, and Expected
15               Earnings approaches.

16        (3)    All models are subject to certain limiting assumptions. However, in market
17               conditions where ROE estimation models are producing return estimates below the
18               current cost of debt for utilities (e.g., Mr. Ellis’ DCF and CAPM estimates), utility
19               regulators recognize that such low returns are not compensatory for investors.
20               Rather than endorsing the results of a specific methodology, the Commission
21               should consider how current market conditions affect the risks for equity investors
22               as well as take into account the results of a broader range of ROE estimation
23               methodologies.

26   A.   The Intervenor Witnesses who performed an independent ROE analysis (Mr. Gorman, Ms.
27        Reno, and Mr. Ellis) recommend an authorized ROE for Georgia Power between 5.54
28        percent and 9.47 percent. Other Witnesses did not perform their own ROE analysis, and
29        instead adjusted certain inputs in my analyses (Ms. LaConte) or referenced authorized

 5            recommendation.2 Mr. Gorman accepts the Company’s proposed 56 percent equity ratio
 6            for 2023, but recommends the Commission impute a hypothetical capital structure of 51
 7            percent equity for 2024 and 2025.3 Ms. LaConte recommends a capital structure that
                                                                           4

 9            structure that contains 61.70 percent equity to offset his 5.54 percent ROE
10            recommendation.

11            As is evident, there is a broad array of recommendations from multiple witnesses. Notably,
12            recommendations as low as 5.54 percent are below any authorized return for any electric
13            utility in the last 40 years5 and are lower than current market indicators of utility and
14            corporate debt costs. Some of the recommendations are supported by analytical approaches
15            while others are more judgmental or based on decisions from other jurisdictions. I submit
16            that the appropriate method for determining the cost of capital is through the application of
17            rigorous analysis using financial models and current market data from reliable sources.

 1        PLEASE DESCRIBE THE LEGAL STANDARDS THAT MUST BE MET TO
 2        ESTABLISH THE AUTHORIZED ROE FOR A REGULATED PUBLIC UTILITY
 3        SUCH AS GEORGIA POWER.

 4   A.   As discussed in my direct testimony, the standards for a just and reasonable return
 5        established by the United States Supreme Court in the Hope and Bluefield cases are:

 6                (1) Financial integrity: the return must be adequate to ensure the company’s

 7                financial soundness and support credit quality;

 8                (2) Capital attraction: the return must be sufficient to enable the company to attract

 9                capital on reasonable terms and conditions; and

10                (3) Comparable return: the return must be comparable to those available to

11                investors in firms with commensurate risk.

12        Importantly, in Bluefield, the U.S. Supreme Court recognized that a return may be
13        reasonable at one time, but may become too high or too low at another time due to changes
14        affecting investment opportunities, and market or business conditions.10 This principle is
15        relevant because, as discussed below, certain of the Intervenor Witnesses refer to recent
16        authorized ROEs to support their recommendations, but do not fully consider the
17        magnitude of the change in market conditions since those ROEs were determined.

18        SEVERAL OF THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES (GORMAN, LACONTE, RENO,
19        CHRISS) REFERENCE AUTHORIZED ROES FOR ELECTRIC UTILITIES IN
20        OTHER JURISDICTIONS. DO YOU AGREE THAT THESE RETURNS ARE
21        RELEVANT IN ESTABLISHING THE ROE FOR GEORGIA POWER IN THIS
22        PROCEEDING?

23   A.   National average returns must be placed in the proper context in order to be useful. While
24        I agree that investors consider authorized returns in other states in assessing the


     10   Bluefield Water Works v. Public Service Commission, 262 U.S. 679, 692 (1923).

 1           reasonableness of the authorized ROE for Georgia Power, I have several concerns with the
 2           nationwide average ROE information presented by the Intervenor Witnesses. First, the
 3           averages referenced by Mr. Gorman and Mr. Chriss include ROE determinations for
 4           transmission and distribution-only (“T&D”) electric utilities, as well as determinations in
 5           formula rate proceedings in which the ROE is determined by a formula solely tied to
 6           changes in Treasury bond yields. These ROE determinations are not comparable to
 7           authorized ROEs for vertically integrated electric utilities, nor are they comparable to
 8           determinations that are based on the use of various financial models as is the process used
 9           by the Commission. Vertically integrated electric utilities have a higher level of business
10           risk than T&D utility companies that do not own regulated generation.11 This higher risk
11           profile differentiates integrated electric utilities from T&D utilities and supports a higher
12           authorized ROE and equity ratio in the capital structure. Lastly, their references to average
13           authorized ROEs do not include approvals earlier this year by the Florida Public Service
14           Commission to increase the authorized ROEs of Florida Power & Light, Tampa Electric
15           Company, and Duke Energy Florida due to increases in the 30-year Treasury yield outlined
16           as part of those utilities’ most recent rate case settlements.

17           Second, market conditions at the time the authorized returns were established are very
18           different than conditions today and going forward. For example, equity returns set when
19           interest rates were very low in 2020 are not a reasonable basis of comparison for an
20           appropriate ROE in 2023 when bond yields have increased and are projected to continue
21           increasing as the economy recovers and the Federal Reserve moves to a more neutral
22           monetary policy. After the decline in interest rates in 2020-2021 driven by the Federal
23           Reserve’s unprecedented actions to respond to the COVID-19 pandemic, interest rates have
24           increased by approximately 160 to 230 basis points since the settlement adopting an ROE
25           of 10.50 percent for Georgia Power was authorized in December 2019.12 Since my direct

 1            testimony was prepared in April 2022, the 30-day average 30-year Treasury yield has

 3            30-year Treasury yield at the time I prepared my direct testimony. Although state utility
 4            commissions mitigated ROE reductions in 2020-2021 due to the knowledge that interest
 5            rates were being artificially suppressed due to the Federal Reserve’s actions,13 the use of
 6            prior decisions during that time period which set ROEs under previously lower interest

 8            rate environment that we are now experiencing.14 In other words, authorized ROEs that
 9            may have been considered reasonable and appropriate in 2020 and 2021, and even into the

 7        DOES CURRENT MARKET DATA ILLUSTRATE THE UNREASONABLENESS
 8        OF MR. ELLIS’S ROE RECOMMENDATION?

 9   A.   Yes. Current utility bond yields measured by Moody’s utility bond indices are
10        approximately 50 to 80 basis points above Mr. Ellis’s ROE recommendation.16 It is simply
11        illogical to expect that a rational investor would take on the risk of a utility equity
12        investment when they could earn a higher return from a safer bond that has a senior claim
13        on cash flow. Mr. Ellis attempts to adjust current utility bond yields below his cost of equity
14        estimates to account for certain risk premia (e.g., liquidity risk) and concludes without any
15        evidence that an equity premium between 67 and 124 basis points would be a sufficient
16        premium for equity investors.17 There is no evidence to demonstrate that equity investors
17        would be willing to accept such a small premium for taking on the risk of utility equity
18        investment.

 1        Mr. Ellis’s 5.54 percent ROE recommendation is substantially below national average
 2        authorized ROEs for vertically integrated electric utilities. In fact, it is nearly 400 basis
 3        points below the 9.53 percent average authorized ROE for vertically integrated electric
 4        utilities in 2021 cited by Ms. LaConte and Ms. Reno. Mr. Ellis’ higher equity ratio
 5        recommendation of 61.70 percent does not offset the unreasonableness of his ROE
 6        recommendation. The weighted cost of equity produced by Mr. Ellis’s ROE and capital
 7        structure recommendation is 3.42 percent (i.e., 5.54 percent multiplied by 61.70 percent).
 8        If adopted, Mr. Ellis’s weighted cost of equity would be well below that recently authorized
 9        for vertically integrated electric utilities. The average weighted cost of equity for vertically
10        integrated electric utilities in 2022 is 5.06 percent,18 163 basis points above Mr. Ellis’s
11        recommended weighted cost of equity. In fact, the 2022 average authorized weighted cost
12        of equity is only 39 basis points below his ROE recommendation, further demonstrating
13        the unreasonableness of his recommendation. Mr. Ellis’s recommendation is far too low to
14        be reasonable and would not satisfy the Hope and Bluefield standards for a risk comparable
15        return that would allow the Company to attract capital and maintain its financial integrity.
16        Moreover, it would represent a substantial departure from Georgia’s supportive regulatory
17        environment, introducing significant regulatory risk and higher costs of both debt and
18        equity to the detriment of customers.

 1                          III.    CAPITAL MARKET CONDITIONS

 2        PLEASE SUMMARIZE THE ECONOMIC AND FINANCIAL MARKET
 3        CONDITIONS OVER THE PAST SEVERAL MONTHS SINCE YOU SUBMITTED
 4        YOUR DIRECT TESTIMONY?

 5   A.   The conditions described in my direct testimony have continued since April 2022 and the
 6        primary capital market indicators continue to support higher return requirements by utility
 7        equity investors. Specifically, (1) inflation remains at levels not seen since the early 1980s,
 8        (2) interest rates across the yield spectrum have continued to increase, and (3) the elevated
 9        capital market volatility has sustained longer than anticipated.

10        It is important to consider current and expected conditions in the general economy and
11        financial markets because the authorized ROE for a public utility should allow the utility
12        to attract investor capital at a reasonable cost under a variety of economic and financial
13        market conditions.

14        PLEASE DISCUSS THESE CHANGES AND ROOT CAUSES OF THESE SHIFTS
15        IN CAPITAL MARKET CONDITIONS.

16   A.   Inflation has been both the greatest surprise and cause for concern among both
17        policymakers and investors. As illustrated below, inflation levels have moved within a
18        relatively narrow band over the past twenty years (other than during the Great Recession
19        of 2007/2008). Starting with the initial stages of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020, inflation
20        levels have been driven higher, reaching a peak of 9.0 percent in June of 2022 and leveling
21        off at 7.7 percent in October 2022. Inflation is being driven by strong consumer demand
22        and supply constraints, some of which have been exacerbated by COVID-19 related
23        government stimulus programs in the U.S. and international lockdowns. More recently, the
24        war in Ukraine, which began in February, is constraining the world’s supply of food and
25        fuel, pushing overall inflation higher and feeding into the cost of other products and
26        services.


                                    Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                    On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 12 of 52

 1           Figure 4: Consumer Price Index – All Urban, Not Seasonally Adjusted21


 2
 3        As a result of these substantially higher inflation rates, the Federal Reserve has been left
 4        little choice but to pull back on its COVID-related monetary policies and battle inflation
 5        with its only effective tool: higher interest rates. In response to the economic effects of
 6        COVID-19, the Federal Reserve had decreased the federal funds rate in March 2020 to a
 7        target range of 0.00 percent to 0.25 percent (which remained in effect until March 2022) in
 8        addition to other stimulus measures which increased the supply of money in the economy.
 9        Since I prepared my ROE analysis provided in direct testimony (with market data through
10        April 29, 2022), the Federal Reserve has continued unwinding its quantitative easing policy
11        and increased the target rate six times, beginning with 25 basis points on March 17, 50
12        basis points on May 5, 75 basis points on June 16, another 75 basis points on July 27,
13        another 75 basis points on September 21, and another 75 basis points on November 3 to a
14        target range of 3.75 – 4.00 percent. Expectations are for further increases this year, with
15        FOMC participants’ median projection of 4.375 percent by the end of 2022, increasing to
16        4.60 percent by the end of 2023.22 This demonstrates the level of Federal Reserve action
17        necessary to reel in inflation. The Fed is willing to risk a slowdown in the economy, and it
18        is clear that the era of record low interest rates and moderate inflation has closed. The 10-

14           require higher returns to compensate them for greater risk, increased equity market
15           volatility is indicative of higher return requirements for equities, including utilities.

 5        MR. GORMAN ASSERTS THAT RISK PREMIUMS AS MEASURED BY CREDIT
 6        SPREADS ARE “REASONABLY ALIGNED WITH LONG-TERM HISTORICAL
 7        AVERAGES.”26 DO YOU AGREE WITH HIS INTERPRETATION?
 8   A.   No, I do not. In his Table 12 on page 51, Mr. Gorman reviews annual average spreads
 9        between utility and corporate A-rated and Baa-rated bond yields and the 30-year Treasury
10        yield from 2019 to 2022 and compares those to the average historical spread. However, his
11        annual averages obscure variation over each year. Notably, his 2022 average masks the
12        widening in credit spreads that have occurred over the course of 2022, resulting in his
13        misleading conclusion that current credit spreads are “reasonably aligned with long-term
14        historical averages.” As Figures 6 and 7 below show, utility credit spreads have widened
15        considerably since January 2022 and are currently above both their 2022 annual average

 4           ARE CONDITIONS EXPECTED TO STABILIZE IN THE NEAR-TERM?

 5   A.      It does not appear so. While difficult to predict in the long-term, capital market conditions
 6           remain unsettled. The economy and capital markets remain in a tenuous state with ongoing
 7           global supply constraints and expectations of weaker consumer demand in the face of
 8           persistent inflation and higher interest rates. Interest rates may not fully reflect the effects
 9           of inflation because no one knows exactly how high interest rates must go before inflation
10           returns to more acceptable levels. The Federal Reserve has been targeting inflation of
11           around 2.0 percent for many years, as compared with current annualized inflation of 7.7
12           percent at the consumer level for the 12-month period ending in October 2022.

13           Looking forward, the inflation risks in the market are twofold: (1) either inflation becomes
14           embedded in the economy if the Federal Reserve does not move aggressively enough in
15           tightening monetary policy, or (2) the Federal Reserve responds to inflationary pressure by
16           raising short-term interest rates to a level that causes a slowdown in economic growth or a

 1                             IV.     COST OF EQUITY ANALYSES

 2        DO THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES ACCEPT YOUR PROXY GROUP?

 3   A.   Yes, the Intervenor Witnesses generally accept my proxy group as being reasonable.
 4        However, Mr. Gorman excludes American Electric Power Company (“AEP”), due to the
 5        pending sale of its Kentucky electric utility, and includes The Southern Company. As
 6        explained in my direct testimony, I disagree with the inclusion of Georgia Power’s parent
 7        company as it reflects circular logic.

 8        IS   AEP’S     PENDING         SALE      OF    KENTUCKY           POWER      COMPANY          A
 9        SIGNIFICANT TRANSACTION THAT WARRANTS AEP’S EXCLUSION FROM
10        THE PROXY GROUP?

11   A.   No, it is not. The primary reason to exclude companies on the basis of merger and
12        acquisition activity is because significant transactions can affect the market data (e.g., stock
13        prices) on which certain of the models rely. At the time the transaction was announced,
14        AEP had a market capitalization of $42.23 billion; the Kentucky Power transaction is
15        valued at $2.646 billion or only approximately 6.3 percent of AEP’s total market value at
16        the time the transaction was announced. Consequently, the transaction is not significant
17        enough to warrant AEP’s exclusion from the proxy group.

18                                            A.      DCF MODEL

19        MS. RENO BASES HER ROE RECOMMENDATIONS ON THE RESULTS OF
20        HER DCF ANALYSIS, WHILE MR. GORMAN AND MR. ELLIS GIVE 50
21        PERCENT WEIGHT TO THEIR DCF MODEL ESTIMATES IN DETERMINING
22        THEIR ULTIMATE ROE RECOMMENDATIONS. DO YOU AGREE THAT IT IS
23        APPROPRIATE TO PLACE THIS DEGREE OF RELIANCE ON THE DCF
24        MODEL FOR A U.S. UTILITY?

25   A.   No, I do not. As discussed in my direct testimony, while many U.S. utility regulators have
26        used the DCF model to establish the authorized ROE, several regulators, including FERC,


                                     Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                     On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                               Docket No. 44280
                                                 Page 21 of 52

 1        have recognized the challenges associated with relying solely on the DCF to establish the
 2        authorized ROE for regulated utilities in recent years.35 For that reason, other federal and
 3        state regulators have relied on the results of multiple methodologies both to test the
 4        reasonableness of the DCF results and to establish a cost of equity that reflects investors’
 5        required return on a going-forward basis.

 6        PLEASE ELABORATE ON YOUR CONCERNS WITH THE DCF MODEL
 7        UNDER CURRENT MARKET CONDITIONS.

 8   A.   Although I have included the DCF model in my analyses, I have concerns with the ability
 9        of the DCF model to produce reliable results under current market conditions. This concern
10        is amplified with an ROE analysis or recommendation relying exclusively on the DCF
11        model. As explained in my direct testimony, high utility valuations are expected to decline
12        as utilities underperform the broader market and, under these circumstances, the results of
13        the DCF model would understate the forward-looking cost of equity because the dividend
14        yield is calculated using historical average stock prices which do not fully reflect these
15        expectations.36 This shift has already begun to occur.

16        Mr. Gorman observes the high valuations of electric utilities, referencing the higher than
17        average P/E ratios. However, he disregards the effect of those high valuations on the results
18        of the DCF model, in particular the dividend yield component. Exhibit__(MPG-3) page 4
19        demonstrates the decline in the Value Line electric utility dividend yields over the past
20        decade, from 4.20 percent in 2012 to 3.36 percent in 2022. This indicates that investors are
21        paying more for a dollar of earnings from electric utilities than they did 10 years ago. As
22        interest rates increase from historic lows, these high valuations are likely to come under
23        pressure and decline. As noted earlier, utility stocks have underperformed the broad market
24        in September and October after outperforming earlier in 2022. As a result, my conclusion
25        is that the DCF model is understating the forward-looking cost of equity for regulated


     35   Direct Testimony of James M. Coyne, at 28-29.

                                     On behalf of Georgia Power Company

 1        utilities such as Georgia Power because the model is based on average historical stock
 2        prices that are based on unsustainable valuation levels.

 3        Mr. Ellis37 regards high market-to-book (“M/B”) ratios as a sign that authorized ROEs for
 4        regulated utilities are higher than the investor required cost of equity. To test this premise,
 5        I performed an analysis that examines the correlation between government bond yields and
 6        the market-to-book ratios for electric utilities since 1980, using data provided in
 7        Exhibit__(MPG-15) and Exhibit__(MPG-18) to Mr. Gorman’s direct testimony. As shown
 8        in Figure 10, the R2 for this analysis is approximately 0.72, indicating a strong linear
 9        relationship between M/B ratios and interest rates. This relationship indicates that utility
10        M/B ratios have increased not because authorized returns were higher than the true cost of
11        equity, but because interest rates on government bonds have steadily declined for the past
12        four decades. Low interest rates are favorable for capital-intensive industries such as
13        utilities, while increasing interest rates are not.

 3                                       1.      Growth Rates in the DCF Model

 4        PLEASE COMMENT ON THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES’ USE OF GROWTH
 5        RATES IN THE DCF MODEL.

 6   A.   Mr. Gorman and Ms. Reno consider a variety of growth rates including projected earnings
 7        per share (“EPS”), dividends per share (“DPS”), and book value per share, as well as
 8        “sustainable” growth rates and projected GDP growth. As explained in my direct
                                                                                                   38
 9        testimony, over the long term, dividend growth can only be sustained by earnings growth,
10        while dividend growth can depend on management decisions regarding the dividend payout
11        ratio over the near-term which do not reflect the long-term growth prospects of the
12        company.

13        I agree, however, with Mr. Gorman’s and Ms. Reno’s decision to essentially discard the
14        results of their Constant Growth DCF analysis that apply sustainable growth rates. I note
15        that their sustainable growth rate calculations rely on Value Line’s projected ROE data for
16        the proxy group companies. Those projected ROEs are substantially higher than the results


     38   Direct Testimony of James M. Coyne, at 26-27.

                                     Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                     On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                               Docket No. 44280
                                                 Page 24 of 52

 1        of the DCF model using sustainable growth rates presented by any of the Intervenor
 2        Witnesses, and demonstrate the fact that investors are expecting to earn higher returns on
 3        equity from the proxy group companies than what is shown by the DCF model using
 4        “sustainable growth rates” calculated from the same source, Value Line. This is an inherent
 5        inconsistency in their sustainable growth approach to the DCF.

 6        MR. ELLIS EXPRESSES CONCERNS THAT ANALYSTS’ PROJECTED EPS
 7        GROWTH RATES ARE “OPTIMISTIC,”39 WHILE MR. GORMAN CLAIMS
 8        THAT LONG-TERM GDP GROWTH SERVES AS A CAP ON LONG-TERM EPS
 9        GROWTH RATES AND SUGGESTS THAT SHORT-TERM EPS GROWTH
10        RATES ARE TOO HIGH.40 DO YOU SHARE THOSE CONCERNS ABOUT
11        ANALYSTS’ PROJECTED EPS GROWTH RATES?

12   A.   No, this is a familiar refrain, but unsubstantiated for utilities. The 2003 Global Analysts
13        Research Settlement (the “Global Settlement”) served to significantly reduce the bias
14        referred to by Mr. Ellis. In fact, the Global Settlement required financial institutions to
15        insulate investment banking business from the analysis business, prohibited analysts from
16        participating in “road shows,” and required the settling financial institutions to fund
17        independent third-party research.

18        A 2010 article in Financial Analysts Journal found that analyst forecast bias declined
19        significantly or disappeared entirely after the Global Settlement:
20                Introduced in 2002, the Global Settlement and related regulations had an
21                even bigger impact than Reg FD on analyst behavior. After the Global
22                Settlement, the mean forecast bias declined significantly, whereas the
23                median forecast bias essentially disappeared. Although disentangling the
24                impact of the Global Settlement from that or related rules and regulations
25                aimed at mitigating analysts’ conflicts of interest is impossible, forecast bias
26                clearly declined around the time the Global Settlement was announced.

 1                   These results suggest that the recent efforts of regulators have helped
 2                   neutralize analysts’ conflicts of interest.41

 3           In addition, analysts covering the common stock of the proxy companies certify that their

 5           compensation. Thus, it is unclear why investors would assume that the proxy companies

                                             Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                             On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                       Docket No. 44280

 1            PLEASE DESCRIBE THE ISSUE WITH THE APPLICATION OF SUSTAINABLE
 2            GROWTH OR RETENTION GROWTH IN THE CONSTANT GROWTH DCF
 3            MODEL.

 4   A.       The sustainable growth rate or retention growth rate calculation assumes that future
 5            earnings will increase as the retention ratio increases. However, this relationship may not
 6            hold for a given company based on management decisions associated with the dividend
 7            payout rate. This conclusion is supported by two articles published in the Financial
 8            Analysts Journal that discussed the theory that high dividend payouts (i.e., low retention
 9            ratios) are associated with low future earnings growth.44 Each of those articles cited to a
10            2003 study by Arnott and Asness45 that found, over the course of 130 years of data, future
11            earnings growth is associated with high, rather than low payout ratios.46 Specifically,
12            Arnott and Asness concluded:

13                     Unlike optimistic new-paradigm advocates, we found that low payout ratios
14                     (high retention rates) historically precede low earnings growth. This
15                     relationship is statistically strong and robust. We found that the empirical
16                     facts conform to a world in which managers possess private information
17                     that causes them to pay out a large share of earnings when they are
18                     optimistic that dividend cuts will not be necessary and to pay out a small
19                     share when they are pessimistic, perhaps so that they can be confident of
20                     maintaining the dividend payouts. Alternatively, the facts also fit a world in
21                     which low payout ratios lead to, or come with, inefficient empire building
22                     and the funding of less than-ideal projects and investments, leading to poor
23                     subsequent growth, whereas high payout ratios lead to more carefully
24                     chosen projects. The empire-building story also fits the initial
25                     macroeconomic evidence quite well. At this point, these explanations are
26                     conjectures; more work on discriminating among competing stories is
27                     appropriate.47


     44       Ping Zhou, William Ruland, Dividend Payout and Future Earnings Growth, Financial Analysts Journal,
     Vol. 62, No. 3, 2006. See also Owain ap Gwilym, James Seaton, Karina Suddason, Stephen Thomas, International
     Evidence on the Payout Ratio, Earnings, Dividends and Returns, Financial Analysts Journal, Vol. 62, No. 1, 2006.
     45       Robert Arnott, Clifford Asness, Surprise: Higher Dividends = Higher Earnings Growth, Financial Analysts
     Journal, Vol. 59, No. 1, January/February 2003.
     46       Since the payout ratio is the inverse of the retention ratio, the authors found that future earnings growth is
     negatively related to the retention ratio.
     47       Robert Arnott, Clifford Asness, Surprise: Higher Dividends = Higher Earnings Growth, Financial Analysts
     Journal, Vol. 59, No. 1, January/February 2003.

                                            Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                            On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                      Docket No. 44280
                                                        Page 28 of 52

 1            Given that all three studies found that there is a negative relationship between earnings
 2            growth and retention ratios, the application of retention growth rates in the DCF model is
 3            not appropriate.

 4                                               2.      Multi-Stage DCF Approach

 5            MR. GORMAN AND MR. ELLIS HAVE ALSO PRESENTED THE RESULTS OF
 6            A MULTI-STAGE DCF MODEL.48 DO YOU AGREE THAT THE RESULTS AND
 7            WEIGHT PLACED ON THOSE ANALYSES ARE REASONABLE?

 8   A.       No, I do not. Mr. Gorman presents the results of a Multi-Stage DCF analysis but elects not
 9            to rely on those results in setting his range or recommendation for Georgia Power,
10            presumably because he views the results as being too low.49 Mr. Ellis also performs a
11            Multi-Stage DCF model, but unlike Mr. Gorman, he relies on those results for his ROE
12            recommendation, even though his Multi-Stage DCF result of 5.97 percent is more than 450
13            basis points below Georgia Power’s current authorized ROE and significantly below any
14            ROE authorized for any electric utility in at least the last 40 years.

15            Mr. Ellis’s use of an inflation rate as a proxy for earnings growth has no reasonable basis
16            of support, as he has provided no evidence that investors would accept growth in perpetuity
17            that tracks inflation. There are many investment alternatives available that offer more
18            attractive growth and return potential. As noted earlier, utility bond yields are currently
19            offering returns comparable to or higher than Mr. Ellis’s DCF-based cost of equity
20            recommendation. No investor would commit capital to an equity investment if they can
21            receive an equal or higher return from a safer debt investment that has a senior claim on
22            cash flow.

 1                                               B.      CAPM ANALYSIS

 2           BEFORE RESPONDING TO THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES’ CRITICISMS OF
 3           YOUR CAPM ANALYSIS, ARE THERE AREAS OF AGREEMENT WITH
 4           RESPECT TO YOUR RESPECTIVE CAPM ANALYSES?

 5   A.      Yes, there are. First, Mr. Gorman, Ms. LaConte, Ms. Reno, and Mr. Ellis, and I agree that
 6           the 30-year Treasury bond yield is appropriate to use as the risk-free rate.50 Second,
 7           although certain of the Intervenor Witnesses disagree with the use of projected interest
 8           rates, Mr. Gorman includes a projection of the 30-year Treasury in his analysis, suggesting
 9           he believes that the current rate underestimates the expected risk-free rate.51 Third, Mr.
10           Gorman, Ms. Reno, and Ms. LaConte each rely on Blume-adjusted Beta coefficients
                                         52
11           reported by Value Line.

12           ARE CAPM RESULTS DEVELOPED BY CERTAIN INTERVENOR WITNESSES
13           REASONABLE?

14   A.      No, they are not. In particular, Mr. Ellis’ CAPM result (5.41 percent) is well below the
15           lowest authorized ROE for any electric utility in the last 40 years.53 Estimates as low as
16           5.41 percent are nearly 100 basis points below the reported cost of debt for utilities in
                              54
17           October 2022.         Debt investors require a return commensurate with the risk that the
18           company will fail to meet that obligation. However, equity investors bear the residual risk
19           associated with ownership, and have a claim on cash flows only after debt holders are paid.
20           As such, equity holders bear greater risk and therefore require a higher return. The fact that

 1        Mr. Ellis’ CAPM analysis produces a result that is well below debt returns demonstrates

 3                                                 1.         Risk-free rate

 4        MR. ELLIS QUESTIONS THE ACCURACY OF INTEREST RATE FORECASTS
 5        AND OBJECTS TO YOUR USE OF A PROJECTED 30-YEAR TREASURY BOND
 6        YIELD AS THE RISK-FREE RATE.55 HOW DO YOU RESPOND?

 7   A.   As explained earlier in this rebuttal testimony and in my direct testimony, I believe the use
 8        of projected 30-year Treasury bond yields as the risk-free rate in the CAPM analysis is
 9        appropriate because interest rates are expected to continue increasing as the Federal
10        Reserve unwinds its balance sheet and tightens monetary policy. Notably, both the current
11        and projected risk-free rates presented in my direct testimony (2.72 percent and 3.40
12        percent, respectively) are well below the 30-day average 30-year Treasury yield of 3.92
13        percent as of October 31, 2022. In addition, I do not share Mr. Ellis’ concerns with the
14        accuracy of projected bond yields. This is because the cost of equity depends on what
15        investors expect, not what actually happens.56 Nonetheless Mr. Ellis has not presented any
16        evidence that current interest rates are any more accurate at predicting future interest rates
17        in the long-term.

18        Further, even though I do not agree with the exclusive reliance on the current average
19        Treasury bond yield as the risk-free rate, I note using current bond yields in the CAPM
20        model produces results (shown in Exhibit JMC-5.2) well above the DCF model results and
21        much higher than the CAPM results put forth by Mr. Gorman, Ms. Reno, Ms. LaConte,
22        and Mr. Ellis. My conclusion is that it is reasonable and appropriate to use the projected
23        30-year Treasury bond yield as the risk-free rate.

 1        WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE TO MR. GORMAN’S CRITICISM THAT YOUR
 2        PROJECTED LONG-TERM RISK-FREE RATE IS “UNREASONABLE”?57

 3   A.   Mr. Gorman’s criticism is puzzling. His CAPM estimate of 9.88 percent relied upon to
 4        develop his recommended ROE range uses a higher projected risk-free rate of 3.60 percent
 5        from Blue Chip published on August 1, 2022 than my 3.40 percent projected risk-free rate
 6        that he criticizes. If his position is that his higher projected long-term risk-free rate of 3.60
 7        percent is more current than the risk-free rates I used in my direct testimony in the current
 8        market environment, I agree. Further, projected risk-free rates align with Georgia Power’s
 9        2023 test year and Alternative Rate Plan that would be effective through 2025, contrary to
10        Mr. Gorman’s position.

11                                                          2.      Beta

12        HOW DO THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES ESTIMATE BETA?

13   A.   As discussed above, Ms. LaConte and Ms. Reno each rely on current Value Line Beta
14        coefficients.58 Mr. Gorman considered current Value Line Beta coefficients before
15        ultimately relying on a historical quarterly average of Value Line Beta coefficients between
16        Q3 2014 and Q2 2022.59 Mr. Ellis relies on Zacks and Yahoo! Finance estimates of Beta
17        that are calculated based on monthly returns, rather than the weekly convention used by
18        Value Line and that I use with Bloomberg.

19        MR. GORMAN OBSERVES THAT CURRENT BETA COEFFICIENTS FROM
20        VALUE LINE ARE HIGHER THAN THE HISTORICAL AVERAGE FOR THE
21        ELECTRIC UTILITY INDUSTRY.60 DO YOU VIEW THIS AS A REASON TO
22        ADJUST THE CURRENT BETA COEFFICIENTS?


     57   Direct Testimony of Michael P. Gorman, at 73.

                                      On behalf of Georgia Power Company

 1   A.   No, I do not. Beta is the measure of relative risk in the CAPM analysis. The utility industry
 2        has typically had lower than average Beta coefficients because utilities generally tend to
 3        be less volatile than the broad market. However, as discussed in my direct testimony,61 that
 4        was not the case during the market dislocation that occurred in response to the COVID-19
 5        pandemic. Five-year Beta coefficients from both Value Line and Bloomberg increased
 6        substantially in February and March 2020 to levels not seen since the financial crisis of
 7        2008/2009 and have remained at those elevated levels ever since.62 In my view, there is no
 8        reason to use the longer-term average Beta coefficients, as suggested by Mr. Gorman. I
 9        have used Value Line and Bloomberg Beta coefficients calculated using five years of
10        weekly return data against a broad market index (either the S&P 500 or the NYSE
11        Composite). This five-year period pre-dates the COVID-19 period by 2.5 years, which
12        suggests that the proxy group Beta coefficients are being affected by factors other than the
13        pandemic.

14        As discussed in my direct testimony, electric utilities have not served as a safe haven for
15        investors during the recent economic downturn.63 According to Mr. Gorman’s
16        Exhibit__(MPG-20) pages 2-5, Beta coefficients for the proxy group and the electric and
17        natural gas utilities have stabilized at elevated levels since Q3 2020, contradicting Mr.
18        Gorman’s position that utilities’ higher Beta coefficients are the result of just two
19        anomalous months in March and April 2020.64 In my opinion, this shift may also be
20        attributable to the market’s recognition of the complex challenges facing the industry in
21        response to climate change, transitioning to a lower carbon generation mix, grid
22        modernization, and shifting consumer preferences.

23        Further, I do not agree with Mr. Ellis’ concerns with Value Line’s methodology for
24        calculating Beta coefficients, including the time period over which Betas are calculated,
25        the market index, and the Blume adjustment. I note that Beta is a measure of relative risk

 5        WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE TO MR. ELLIS’ USE OF MONTHLY BETAS
 6        REPORTED BY YAHOO! FINANCE AND ZACKS?

 7   A.   By their nature, Beta coefficients calculated using monthly returns do not capture intra-
 8        month return volatility and could lead one to conclude utility stock returns are more stable
 9        than they actually are. Consider, for example, stock prices for two utilities – Utility A and
10        Utility B – over a 60-trading day period (roughly three calendar months), illustrated in
11        Figure 12 below. Both utilities start trading at a price of $100 per share. Both utilities report
12        a stock price return of 11.69 percent at the end of three months. At the end of every month
13        (the 20th trading day), both stocks have the same price. However, Utility B’s stock price is
14        clearly more volatile (and therefore riskier) than Utility A’s.

15               Figure 12: Illustrative Example of Weekly vs. Monthly Stock Volatility


16

17        If the Beta coefficient for Utility B was calculated using monthly returns, it would not
18        capture intra-month stock price volatility and Utility B’s Beta coefficient would be the


                                    Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                    On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 34 of 52

 1           same as Utility A’s. On the other hand, if the Beta coefficients were calculated using
 2           weekly returns (every five trading days), Utility B’s Beta coefficient would be higher than
 3           Utility A’s, all else equal, better reflecting its higher volatility. In other words, Mr. Ellis
 4           suggests that the Commission should ignore evidence of increasing volatility and risk for
 5           utility companies, and the proxy group specifically.

 6                                                  3.      Market Risk Premium

 7           THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES CHALLENGE THE FORWARD-LOOKING
 8           MARKET RISK PREMIUM YOU USED IN YOUR CAPM ANALYSIS.65 CAN
 9           YOU PLEASE RESPOND TO THEIR CONCERNS?

10   A.      The use of a forward-looking or projected market risk premium (“MRP”) is appropriate
11           because the historical average market risk premium does not reflect the inverse relationship
12           between interest rates and the equity risk premium. The Ibbotson data used to calculate the
13           historical MRP of 7.46 percent indicates that the long-term average return on large
14           company stocks from 1926-2021 was 12.33 percent, while the average income-only return
                                                                                            66
15           on government bonds was 4.87 percent over the same period.                          It is therefore not
16           reasonable to use the historical MRP when the current average yield on the 30-year
17           Treasury bond is lower than the bond yield used to calculate the historical MRP. With
18           interest rates at these levels, the forward-looking MRP should be higher than 7.46 percent.

19           Second, the method I have used to calculate the forward-looking MRP is similar to the
20           methodology used by FERC in Opinion No. 531-B.67 Specifically, the forward-looking
21           MRP in my CAPM analysis is derived by calculating the expected total return for the
22           companies in the S&P 500 Index less the projected risk-free rate. Ms. LaConte adjusts my
23           forward-looking MRP to exclude companies with negative growth rates and growth rates
24           above 20 percent. However, when investors purchase the S&P 500 Index or a mutual fund

 7        market risk premium would be appropriate only if the expected risk-free rate was similar

 1   A.      Mr. Gorman expresses several concerns with my Risk Premium analysis, including: (1) he
 2           disputes the inverse relationship between interest rates and risk premia; (2) he claims that,
 3           while academic studies have shown that in the past there was such an inverse relationship,
 4           the relationship has changed over time, particularly since interest rate volatility is not as
 5           extreme as it was in the 1980s; and (3) he contends that I have ignored investment risk
 6           differentials in my regression analyses, and that my adjustment to the equity risk premium
                                                                                73
 7           is based exclusively on changes in nominal interest rates.

 8           In response to Mr. Gorman’s first concern, there is a large body of research in addition to
 9           my own statistical analyses that supports the inverse relationship between interest rates and
10           equity risk premia, including the March 1998 article published by Dr. S. Keith Berry, which
11           came to similar conclusions regarding the inverse relationship between interest rates and
12           the risk premia.74 Several other studies were published after those that Mr. Gorman cites
13           as evidence that this inverse relationship is a relic of the 1980s. As summarized in Dr.
14           Roger Morin’s New Regulatory Finance, two of these studies were published in 2005,
15           demonstrating that the inverse relationship between interest rates and the equity risk
16           premium are contemporary concepts in finance:
17                    Published studies by Brigham, Shome, and Vinson (1985), Harris (1986),
18                    Harris and Marston (1992, 1993), Carleton, Chambers, and Lakonishok
19                    (1983), Morin (2005), and McShane (2005), and others demonstrate that,
20                    beginning in 1980, risk premiums varied inversely with the level of interest
21                    rates—rising when rates fell and declining when interest rates rose. The
22                    reason for this relationship is that when interest rates rise, bondholders
23                    suffer a capital loss. This is referred to as interest rate risk…. Conversely in
24                    low interest rate environments, when bondholders’ interest rate fears
25                    subside and shareholders’ fears of loss of earning power dominate, the risk
26                    differential will widen and hence the risk premium will increase.75


     73       Direct Testimony of Michael P. Gorman, at 74-76.
     74       See e.g., S. Keith Berry, Interest Rate Risk and Utility Risk Premia during 1982-93, Managerial and
     Decision Economics, Vol. 19, No. 2 (March, 1998), in which the author used a methodology similar to the
     regression approach described below, including using allowed ROEs as the relevant data source, and came to similar
     conclusions regarding the inverse relationship between risk premia and interest rates.
     75       Morin, Roger A., New Regulatory Finance, Public Utilities Reports, Inc. (2006), at 128.

                                          Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                          On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                    Docket No. 44280

 1        Furthermore, my Risk Premium analysis has an R2 of approximately 0.84, which indicates
 2        that there is a high degree of correlation between the observed equity risk premium and
 3        changes in interest rates.

 4        With regard to Mr. Gorman’s statement that interest rate volatility was more extreme in
 5        the 1980s than it is today, I conducted an analysis that compares the volatility in 30-year
 6        Treasury bond yields in each year during the 1980s to the volatility from 2019 to present.
 7        As shown in Figure 16, the relative standard deviation of Treasury bond yields is
 8        substantially higher in 2022 than it was during any year in the 1980s, indicating that interest
 9        rate volatility is higher in recent years than it was in the 1980s, and has been higher since
10        2019 than all but one year during the 1980s (i.e., 1982).

11        DOES MR. GORMAN’S RISK PREMIUM ANALYSIS SUPPORT THE INVERSE

                                    On behalf of Georgia Power Company

 1           Figure 17: Mr. Gorman’s Treasury Bond Yield and Indicated Risk Premium77


 2

 3        MS. LACONTE ADJUSTS YOUR RISK PREMIUM ANALYSIS TO COMBINE
 4        THE LONG-TERM AVERAGE EQUITY RISK PREMIUM OF 6.06 PERCENT
 5        WITH YOUR PROJECTED 30-YEAR TREASURY BOND YIELD. IS HER
 6        ADJUSTMENT APPROPRIATE?

 7   A.   No, it is not. The risk premium model is designed to adjust the risk premium for any given
 8        level of bond yield, based on the historical relationship between the two variables over the
 9        last 30 years. Adding the long-term average to a current or projected bond yield does not
10        appropriately reflect the statistical relationship between the equity risk premium and 30-
11        year Treasury bond yield. As noted earlier in my discussion of the market risk premium in
12        the CAPM, the equity risk premium and bond yields are inversely related. The only time
13        the historical average equity risk premium is appropriate is when the current or projected
14        bond yield is approximately equal to the historical average bond yield. Ms. LaConte’s
15        adjustment to my Risk Premium analysis is inappropriate and should be rejected.

 1                                    D.       EXPECTED EARNINGS ANALYSIS

 2           BEFORE RESPONDING TO THE CRITICISMS OF YOUR EXPECTED
 3           EARNINGS ANALYSIS, DO ANY OF THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES
 4           PERFORM A SIMILAR ANALYSIS?

 5   A.      Yes, Ms. Reno’s Comparable Earnings Model (“CEM”) reviews historical and forecasted
 6           earned returns on book value and market-to-book ratios for the same proxy group of
 7           companies that I use. Her 2025-2027 proxy group projected CEM results of 10.50 percent
 8           to 10.86 percent are reasonably consistent with my Expected Earnings results presented in
 9           Exhibit JMC-7 that range from 11.03 (mean) to 11.08 percent (median). However, Ms.
10           Reno does not give these results any weight in developing her 9.47 percent
11           recommendation, which forces her overall ROE recommendation downward.

12           PLEASE SUMMARIZE THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES’ POSITIONS WITH
13           THE USE OF AN EXPECTED EARNINGS ANALYSIS TO ESTIMATE THE
14           COST OF EQUITY FOR GEORGIA POWER IN THIS PROCEEDING.78

15   A.      Mr. Gorman, Mr. Ellis, and Ms. LaConte disagree with the Expected Earnings analysis
16           because they believe it does not measure the market cost of equity capital. In addition, Mr.
17           Gorman contends that “the earned return on book equity is simply not an accurate or
18           legitimate basis upon which to determine a fair and reasonable return on equity for both
19           investors and customers.”79 Mr. Ellis further asserts that it has been rejected by the FERC
20           and therefore is not appropriate.

21           WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE TO THESE CONCERNS RELATED TO THE
22           EXPECTED EARNINGS APPROACH?

23   A.      The Expected Earnings approach provides an expected return for like-risk companies,
24           which is a core strength of the model and consistent with the basic tenets of Hope, which


     78       Direct Testimony of Michael P. Gorman, at 77-78; Direct Testimony of Billie S. LaConte, at 11-12; and
     Direct Testimony of Mark E. Ellis, at 3, 29-32.
     79       Direct Testimony of Michael P. Gorman, at 78.

                                           Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                           On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                     Docket No. 44280
                                                       Page 44 of 52

 1   requires that “the return to the equity owner should be commensurate with returns on
 2   investments in other enterprises having corresponding risks.” In the context of considering
 3   regulated utility investment opportunities, an investor would naturally consider both
 4   current market valuations in deciding between companies of like risk and the value of the
 5   expected return on book value.

 6   The Hope and Bluefield standards establish that a utility should be granted the opportunity
 7   to earn a return that is commensurate with the return on other investments of similar risk.
 8   However meritorious the theoretical criticism of the Expected Earnings methodology in
 9   the context of comparing non-utility investments, the Intervenor Witnesses fail to
10   acknowledge the regulated utility’s authorized book return does, in fact, materially affect
11   the earnings and earnings growth of a regulated utility. The Expected Earnings approach
12   provides valuable insight into the opportunity cost of investing in Georgia Power’s electric
13   utility operations precisely because of the regulated context of electric utility investment
14   opportunities.

15   If investors decide to devote capital to the Company (which would offer a return of only
16   5.54 percent on book value if Mr. Ellis’ recommendation was adopted), they forgo the
17   opportunity for that same capital to earn a potentially greater return on book value through
18   investment in the proxy companies. As a result, the Expected Earnings approach is
19   informative because it provides a measure of the return on book value that is available to
20   investors through other investments with comparable risk to Georgia Power.

21   As Dr. Morin explains, the Expected Earnings approach (also referred to as the Comparable
22   Earnings approach below and by Ms. Reno) is highly meaningful in a regulatory setting
23   similar to the one being used to set the cost of equity for Georgia Power:

24          The Comparable Earnings approach is far more meaningful in the
25          regulatory arena than in the sphere of competitive firms. Unlike industrial
26          companies the earnings requirement of utilities is determined by applying a
27          percentage rate of return to the book value of a utility’s investment, and not
28          on the market value of that investment. Therefore, it stands to reason that a
29          different percentage rate of return than the market cost of capital be applied
30          when the investment base is stated in book value terms rather than market

                              Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                              On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                        Docket No. 44280
                                          Page 45 of 52

 1                   value terms. In a competitive market, investment decisions are taken on the
 2                   basis of market prices, market values, and market cost of capital. If
 3                   regulation’s role was to duplicate the competitive result perfectly, then
 4                   the market cost of capital would be applied to the current market value
 5                   of rate base assets employed by utilities to provide service. But because
 6                   the investment base for ratemaking purposes is expressed in book value
 7                   terms, a rate of return on book value, as is the case with Comparable
 8                   Earnings, is highly meaningful.80

 9           Further, in developing his sustainable growth rates for the DCF model, Mr. Gorman
10           assumes the reasonableness of the projected returns on book equity from Value Line for
11           the proxy group companies, which are the same returns that he dismisses as unreliable in
12           the Expected Earnings analysis.

13           Lastly, although the FERC has not included the Expected Earnings analysis in its most
14           recent orders setting its ROE methodology (i.e., Opinion Nos. 569-A and 569-B) for
15           electric transmission companies, FERC has left the door open for presentation of an
16           Expected Earnings analysis on a case-by-case basis.81 In my view, the Expected Earnings
17           analysis provides a more stable picture of the returns that investors are expecting for
18           companies in the Electric Utility sector based on Value Line data. This stability is due to
19           Value Line’s analysis and projections which change when updated, in contrast to the
20           CAPM and DCF results which shift with more volatile market data. Moreover, as explained
21           in this section, the use of accounting returns is appropriate because the authorized ROE is
22           being applied to an accounting rate base in order to determine the net income a company
23           is authorized to recover in rates. For all of these reasons, I continue to support the use of
24           an Expected Earnings analysis as one model to estimate the cost of equity for Georgia
25           Power in this proceeding.

 1        be consistent with the normalized industry ratemaking capital structure” and support the
 2        Company’s current bond rating, or even improve it.87

 3        MR. GORMAN’S RECOMMENDATION TO REDUCE GEORGIA POWER’S
 4        RATEMAKING CAPITAL STRUCTURE IN 2023 AND 2024 IS BASED, IN PART,
 5        ON A REVIEW OF NATIONAL AVERAGE AUTHORIZED CAPITAL
 6        STRUCTURE FOR ELECTRIC AND NATURAL GAS UTILITIES.88 PLEASE
 7        COMMENT ON THAT COMPARISON.

 8   A.   Mr. Gorman’s analysis is not an apples-to-apples comparison to Georgia Power’s proposed
 9        capital structure. First, his analysis includes authorized equity ratios for all rate case types
10        reported by Regulatory Research Associates, including electric T&D rate cases and limited
11        issue rate rider rate cases that are not base rate cases.89 Electric T&D utilities have a
12        different risk profile than vertically integrated electric utilities. As Figure 18 shows, the
13        average annual authorized equity ratios for vertically integrated electric utilities are higher
14        than Mr. Gorman’s annual averages that include electric T&D utilities.

 1        authorized equity ratio between 2010 and 2022. Particularly important is the increase since

 4                     VI.     BUSINESS RISK AND FLOTATION COSTS

 5        DO YOU AGREE WITH MR. GORMAN’S CONTENTION THAT CREDIT
 6        RATINGS TAKE INTO ACCOUNT ALL BUSINESS AND FINANCIAL RISKS
 7        THAT ARE RELEVANT TO INVESTORS?93

 8   A.   No. Credit ratings, while important, are not the only consideration in assessing business or
 9        financial risk, and the risks for equity investors are not the same as the risks for
10        bondholders. Credit ratings are assessments of the likelihood a company could default on
11        its debt, whereas, the purpose of setting an ROE is to determine the riskiness and cost of
12        the Company’s equity. Equity investors are more concerned with earnings and rate base
13        growth, regulatory support for recovery of prudently incurred costs, the strength of the
14        local economy and housing markets, capital spending, changes in interest rates, changes in
15        long-term weather patterns, and more recently exposure and opportunities related to
16        decarbonization of the industry. Bondholders are primarily concerned with stability and
17        predictability of cash flows and timeliness of cost recovery as those affect a utility’s ability
18        to meet its fixed obligations.

19        S&P states that it puts a negative ratings modifier on Georgia Power and ranks the
20        Company at the lower end of S&P’s range for the excellent business risk category based
21        upon Georgia Power’s exposure to energy transition risk and the risks associated with the
22        completion of Vogtle.94 Further, while I have considered Georgia Power’s relative
23        regulatory risk, it is important to recognize that I did not make an adjustment to my ROE
24        recommendation for business risk. Instead, I relied on the mean results of the four financial
25        models I used to estimate the cost of equity for Georgia Power.


     93   Direct Testimony of Michael P. Gorman, at 79-80.
     94   S&P Global Ratings, Georgia Power Company, November 18, 2021 at 6.

                                    Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                    On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 50 of 52

 1        SEVERAL INTERVENOR WITNESSES ARGUE THAT THE COMPANY’S
 2        ALTERNATIVE RATE PLAN AND RATE STRUCTURES MITIGATE GEORGIA
 3        POWER’S RISK.95 WHAT IS YOUR RESPONSE?

 4   A.   It is important to remember that the cost of equity is a comparative analysis. As such, the
 5        relevant point of comparison is the Company’s risk relative to its peers. As explained in
 6        my direct testimony and shown in Exhibit JMC-9, a substantial majority of the proxy group
 7        companies employ a variety of rate structures and mechanisms to mitigate regulatory lag.
 8        As demonstrated in my direct testimony, the regulatory structures available to Georgia
 9        Power are no different from the perspective of the investment community than those in
10        place at the proxy companies.96 As a result, Georgia Power is no less risky than the proxy
11        companies on account of its regulatory mechanisms. Therefore, contrary to these
12        Intervenor Witnesses, my testimony and recommendation accounts for Georgia Power’s
13        regulatory mechanisms.

14        THE INTERVENOR WITNESSES DISAGREE WITH THE INCLUSION OF
15        FLOTATION COSTS. WHY IS IT APPROPRIATE AND NECESSARY TO
16        CONSIDER FLOTATION COSTS IN THE AUTHORIZED ROE?

17   A.   Flotation costs are the direct out-of-pocket costs associated with the sale of new issues of
18        common stock. In the same way that costs associated with debt issuances are recovered
19        through the cost of debt, equity issuance costs should also be recovered. Flotation costs are
20        not expenses, so they do not appear on the income statement. Instead, they are reflected on
21        the balance sheet under “Paid in Capital.” Further, flotation costs are accumulated over
22        time and remain on the balance sheet even if no new equity issuances are planned. For
23        these reasons, I continue to include an adjustment for flotation costs in my ROE
24        recommendation.


     95   See, e.g., Billie S. LaConte, at 15-17; Direct Testimony of Maureen L. Reno, AT 24-25.
     96   Direct Testimony of James M. Coyne, at 42-43.

                                      Rebuttal Testimony of James M. Coyne
                                      On behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                Docket No. 44280
                                                  Page 51 of 52

 1                                      VII.     CONCLUSIONS

 2        WHAT ARE YOUR CONCLUSIONS REGARDING A FAIR ROE FOR GEORGIA
 3        POWER?

 4   A.   The only reliable method for determining the cost of capital is through the application of
 5        rigorous analysis using financial models and market data from reliable sources.
 6        Additionally, the Commission’s cost of capital determination should consider the general
 7        economic and capital market environment and the influence capital market conditions exert
 8        over the results of the ROE estimation models. Interest rates on government and utility
 9        bonds have increased substantially in 2022 and projections suggest a continued upward
10        pressure in the coming years. This increase in the capital costs, and other risk factors
11        indicate that the uncertainty and volatility in financial markets have caused equity investors
12        to require a higher rate of return, including for electric utilities.

13        My recommended ROE of 11.0 percent and capital structure with a common equity ratio
14        of 56.0 percent is fair and reasonable for Georgia Power. This capital structure is consistent
15        with the Company’s actual equity ratio, and combined with the authorized ROE will
16        support continued financial strength and access to debt and equity capital to meet the
17        Company’s operating requirements.

18        DOES THIS CONCLUDE YOUR REBUTTAL TESTIMONY?

19   A.   Yes, it does.

11        (on September 28, 2018) had already placed Negative outlooks on their Georgia Power

14        indicate that the financial strength of the Company had already been weakening well before

16        decision at the end of 2019, downgrades at S&P and Fitch might occur.

10   Q.   YOU ALSO CHALLENGE MR. O’DONNELL’S APPROACH OF FOCUSING ON
11        THE FINANCIAL SIDE OF CREDIT RATINGS WITHOUT CONSIDERING THE
12        QUALITATIVE FACTORS INVOLVED IN THE RATINGS PROCESS. PLEASE
13        EXPLAIN.

14   A.   While credit rating agencies certainly give great weight to financial metrics when
15        formulating their credit ratings, Mr. O’Donnell has not given appropriate consideration to
16        qualitative rating factors, most especially “Regulation.” Based on my leadership role at a
17        major rating agency, I know that the qualitative assessment is given virtually equal weight
18        as the quantitative factors in coming to a rating determination. As I discussed in my direct
19        testimony, “Regulation” is the most important qualitative factor. Mr. O’Donnell supports
20        Mr. Gorman’s proposed significant downward movement in Georgia Power’s financial
21        measures, while both of them ignore the qualitative impacts. This is a mistake, and, in my
22        view, has resulted in incomplete analyses by those two witnesses. If adopted by this
23        Commission, Mr. Gorman’s recommendations would represent a clear break from the
24        Commission’s past constructive decision-making. The likely result would be negative
25        actions by the rating agencies as they recognize both the less supportive financial measures
26        as well as qualitative judgments pointing toward a potential reordering of the state’s
27        regulatory philosophy.

                                     On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                               Docket No. 44280

 1   Q.   WOULD SUCH A MAJOR RESETTING OF THE GEORGIA REGULATORY
 2        CLIMATE HAVE A SIGNIFICANT EFFECT ON GEORGIA POWER’S DAY-TO-
 3        DAY OPERATIONS?

 4   A.   Yes, and the key reason is “Access” to the capital markets. During the past 15 years, we
 5        have seen even “A”-rated utilities encounter issues regarding access to capital markets during
 6        times of stress such as the 2008-2009 recession and the economic concerns that
 7        accompanied the early onset of the COVID contagion. At cross-examination of my direct
 8        testimony, I shared the story of Entergy New Orleans (“ENO”) pre-and-post Hurricane
 9        Katrina. Prior to Katrina, ENO was a financially improving regulated utility that had
10        solidified its Moody’s credit rating at the Baa3 investment-grade level with expected near-
11        term positive movement. Katrina hit New Orleans during the last week of August 2005.
12        Within three weeks, ENO’s rating had fallen to Ca, far below investment-grade level. With
13        its access to the capital markets completely shut off, parent Entergy Corp. moved ENO into
14        bankruptcy, which then opened the way for the parent to begin funding ENO’s long
15        struggle back toward financial stability. It is important to note that all of my talk about the
16        precarious nature of BBB- ratings can be seen within ENO’s saga – that utility has had its ups and
17        downs since Katrina, but as of now, 17 years later, ENO sits below investment grade in the Ba /
18        BB category at both Moody’s and S&P.

19        With access in mind, I attempted to follow Mr. O’Donnell’s complex financial analysis
20        (pages 25-27) about the cost of a downgrade compared to the potential cost to customers
21        resulting from this rate case. I found it interesting that, if I understand his methodology
22        correctly, Mr. O’Donnell is calculating Georgia Power’s potential movement down
23        through the BBB category, with each step equal in financial pain. Thus, he sets the cost of
24        a downgrade from Baa1 to Baa2 as 14 basis points of interest, resulting in additional
25        financing cost for the Company of $96 million. He then compares this to his hypothetical
26        calculated increased customer rate cost of $1.26 billion. He at no time states that a Baa1
27        utility (which would be within my recommended range) would be a much more attractive
28        investment target for the financial community than would be the declining number of
29        utilities holding Baa2 ratings. I have always believed that the best way to test a hypothesis that

               ______________________________________________________________________________

 2   further, and even still further (eventually to absurd levels), which I will proceed to do.

 3   I noted earlier the precarious situation a regulated utility would find itself in with a rating
 4   of Baa3 – the lowest investment-grade level and thus the bane of investors who fear that
 5   one notch down puts the rating “off a cliff.” Taking Mr. O’Donnell’s assumptions,
 6   financing at the anxiety-producing Baa3 level would cost another 14 basis points, or $96
 7   million more, providing this comparison: additional financing costs of $192 million
 8   compared to consumer costs of $1.26 billion. Now, let’s take this to the absurd level – Georgia
 9   Power and its regulators, with the encouragement of some intervenors, decide to allow the

19   O’Donnell’s one-notch downgrade analysis by 100% and still show a preference for the increased

                                Rebuttal Testimony of Steven M. Fetter
                                 On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280

 1           interest costs at close to $400 million for the three-notch downgrade versus consumer rate costs
 2           of $1.26 billion, all the while basically eliminating most of the outside financing access then
 3           available to the Company. At cross-examination on his testimony, Mr. O’Donnell even suggested
 4           that if the Company’s ratings were to fall too low to access traditional financing, there would
 5           always be the option to run its multi-billion-dollar business using private placement financing,
 6           usually reserved to fund construction of individual plants or projects.2 Mr. O’Donnell’s suggestion
 7           stretches access to capital markets far beyond logical reasoning and does not equate to the required
 8           level of access on reasonable terms.

 9           The bottom line is that credit rating analysis is not solely a numbers game. Rating agencies
10           weigh quantitative and qualitative factors on a virtually equal basis, so only looking at
11           quantitative factors will not render an appropriate strategy, nor will only focusing on
12           qualitative factors, most especially regulation, provide a valid plan. The two sides must be
13           weighed equally, which I have done in every one of the cases in which I have testified,
14           using the same fairness parameters whether I was testifying for a regulated utility, a public
15           utility commission, or a consumer advocate.

24           and setting authorized ROE at 10.5% with a 56% equity layer, the regulators in 2019 had
25           adopted Mr. Gorman’s recommendation of authorized ROE of 9.25% with equity at 51%?”
26           Purely on a financial analytical basis, I expect the Company would have probably been just

 1        slightly “north” of the investment-grade / non-investment grade dividing line at BBB- /
