 1   Q.   ARE YOU PROVIDING ANY EXHIBITS TO YOUR TESTIMONY?

 2   A.   No.

 3   Q.   PLEASE SUMMARIZE YOUR TESTIMONY.

 4   A.   As part of our longstanding commitment to meet our customers’ energy needs, Georgia
 5        Power offers our customers an extensive portfolio of more than 40 individual electric
 6        service base rate options from which to choose, with multiple options available for
 7        customers of differing sizes and classes. These rate offerings, including the changes
 8        proposed by Georgia Power in this rate case, will enable the Company to continue
 9        providing customers with clean, safe, and reliable electricity at prices that are below the
10        national average. While a more thorough discussion of the issues we address on rebuttal
11        appears in the following sections of our testimony, here we provide a general summary of
12        our testimony with respect to a number of key issues.

13        Residential Rates

14        Notably, and for the avoidance of doubt, the Company is not proposing to discontinue the
15        Residential Service (“R”) tariff; rather, the Company proposes to make the rate unavailable
16        to new residential premises that establish service after December 31, 2022. Georgia Power
17        also opposes the structural changes to the R rate proposed by PIA Staff, as these changes
18        would fundamentally alter the rate for the more than 1.8 million customers currently taking
19        service under the rate without due consideration of this impact.

20        The Company’s proposal to close the R rate for new residential premises effective January
21        1, 2023, should be approved. This proposal is a logical step in the transition to more modern
22        rate structures, such as Time of Use Residential Demand (“TOU-RD”), with price signals
23        that more accurately reflect the cost of electric service for customers. To be clear, if the R
24        rate is closed to new premises, customers will still be able to choose from among any of
25        the Company’s residential rate options for which they qualify, in addition to TOU-RD as
26        the default residential rate. Contrary to the assertions of PIA Staff and certain intervenors,
27        the TOU-RD rate is designed to be revenue neutral and not cost customers more than the

                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 2 of 23

 1   R rate. Because the TOU-RD rate provides better price signals, customers are able to make
 2   more informed decisions about their energy use and can save on their electric bills by
 3   shifting usage or staggering the use of key appliances.

 4   Commercial and Industrial Rates

 5   The Company’s commercial and industrial rates continue to be appropriately structured,
 6   and the Company does not agree with the proposed changes to these rates. PIA Staff’s
 7   recommendation that customers on marginal rates should be assessed the fully embedded
 8   cost of the Environmental Compliance Cost Recovery (“ECCR”) tariff is fundamentally
 9   inconsistent with how marginal rates like Real-Time Pricing (“RTP”) are designed to
10   operate and should be denied. Fully embedded costs are recovered from the Customer
11   Baseline Load (“CBL”) portion of the marginally priced rates, while marginal costs are
12   recovered from the marginal portion of the rates. To include fully embedded costs such as
13   ECCR in the marginal portion of the rates like RTP would undermine the rate structure and
14   significantly decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future customers. Altogether,
15   PIA Staff’s recommendation to include ECCR in marginal RTP prices, as well as their
16   recommendation to recover ECCR costs on a per kWh basis rather than as a percent of base
17   revenue, results in an overall increase to the RTP tariffs of $150 million. The Commission
18   should reject both recommendations. The Commission should also reject Department of
19   Defense Witness Blank’s recommendation to modify the Company’s criteria for the
20   reduction of CBLs for customers who install behind the meter generation because customer
21   generation does not permanently remove the customer’s load and does not justify the
22   reduction of a customer’s CBL.

23   Community Solar

24   The Commission should approve the Company’s requested increase to the Community
25   Solar block prices as agreed to by PIA Staff, since the purpose of this program is to support
26   the development of renewable energy but does not guarantee bill savings. In addition, the
27   Georgia Coalition of Local Government’s (“Coalition”) proposed price reduction is
28   premised on a misunderstanding of how the program operates and should be rejected.

                          Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                              On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                         Docket No. 44280
                                           Page 3 of 23

 1        Charge It Electric Vehicle Tariff

 2        The Commission should also approve the Charge It Electric Vehicle (“CIEV”) tariff, as
 3        agreed to by PIA Staff, as an appropriately structured and time limited offering to
 4        encourage the growth of electric vehicles (“EVs”) in Georgia.

 5        Cost-of-Service Study

 6        The Company’s cost-of-service study (“COSS”) continues to reasonably allocate the
 7        Company’s revenue requirement across retail rates based on principles of cost causation.
 8        Georgia Power’s continued use of a 12 coincident peak (“12-CP”) methodology to allocate
 9        production costs is reasonable and appropriately recognizes the Company’s need to plan
10        and operate its system in every month of the year. Staff and Intervenor proposals to replace
11        the 12-CP method with an energy-based allocation method or summer-focused allocation
12        method are neither compelling nor justified.

13        Parity Adjustments

14        Finally, the Company is supportive of general movement towards parity but not the rate
15        specific adjustments proposed by PIA Staff or Georgia Association of Manufacturers
16        (“GAM”) Witness Pollock. Instead, the Company supports parity adjustments rooted in the
17        Company’s COSS that are made at the rate group rather than individual rate level.

 4                                      II. RESIDENTIAL RATES

 5   Residential Service “R” Tariff

 6   Q.     PLEASE EXPLAIN THE COMPANY’S PROPOSED CHANGES TO THE R RATE.

 7   A.     As Mr. Legg states in his Direct Testimony, the Company proposes to update the R tariff
 8          to make the rate unavailable to newly constructed residential premises that establish service
 9          on Georgia Power’s electric system after December 31, 2022. This update will encourage
10          residential customers to adopt more modern rate structures with price signals that more
11          accurately and appropriately reflect the cost of electric service. However, the R tariff will
12          remain available to residential premises for which electric service is established prior to
13          January 1, 2023.

14          As part of its commitment to customer satisfaction, Georgia Power has actively promoted
15          the variety of residential rate options it offers to help customers select the rate that best fits
16          their needs. As a result, customer adoption of residential rate options such as the TOU-RD
17          (also known as “Smart Usage”), TOU-PEV, Pay by Day (“PBD”), and FlatBill tariffs has
18          increased significantly. Indeed, the number of residential customers who have chosen to
19          participate in a time-of-use rate has increased by more than 250%. Many customers have
20          demonstrated a preference for these rate plans, which are designed to provide customers
21          with options that best meet their needs. By closing the R rate to new premises, the Company
22          will continue to gradually transition new residential accounts onto the other rates within
23          the Domestic Group that better align with the cost to serve customers.


                                  Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                      On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                 Docket No. 44280
                                                   Page 5 of 23

 1   Q.   IS PIA STAFF PROPOSING ANY MODIFICATIONS TO THE R RATE?

 2   A.   Yes. PIA Staff proposes to eliminate the declining block rates during non-summer months
 3        and move to a flat rate for all kilowatt hour (“kWh”) usage.

 4   Q.   DO YOU AGREE WITH PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATION TO REPLACE
 5        THE DECLINING WINTER BLOCK RATE STRUCTURE WITH A FLAT
 6        ENERGY RATE?

 7   A.   No. Notably, the Commission has declined to adopt this recommendation in each of the
 8        last two rate cases and should continue to do so in this case. Although the price signal the
 9        R rate sends is not as strong as more recent tariff designs, the Company originally designed
10        the R rate to reflect the seasonality of costs to serve residential customers. The inclining
11        price blocks in June through September appropriately reflect the fact that the Company’s
12        costs are higher in the summer months. Importantly, the inclining price structure provides
13        incentives for energy efficiency and conservation. The declining block structure in the
14        winter months recognizes that off-peak costs are generally lower and, therefore, provides
15        price signals to reflect this reality. The declining block structure provides an economical
16        option for customers choosing to use electric heating. The prices are set to more than cover
17        the Company’s marginal costs, and resulting revenues place downward pressure on rates
18        for all customers.

19   Q.   ARE THERE OTHER CONCERNS WITH PIA STAFF’S PROPOSAL TO
20        SIGNIFICANTLY MODIFY THE R RATE?

21   A.   Yes. PIA Staff is proposing significant modifications to the R rate without providing any
22        analysis or discussion of the impacts these changes would have on the large number of
23        customers who are currently on that rate. Currently, more than 1.8 million customers take
24        service on the R rate, and it is not clear that PIA Staff has considered how their proposed
25        modifications will impact these customers or what steps should be taken to educate
26        customers prior to the implementation of such changes. PIA Staff is proposing to
27        fundamentally alter the rate through which Georgia Power’s largest number of customers


                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 6 of 23

 1        are being served, without due consideration of the impact to those customers. Further, the
 2        R rate design is antiquated and sends poor price signals to customers about the cost of the
 3        electricity they consume, as compared to the Company’s newer rate designs. Thus, it makes
 4        no sense to fundamentally alter the longstanding R tariff while customers are transitioning
 5        to more modern rates that more appropriately reflect the cost of electric service for
 6        customers.

 7   Q.   IS THE COMPANY PROPOSING TO ELIMINATE THE R RATE?

 8   A.   No. The Company is not proposing to eliminate the R rate. Under the Company’s proposal,
 9        customers currently on the R rate will be able to remain on the rate. Customers currently
10        on the R rate who subsequently move to another existing premises will also be able to
11        remain on the rate. The Company is only proposing to close the R rate for new residential
12        premises that initially establish service with Georgia Power after December 31, 2022. This
13        incremental change in the availability of the R rate is a measured and logical step in Georgia
14        Power’s gradual movement towards more modern rate designs that send price signals that
15        more appropriately reflect the cost of electric service for customers.

16   Q.   ASIDE FROM THE R RATE, WHAT OTHER RATE OPTIONS ARE AVAILABLE
17        FOR CUSTOMERS AT A NEW PREMISES?

18   A.   If the Company’s proposal is adopted, customers at new residential premises can choose
19        from the Company’s other residential rate options, which include TOU-RD, TOU-PEV, or
20        PrePay. Additionally, once those premises have twelve months of service history,
21        customers can also select the Company’s FlatBill or Pay by Day rates.

22   Q.   DOES THE COMPANY HAVE ANY FEEDBACK FROM CUSTOMERS
23        REGARDING THEIR EXPERIENCE ON TOU-RD?

24   A.   Yes. Customer surveys have shown that, as compared to customers on the R rate, TOU-
25        RD customers are more satisfied with their rate and feel more knowledgeable regarding the
26        behaviors that can help them capitalize on their rate plan and reduce their energy bills.


                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 7 of 23

 1        These same surveys showed that TOU-RD customers had an overall higher satisfaction
 2        score for Georgia Power as compared to R rate customers.

 3   Q.   IS THE TOU-RD RATE INHERENTLY MORE COSTLY FOR CUSTOMERS
 4        THAN THE R RATE, AS CLAIMED BY PIA STAFF AND INTERVENORS?

 5   A.   No. The TOU-RD rate is not designed to cost customers more than the R rate; rather, the
 6        TOU-RD rate was designed to be revenue neutral for the average customer – meaning that
 7        the average customer would pay the same annual bill amount on the TOU-RD rate as they
 8        would on the R rate. In fact, because TOU-RD provides better price signals to customers,
 9        they can make more informed decisions about their energy use and save on their electric
10        bills by shifting usage or staggering the use of key appliances. As a result, customers
11        receive more efficient economic signals regarding their usage and energy choices.

12   Q.   IS THE BILL COMPARISON NOTED IN PIA STAFF’S TESTIMONY AN
13        APPROPRIATE REPRESENTATION OF THE PRICE IMPACTS OF THE TOU-
14        RD RATE AS COMPARED TO THE R RATE?

15   A.   No. As new premises are added to the Georgia Power system, it can take anywhere from
16        one to two years for the premises’ monthly usage to normalize. This ramp-up period could
17        be due to numerous factors, including the timing of home construction, installation of
18        appliances, or occupancy. Because the bill comparisons that Staff highlight in their
19        testimony represent premises that are new to our system, the average monthly usage for
20        those premises is far below the level to which the usage will ultimately mature (see Figure
21        1). Therefore, the bill comparisons used by Staff are heavily skewed by extremely low
22        usage and therefore fail to tell a complete story.


                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 8 of 23

 1                                                  Figure 1


 2

 3        As Figure 1 above demonstrates, a customer’s average monthly usage will increase
 4        continually for the first 24 months of existence on Georgia Power’s system. At least one
 5        intervening witness commented during their direct testimony that the TOU-RD rate is an
 6        effective rate for customers that demonstrate a “good load factor.” By allowing the new
 7        premises to mature towards a normal usage level, customers at these new premises will
 8        greatly improve their load factors and have a better bill experience when comparing TOU-
 9        RD to the R rate.

10   Q.   AS COMPARED TO THE R RATE, WHY ARE THREE-PART RATES – LIKE
11        TOU-RD – MORE COST-REFLECTIVE?

12   A.   Costs to serve our customers are divided into three components: (1) customer-related costs;
13        (2) demand-related costs; and (3) energy-related costs. To ensure our rates most accurately
14        reflect the cost-of-service, all three cost components should be accounted for within the
15        design of a rate. As such, three-part rates, which include customer, demand, and energy
16        related components, are the most cost-reflective rates because they appropriately account
17        for each of the three cost categories. Conversely, when a rate, such as the R rate, lacks a


                              Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                  On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 9 of 23

 1        demand component, the demand-related costs of electric service must be recovered through
 2        the rate’s energy component, which does not appropriately match the collection of revenues
 3        with how the Company incurs costs. The result is a less efficient price signal that results in
 4        less efficient use of the electric system by customers and higher prices overall.

 5   Q.   WHY IS THE TOU-RD RATE A BETTER DEFAULT RATE THAN TOU -
 6        RESIDENTIAL ENERGY ONLY (“TOU-REO”) AS PROPOSED BY GSEIA, SEIA,
 7        AND VOTE SOLAR?

 8   A.   As described above, TOU-RD is a modern, three-part rate that sends price signals that more
 9        accurately and appropriately reflect the cost of electric service. In addition, the TOU-RD
10        rate is more stable than the TOU-REO rate from a monthly billing standpoint, meaning that
11        it has a smaller margin between minimum and maximum bills during an annual period.
12        Figure 2 below provides an example of the bill volatility between the R, TOU-REO and
13        TOU-RD rates for the average residential customer and shows that TOU-RD is much less
14        volatile than either the TOU-REO or the R Rate when comparing the difference between
15        minimum and maximum bills over a given year.

16                                               Figure 2


17
18


                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 10 of 23

 1   Q.   WHY IS IT APPROPRIATE TO INCREASE THE BASIC SERVICE CHARGE AS
 2        PART OF THE COMPANY’S REQUEST?

 3   A.   The increased basic service charge better aligns with the Company’s costs to serve
 4        customers. As stated by Mr. Legg during the Company’s direct hearings, Georgia Power
 5        has proposed to increase all traditional base rates on an equal percentage basis, including
 6        rate elements. This includes the residential basic service charge, which recovers the
 7        customer-related costs the Company incurs to serve a customer, regardless of the amount
 8        of energy that customer may use. As proposed, the monthly increase to the basic service
 9        charge would be approximately $2.27 for a residential customer.

10   Q.   DOES THE COMPANY AGREE WITH STAFF’S PROPOSAL TO INCREASE
11        THE INCOME QUALIFIED SENIOR CITIZEN DISCOUNT BY THE AMOUNT
12        OF ANY INCREASE TO THE BASIC SERVICE CHARGE?

13   A.   Yes.

14                                III.    COMMERCIAL RATES

15   Q.   DO     YOU    AGREE       WITH      PIA     STAFF’S       RECOMMENDATION           THAT
16        CUSTOMERS ON MARGINAL RATES SHOULD BE ASSESSED THE FULL
17        EMBEDDED COST OF THE ENVIRONMENTAL COMPLIANCE COST
18        RECOVERY (“ECCR”) TARIFF?

19   A.   No. Staff’s recommendation is fundamentally inconsistent with how marginal rates like
20        RTP are designed to operate. For RTP as designed, the Company’s fully embedded costs
21        are recovered from the CBL portion of the rate, while marginal costs are recovered from
22        the marginal portion of the rate. The ECCR costs include coal combustion residual asset
23        retirement obligations (“CCR ARO”) that are fully embedded costs incurred by the
24        Company independent of a customer’s kWh usage. As such, they are appropriately
25        recovered through the CBL portion of marginally priced rates. To include fully embedded
26        costs such as ECCR in the marginal portion of the RTP rate would not only undermine the


                              Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                  On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 11 of 23

 1        rate structure, but also prevent these rates from actually functioning as marginal rates. The
 2        hourly prices would no longer reflect marginal costs or real-time prices.

 3   Q.   WHAT WOULD HAPPEN IF ECCR COSTS WERE ALLOCATED TO THE
 4        MARGINAL PORTION OF RTP AND OTHER INCREMENTAL RATES?

 5   A.   If ECCR costs, or other fully embedded costs, were allocated to the marginal portion of
 6        RTP rates, RTP costs would increase by approximately $150 million overall. A cost shift
 7        of this magnitude would significantly decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future
 8        customers. The RTP rate is a critical rate for large, economic development customers that
 9        are considering locating, relocating, or expanding operations in Georgia. PIA Staff’s
10        proposal undermines the principles of marginal rate design by dramatically altering the
11        allocation of ECCR costs within the RTP rate and would have a significant negative impact
12        on the Company’s ability to help attract economic development opportunities to the state.

13   Q.   DOES     PIA     STAFF’S      PROPOSED          50%      MOVEMENT         OF     ECCR     IN
14        INCREMENTAL            RATES        MITIGATE          THE       IMPACT      OF    STAFF’S
15        RECOMMENDATION?

16   A.   No. As described above, even an incremental move of 50% would have a dramatic and
17        negative impact on RTP customers by fundamentally changing the rate and undermining
18        the core principles of a marginal rate structure.

19   Q.   DOES GEORGIA POWER AGREE WITH DEPARTMENT OF DEFENSE
20        WITNESS        BLANK’S       RECOMMENDATION                  TO       MODIFY     THE    CBL
21        REDUCTION CRITERIA?

22   A.   No. CBLs may be reduced when load originally represented in the CBL has been
23        permanently removed. Mr. Blank proposes to reduce CBLs when customers install
24        generation that runs “at least 6,000 hours” (68%) of the year, even though no load has been
25        removed. For almost one third of the year, the customer’s usage on the system would be
26        the same as it was before the installation of the generation. Generation does not


                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 12 of 23

 1        permanently remove load, and therefore does not justify the reduction of the customer’s
 2        CBL.

 3        Second, Mr. Blank criticizes the Company’s requirement that a CBL reduction assessment
 4        based on historical load is not permitted unless the customer stops taking service under the
 5        RTP tariff for two years. The Company supports the requirement that a customer remain
 6        off the RTP rate for two years before returning or requesting a CBL reduction, because
 7        short term changes to a customer’s usage should not result in the permanent conversion of
 8        embedded load into marginal load on the RTP rate. The RTP tariffs include provisions to
 9        revise the CBL as the result of actions like permanent equipment removal and energy
10        efficiency improvements, but the tariffs specifically prohibit CBL revisions due to
11        temporary factors such as economic downturns. Requiring customers who have not
12        removed load to stay off RTP for 24 months in order to set a new CBL preserves the
13        integrity of the RTP program and protects other customers from potential cost shifts that
14        might result from the premature revision of the CBL.

15   Q.   DOES       THE       COMPANY           AGREE          WITH           WITNESS    BLANK’S
16        RECOMMENDATION TO ALLOW RTP CUSTOMERS TO ALSO TAKE
17        SERVICE ON THE BACKUP (“BU”) TARIFF?

18   A.   No. The purpose of the BU tariff is to enable customers who are not currently receiving
19        hourly marginal pricing to avoid yearly demand ratcheting, if their generation operates as
20        expected. Customers may receive this benefit or they may benefit from marginal pricing,
21        but not both.

22   Q.   DOES THIS MEAN RTP CUSTOMERS ARE NOT ABLE TO BENEFIT FROM
23        INSTALLING GENERATION?

24   A.   No. RTP customers that install and use generation receive the benefit of the Company’s
25        marginal price for that hour. This is true when the customer avoids paying the marginal
26        price while operating above their CBL level, or when the customer receives a credit at the
27        marginal price when operating below their CBL level.


                              Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                  On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 13 of 23

 1   Q.    DOES     THE     COMPANY          SUPPORT         MODIFYING           THE   TERMS     AND
 2         CONDITIONS OF THE FIXED PRICE ALTERNATIVE (“FPA”) RATE AND
 3         PRICE       PROTECTION         PRODUCT          (“PPP”)     OFFERING        TO   REMOVE
 4         COMPANY DISCRETION REGARDING WHEN TO OFFER THESE RATES?

 5   A.    No. The Company considers multiple factors when deciding whether to offer the FPA rate
 6         and PPP offerings to customers. These factors include, but are not limited to, uncertainty
 7         around environmental regulation, fuel markets, and other potential factors that could
 8         impact the pricing of these offerings. Additionally, it is important for the Company to
 9         maintain flexibility to act quickly and update PPP offerings to best reflect market
10         conditions, especially during times of fuel market volatility as have been experienced for
11         the last year. As a result, removing the Company’s discretion regarding when to offer these
12         products adds unnecessary risk that could be borne by other customers, since delays in
13         acting during volatile markets could result in the development of product offerings that do
14         not properly reflect pricing impacts.

15   Q.    DOES GEORGIA POWER SUPPORT MARTA’S REQUEST TO NOT APPLY
16         ANY INCREASES TO THE ELECTRIC TRANSPORTATION (“ET”) RATE?

17   A.    No. Georgia Power does not support MARTA’s request because it would result in a
18         revenue deficiency being spread to other customers. Georgia Power is sympathetic to
19         MARTA’s ridership challenges during the pandemic; however, these challenges are not
20         unique to MARTA and many other businesses have dealt with similar challenges.

21        IV. COMMUNITY SOLAR AND CHARGE IT ELECTRIC VEHICLE TARIFFS

22   Community Solar

23   Q.    WHAT IS THE PURPOSE OF THE COMMUNITY SOLAR TARIFF?

24   A.    The Community Solar tariff is a program that offers qualifying customers the opportunity
25         to support renewable energy and the development of solar power in Georgia by subscribing
26         to a portion of the output from local solar facilities. Every month, participating customers

                                Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                    On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                               Docket No. 44280
                                                 Page 14 of 23

 1        pay a fee for a subscription block of the energy produced by Community Solar facilities,
 2        and in return receive energy credits on their monthly bill that are based on the production
 3        from those facilities. Additionally, the equivalent renewable energy credits (“RECs”) are
 4        retired on the customer’s behalf. As noted in this Commission’s 2017 Order approving the
 5        program, the Community Solar tariff is not intended to create bill savings for participating
 6        customers, but rather, is targeted toward customers who are interested in supporting solar
 7        but either do not want or are unable to install solar panels at their premises. The Company
 8        currently operates three Community Solar facilities located near Athens, Augusta, and
 9        Savannah.

10   Q.   ARE GUARANTEED BILL SAVINGS PART OF THIS PROGRAM?

11   A.   No. The Company does not guarantee or advertise the Community Solar program as a bill
12        savings opportunity. In some months throughout the year, the credit that participating
13        customers receive on their bill based on the solar facility’s production may exceed the
14        charge to participate, resulting in bill savings. However, as noted above, the purpose of the
15        program is to support renewable energy and the development of solar power in Georgia
16        and not to achieve bill savings.

17   Q.   WHAT DID THE COMMISSION APPROVE IN THE 2022 INTEGRATED
18        RESOURCE PLAN REGARDING THE COMMUNITY SOLAR TARIFF?

19   A.   The Commission approved updates to the Community Solar program to make it more
20        accessible to customers. More specifically, the Commission approved certain changes as
21        proposed by the Company, including the Income Qualified Community Solar Pilot. The
22        Commission did not approve the Company’s requested pricing change “to increase the
23        residential block charge to $27.99 and to set the commercial option at $29.99/month,”
24        electing instead to reserve the pricing decision for consideration in this rate case.


                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 15 of 23

 1   Q.   WHAT ARE THE PROPOSED CHANGES TO THE COMMUNITY SOLAR
 2        TARIFF THAT ARE TO BE ADDRESSED IN THIS CASE?

 3   A.   The Company proposes to update the pricing for the existing residential Community Solar
 4        Charge from $24.99 to $27.99 per one kilowatt block in order to ensure that participants
 5        are not subsidized by non-participants. In addition, following the Commission’s approval
 6        to expand the opportunity to participate in Community Solar to customers on the General
 7        Service (“GS”) tariff, the Company has proposed that the pricing for GS Community Solar
 8        participants be set at $29.99 per one kilowatt block.

 9   Q.   WHY SHOULD THE COMMISSION REJECT PIA STAFF’S AND THE
10        COALITION’S RECOMMENDATIONS TO DECREASE THE PER KILOWATT
11        (“KW”) BLOCK PRICE FOR A COMMUNITY SOLAR SUBSCRIPTION?

12   A.   The Community Solar subscription price is designed to ensure that participating customers
13        do not shift costs to other customers when kilowatt hours reduce the monthly bills of
14        participating customers. As such, the Company has requested an increase in its subscription
15        rates for residential and commercial customers participating in Community Solar. Based
16        on the approved structure of the Community Solar program, a reduction in the price per
17        kW block of energy would leave costs unrecovered and, therefore, shift cost recovery to
18        non-participants.

19        In addition, the Coalition bases their reduction on the misguided premise that Community
20        Solar participants pay for rate riders like NCCR, ECCR, and fuel more than once.
21        Customers participating on the Community Solar rate still receive and pay for service on
22        their normal retail electric service rate and are still served from all energy resources
23        generating into or otherwise supplying the Company’s system. When a participating
24        customer pays the subscription rate to participate in the Community Solar program, this is
25        a voluntary premium the customer opts to pay to support the addition of solar resources in
26        Georgia. This subscription affords the participating customer the opportunity to offset their
27        purchase of energy from Georgia Power by the amount of energy produced at the
28        Community Solar facilities. In turn, this energy credit offsets the purchase of delivered

                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 16 of 23

 1         energy applied to the customer’s bill, including with respect to the components for ECCR,
 2         NCCR, and fuel. Therefore, savings on the riders are achieved when the participating
 3         customers’ kWh usage is reduced by the Community Solar production amount.

 4         The Company includes the rate riders as part of the calculation in setting the Community
 5         Solar monthly charge because (i) that is consistent with how the R rate works and (ii) the
 6         Company must ensure that the riders are not under recovered simply because the
 7         participating customer’s bill reflects fewer kWhs as a result of the energy credit applied
 8         under the Community Solar program.

 9         Finally, because the Community Solar participant still takes service on their normal retail
10         rate, participation in the Community Solar tariff does not automatically excuse the
11         customer from paying their share of NCCR, ECCR, and fuel.

12   Q.    WHY IS IT INAPPROPRIATE TO LOCK IN A COMMUNITY SOLAR
13         PARTICIPANT’S           RATE        FOR       THE       DURATION       OF      PROGRAM
14         PARTICIPATION AS PROPOSED BY THE COALITION?

15   A.    The Company designed program pricing to prevent cost shifting to non-participating
16         customers. The Company’s costs and revenues are constantly changing. To hold a
17         customer’s cost of participation static for the duration of their participation would lead to
18         inappropriate cost shifting to other participating and non-participating customers.

19   Charge Electric Vehicle (“CIEV”) Rider

20   Q.    PLEASE DESCRIBE THE CIEV RIDER.

21   A.    Georgia Power is supporting the rapidly expanding EV market with a rider that is tailored
22         to provide affordably priced electric service and to encourage the growth of EVs in our
23         state. The CIEV rider will modify how the Power & Light tariffs price commercial EV
24         charging loads. Specifically, the new CIEV rider will modify the monthly billing demands
25         by using Billing Demand Adjustment Factors as specified in the tariff. CIEV is a temporary
26         rider that will expire either at the end of 48 months or when the rolling 12-month load

                                Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                    On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                               Docket No. 44280
                                                 Page 17 of 23

 1        factor for the account reaches 15% or greater – whichever comes first. Participation is
 2        available to new, separately metered charging loads that are served on Georgia Power’s
 3        system after January 1, 2023.

 4   Q.   WHY IS IT INAPPROPRIATE TO OPEN THE CIEV RIDER TO EXISTING
 5        LOCATIONS AS RECOMMENDED BY SEVERAL INTERVENORS?

 6   A.   First, the CIEV rider was designed to incentivize the installation of new EV charging
 7        stations and opening this rate to existing customers would defeat the purpose of
 8        incentivizing new installations. Second, by limiting this tariff to new, separately metered
 9        premises, the Company designed the CIEV rider to avoid a cost shift among existing
10        customers. If existing customers were permitted to participate on the CIEV rider, the
11        reduced billing demand allocation in each year would necessarily shift demand costs to
12        other customers. The Company does not support this type of cost shift and supports keeping
13        the CIEV rider limited to new, separately metered premises in order to appropriately
14        incentivize new EV charging installations without doing harm to existing customers.

15                       V. INTERCONNECTION APPLICATION FEE

16   Q.   PLEASE        EXPLAIN           WHY       THE        COMPANY         PROPOSED           AN
17        INTERCONNECTION             APPLICATION           FEE     IN    SECTION G       OF    THE
18        COMPANY’S RULES AND REGULATIONS FOR ELECTRIC SERVICE.

19   A.   The Company proposed to introduce a one-time $200 interconnection application fee that
20        will be required for all customers seeking to interconnect a customer generating facility to
21        the Georgia Power system. This fee is designed to recover costs associated with supporting
22        the safe and reliable interconnection of a customer generator to the system and will help
23        ensure that such costs are not borne by all other customers.


                              Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                  On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 18 of 23

 1   Q.   WOULD THE COMPANY CONSIDER MAINTAINING THE EXISTING $5/KW
 2        WITNESS TESTING FEE AS PROPOSED BY GSEIA, SEIA, AND VOTE SOLAR?

 3   A.   No. The current $5/kW witness testing fee was agreed to by settlement in 2017 as part of
 4        the application of the RCB Framework to behind the meter solar. As settled to, this fee was
 5        not implemented to fully recover the Company’s cost to witness test new customer
 6        generators smaller than 250 kW let alone the administrative costs to review, process, and
 7        update the customer’s account for the installation of generation at its premises. In contrast,
 8        the Company’s proposed $200 one-time fee is designed to cover the application,
 9        administrative costs, impact study fees and witness testing fees (where required) for new
10        customer generation. The cost to provide these services does not vary significantly based
11        on the size of the facility and the Company believes a flat, one-time fee is more appropriate
12        than the outdated $5/kW witness testing fee.

13                              VI.     HOURLY INTERVAL DATA

14   Q.   DID THE COMPANY COMPLY WITH THE COMMISSION’S DIRECTION TO
15        EVALUATE         CUSTOMER          ACCESS         TO     HOURLY       INTERVAL        DATA
16        FOLLOWING THE 2019 RATE CASE?

17   A.   Yes. Consistent with the Commission’s direction following the 2019 rate case, Georgia
18        Power worked with Staff to develop options for customer access to usage data and then
19        submitted recommendations to the Commission for approval. In addition, the cost to
20        provide additional options for access to customer usage data was directed to be deferred as
21        a regulatory asset and recovered in a future base rate proceeding, which the Company has
22        requested for recovery in this case.

23   Q.   IS GEORGIA POWER REQUIRED TO PROVIDE HOURLY USAGE DATA TO
24        ALL CUSTOMERS FREE OF CHARGE AS ASSERTED BY THE COALITION?

25   A.   No.

 1   Q.   DO THE VAST MAJORITY OF CUSTOMERS HAVE ACCESS TO THEIR
 2        HOURLY USAGE DATA?

 3   A.   Yes. The Company made hourly usage data available to customers via My Power Usage
 4        (“MPU”) in January 2022. This was the result of the hourly usage project approved by the

 6        customers who opt-out of an AMI meter and certain unmetered accounts. Special Service

14                        VII.     PROPOSED CHANGES TO THE COSS

15   Q.   DO YOU BELIEVE THAT OTHER ALLOCATION METHODOLOGIES IN
16        ADDITION TO THE COMPANY’S FILED 12-CP METHODOLOGY SHOULD BE
17        CONSIDERED BY THIS COMMISSION FOR THE ALLOCATION OF THE
18        CAPACITY COMPONENT OF THE PRODUCTION FUNCTION?

19   A.   No. When preparing for a rate case filing, Georgia Power considers other allocation
20        methodologies in addition to its historical preference of 12-CP. For this rate case, the
21        Company determined that 12-CP remains the most accurate and appropriate methodology.
22        As stated in Mr. Evans’ direct testimony, the 12-CP methodology incorporates the fact that
23        Georgia Power’s system is planned and operated for the purpose of serving load requests
24        every month of the year. Additionally, PIA Staff’s proposed methodologies not only impact
25        the allocation of costs in base rate cases, but also may impact the allocation of fuel costs in
26        the Fuel Cost Recovery docket. The trade-off between the level of Production fixed
27        investment and the variable cost of producing a unit of output is one primarily made

                                 Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                     On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                                Docket No. 44280
                                                  Page 20 of 23

 1        between base capital costs and fuel costs. Altering the allocation of Production capital costs
 2        with such a complex calculation would necessitate a similar alteration to the allocation in
 3        the Fuel Cost Recovery docket. Recognizing the current, approved allocation of fuel costs
 4        in the Fuel Cost Recovery docket, the Company continues to support the 12-CP
 5        methodology as the most appropriate Production allocator. It has been employed by
 6        Georgia Power for allocating production capacity-related costs since 1989, and as such,
 7        has therefore influenced present rates.

 8   Q.   ARE PIA STAFF’S RECOMMENDATIONS CONSISTENT WITH THEIR 2019
 9        RECOMMENDATIONS TO CHANGE HOW THE COMPANY ALLOCATES
10        GENERATION PLANT PRODUCTION COSTS?

11   A.   No. Staff is recommending energy-based allocation methods in this case, which are
12        substantially different from the “up to” 8-CP method proposed by PIA Staff in the last case.
13        If PIA Staff’s recommendation had been adopted in the last case, it would be wholly
14        inappropriate to pivot to the other end of the spectrum so drastically in this case. Adopting
15        PIA Staff’s vacillating recommendations in the 2013 and 2019 cases, and now this current
16        rate case, would have resulted in great swings in parity results from case-to-case. To depart
17        from using 12-CP as an allocator for Production cost, especially without compelling
18        evidence to do so, could cause significant and unexpected disruptions to customer bills.

19   Q.   WOULD THE COMPANY CONSIDER MOVING TO A 4-CP OR SUMMER PEAK
20        PRODUCTION COST ALLOCATION METHOD AS RECOMMENDED BY
21        INTERVENORS?

22   A.   No. The generation planning for Georgia Power’s system is driven and influenced by non-
23        summer periods in addition to summer peaks. Georgia Power utilizes Expected Unserved
24        Energy (“EUE”) in its determination of the optimal reserve margin for both Summer and
25        Winter seasons. A pure summer peak or 4-CP allocator is an oversimplification of the
26        Company’s generation planning process. If one were to use a pure summer peak allocator,
27        this could create unstable, unbalanced, and inequitable cost allocation in which some rate
28        groups receive little if any Production demand cost allocation whatsoever. An example of

                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 21 of 23

 1        this could be if the pure summer peak allocator for that year occurred in daytime hours
 2        such that there was no subsequent cost allocation to outdoor lighting. The ongoing capacity
 3        needs of Georgia Power are heavily driven by winter needs and to not recognize or allocate
 4        costs based on winter loads would, therefore, be inappropriate.

 5   Q.   WHY IS THE 12-CP METHOD THE MOST APPROPRIATE WAY TO
 6        ALLOCATE THE COMPANY’S PRODUCTION COSTS?

 7   A.   A 12-CP demand is defined as the sum of the highest kW load for the Company in each
 8        month of the 12-month period divided by twelve. At the jurisdictional, class, or rate group
 9        level, 12-CP is the respective coincident demands at the time of the monthly system peaks.
10        Using this allocator ensures that the coincident load burden placed on the system by a
11        jurisdiction, class, or rate group is allocated an appropriate share of the costs of the system.

12        The Company’s adoption of seasonal planning further reinforces the appropriateness of the
13        12-CP approach. The Company must plan to have sufficient generation to serve its
14        customers in all twelve months of the year and the 12-CP method appropriately captures
15        the summer and winter peaks targeted through seasonal planning. The summer and winter
16        reliability planning of the Company further underscores that no one month or season is
17        adequate at representing the coincident impact of customers on system capacity. Georgia
18        Power forecasts to remain a summer peaking utility. While the IRP identified winter
19        reliability concerns, the Company’s rate designs remain appropriate to address the
20        forecasted peak loads and higher marginal costs that occur in summer months.

21        In addition, as stated in Mr. Evans’ Direct Testimony, the 12-CP allocation methodology
22        has been used in every COSS study filed by the Company with this Commission since
23        1989. Further, the 12-CP method has been one of FERC’s preferred allocation techniques
24        for determining wholesale jurisdictional obligations, and it has been used by the Company
25        in its filings before FERC. It has proven to be a common and popular allocation technique
26        employed in numerous retail filings before many state commissions throughout the United
27        States. It is a straightforward methodology, has a strong cost causative relationship, and
28        has a history of providing stable and sound results.

                               Rebuttal Testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg
                                   On Behalf of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 22 of 23

 1                                VIII. PARITY ADJUSTMENTS

 2   Q.   DOES THE COMPANY SUPPORT MOVEMENTS TOWARD PARITY AMONG
 3        RATE GROUPS?

 4   A.   Yes. The Company has made movements toward parity in previous rate cases based on
 5        PIA Staff’s recommendation. In fact, since 2010, all of Georgia Power’s rate groups have
 6        moved toward parity or maintained relative parity. While the Company supports movement
 7        towards parity, the Company does not support the parity adjustments outlined in the
 8        testimony of Mr. Pollock on behalf of GAM or the testimony of the Barber, Deitchman,
 9        Watkins panel for PIA Staff. Any adjustments toward parity should be based upon the
10        COSS presented in Exhibit LPE-1 filed by the Company in the rate case filing. The
11        precision of a COSS and the accuracy of the results are greatly impacted by the number of
12        customers and the amount of load in each rate. The smaller the number of customers and
13        the smaller the amount of load in a particular rate, the lesser the potential accuracy of the
14        results associated with that rate. Some rates have very limited participation and may
15        produce volatile rates of return and parity results in cost-of-service studies for different
16        time periods. With this in mind, the Commission should consider the cost-of-service to the
17        eight rate groups instead of by individual rates.

18                                      IX.     CONCLUSION

19   Q.   DOES THIS CONCLUDE YOUR TESTIMONY?

20   A.   Yes.
